TL;DR: Mortgage rates eased today, but borrowing costs are still expensive for many home buyers.

Mortgage rates dropped today, but anything above 7% still puts pressure on home buyers’ budgets.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage dropped to 7.24% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is 14 basis points lower than yesterday and three basis points lower than a week ago. (A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.)

A significant drop is eye-catching, but you should consider mortgage interest rates' overall direction, not just what's going on today. While the economy never sleeps, markets are closed on the weekends. The rates you see Friday are unlikely to change much (if at all) until Monday.

Here are the average APRs we're seeing today, as well as movement over the past month for context:

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

Next week, the Nerds are waiting for the Personal Consumption Expenditures (PCE) price index, due out Wednesday. It's the Federal Reserve's preferred measure of inflation, so investors will be watching closely. The Fed doesn't set mortgage rates, but lenders anticipate its decisions, so mortgage rates often move before the Fed acts. Stubbornly high inflation is a big reason why we’re seeing upward pressure on mortgage rates right now.

If PCE shows inflation comes in hotter than expected, mortgage rates could climb as markets anticipate further Fed rate hikes. (An increase to the federal funds rate is already widely expected at the Fed’s Dec. 8-9 meeting.) A cooler reading could ease some pressure on mortgage rates for now.

I’m not getting my hopes up too high for meaningfully lower inflation, though. Last week's Producer Price Index showed wholesale inflation climbed 6.5% over the past year — its fastest pace in four years. Wholesale inflation measures the price businesses pay for things. When businesses’ costs go up, they could pass those along to consumers in the form of higher prices — keeping the flames of inflation alive.

Whether Wednesday's PCE brings relief or another helping of bad inflation news, we'll know before the turkey hits the table on Thursday.

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.74% or higher.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

🤓 Nerdy Reminder: Rates can change daily, and even hourly. If you’re happy with the deal you have, it’s okay to commit.

» Stay informed: Check out NerdWallet's mortgage news hub for all our latest coverage.

#3052. College Scholarship Breakdown: Types, Sponsors and Eligibility at a Glance

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Scholarships vary in what they pay for and who can apply. Understanding the differences can save you time during your application process.

Scholarships have become a standard part of applying for college. You can learn about scholarships in a variety of ways, but looking through an online database of available scholarships — the most common way — can be overwhelming.

Before you go searching for a scholarship, it's important to understand the types of scholarships available and how to identify the ones that give you the best chance of winning, which can save you time and potentially earn you more funding. Here’s what you need to know about scholarships to decide which ones are best for you.

Scholarships are often funded by three groups: private organizations, institutions like universities, and government agencies. None is better than the other when it comes to awarding funds.

Nonprofit organizations and clubs that are looking for students either majoring in or researching certain fields, or who have experience volunteering in a specific field.

Companies that want to support all students or only fund those majoring in a field that relates to the companies’ industries.

Financial institutions, such as banks or credit unions, that want to support their community or their members.

Individuals or families that are funding private scholarships, usually in memory of a loved one.

Universities and colleges that are offering funds to students who attend their campuses.

Research organizations that want to fund students who will research in their areas of interest and further their causes.

These scholarships generally are provided by local, state or federal government agencies for a variety of reasons. Despite being funded by a government agency, the scholarships do not require you to pay back the money — unlike federal student loans.

Some scholarships pay for anything related to college expenses, including tuition, room and board, books and supplies, and more. Other scholarships limit what you can pay for with the funds, such as only tuition or tuition along with books. It’s important to look at this restriction when applying for scholarships to ensure the funds will go toward what you need to cover.

Merit scholarships require you to show excellence or achievements in your schoolwork, through talents and abilities, and even leadership or service work. Because these types of awards can be highly competitive, merit scholarships might require more than just achievements, such as volunteer or leadership experience. This means merit-based scholarships can overlap with other types of scholarship categories. Here are some examples of merit-based scholarships:

Merit scholarships often require you to have earned a minimum grade point average (GPA). The minimum GPA depends on the scholarship but often ranges from 2.0 to 4.0, with more competitive scholarships usually requiring higher GPAs. Scholarships with lower GPA minimums are more likely to consider additional factors, such as financial need.

Some merit scholarships might require a minimum ACT or SAT score instead of a GPA — or in addition to a minimum GPA.

Merit scholarships might also require applicants to have participated in activities outside of their schoolwork. This is common for scholarships that require membership in a certain group or organization. For example, membership in agricultural clubs, such as 4-H or FFA, and scouting groups, such as the National Eagle Scout Association, appear in scholarship requirements.

Some scholarships emphasize volunteering or community service and only consider applicants who have that experience. Applications vary in how detailed their requirements can be. Some scholarships want only a general description of where and how you’ve volunteered, and others might require experience with a service project that shows more leadership experience or time volunteering with a certain type of organization.

Scholarships that are awarded to students who can’t afford college are called need-based scholarships.

Unmet financial need is the difference between what it will cost you to attend college and what you can afford to pay. Because calculating how much you can afford is a complicated process, many scholarships refer to your FAFSA to document your financial need.

Some scholarship requirements may be as straightforward as demonstrating that you have financial need, but some scholarships might have income limits. However, income restrictions are less common.

Athletic scholarships are for — you guessed it — athletes. They are usually offered by colleges and universities to recruit athletes onto their collegiate teams and can be competitive. Which sports have scholarships available and the scholarship amounts vary by college.

Even though the main criterion for an athletic scholarship is being good at your sport, you’ll likely still need to show that you can keep up with your schoolwork. Most athletic scholarships require students to earn a minimum GPA or test scores to remain eligible while they’re in college.

Some scholarships are designated for students majoring in a general field, such as science or education, but others can be for very specific areas of study. For example, a scholarship for students majoring in a natural resource field could include majors in biology, ecology, wildlife biology, conservation education, forestry, soils and hydrology. However, a stricter scholarship might state that students have to major in more specific topics, such as biochemistry or microbial ecology.

Scholarships are available for active and former members of the military and members of their families. In addition to federal funds available for veterans to attend college, private organizations and some universities have scholarships for veterans to offset the cost of a degree.

Which family members can apply for funds depends on the individual scholarship. For example, some scholarships extend to children or spouses of veterans, but some go as far in a family tree as great-grandchildren.

Veteran- and military-focused scholarships might require that applicants or their family members enlisted in a certain branch, served during a particular time or conflict, or meet a certain rank or status such as receiving disability. Scholarships might also require that the applicant be a wartime veteran or family member of a wartime veteran; not all veterans meet this status.

Scholarships are also awarded to people who identify with a certain group. Common examples of identity-based scholarships include those for people who have disabilities, belong to certain ethnic or religious groups, identify as LGBTQIA+ or are first-generation students. Some identity-based scholarships require applicants to meet several criteria, such as belonging to more than one identity group.

Outside of what would be considered standard DEI groups, there are scholarships for applicants who identify with less common groups, such as veganism, homelessness and model railroad builders.

The best scholarships for you depend on your situation. For example, if you are a skilled athlete with a good academic record, going for an athletic scholarship might give you the most funding. On the other hand, if you’re going to research a topic that isn’t common, looking for scholarships that support your research topic might be your best bet because competition could be lower.

Good tactics to figure out which scholarships are worth your time include:

Think of the skills, talents, experience and identities that are unique to you. For example, being a child of a veteran immediately qualifies you for scholarships with fewer applicants than scholarships open to everyone. Likewise, if you embrace a certain ethnicity, identify with a specific group or have experience with a type of work, you might qualify for scholarships that appreciate and recognize those attributes more than the average scholarship.

Apply if you think you're eligible. Don’t apply to just a few scholarships and hope you get them. Instead, apply for as many scholarships as you can and for which you’re eligible. This increases your chances of receiving funds. Even small scholarships can make a big impact when you add several of them together.

Understand how you can use the money. If you already have a scholarship that covers only books, applying for the same type of scholarship isn’t helpful if you don’t need the extra funds. Instead, look for scholarships that cover the costs you have remaining.

Not sure where to look? Our sister site Scholarships360.org provides students with vetted scholarship opportunities. That would be a good place to start.

#3039. Global Net Lease: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Office exposure is being cut, and noncore sales, not new stock, are funding buybacks that management says keep equity issuance off the table.

By The Motley Fool Team – Nov 21, 2026 at 5:13PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Global Net Lease ›

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 5, 2026, Global Net Lease (GNL -0.17%) raised the lower end of its full-year adjusted funds from operations (AFFO, a real estate cash flow metric) guidance to $0.83 per share. For long-term investors, this focus on disciplined capital allocation helps protect dividend safety and long-term value.

The company is selling office properties to eliminate carrying costs and protect straight-line rent, an accounting method that averages lease payments across contract terms. Shifting capital away from office buildings protects cash flow durability across the broader portfolio.

Low stock prices make issuing new equity unappealing for funding growth. Instead, management sells noncore assets like gas stations and car washes at favorable capitalization rates (cap rates, the annual return expected from an income property) to repurchase undervalued shares.

Rising interest rates create headwinds for real estate debt refinancings. Global Net Lease insulated itself by swapping most borrowing to fixed terms, leaving zero debt maturities until late 2027.

Capital allocation stays disciplined even as borrowing costs remain volatile across commercial real estate markets. The company holds strong liquidity while prioritizing debt reduction and high-return industrial acquisitions.

Contractual rent increases anchor cash flow durability. The strategy centers on reducing office exposure while using property sales to pay down debt.

#3035. Novo Nordisk's Wegovy Pill Is Capturing 80% of New Oral Obesity Prescriptions. Is NVO Stock a Buy?

Novo Nordisk's GLP-1 pill is performing incredibly well, which is a good sign if you are considering buying the stock.

By Reuben Gregg Brewer – Nov 21, 2026 at 5:15PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Novo Nordisk ›

Shares of Novo Nordisk (NVO -2.70%) have fallen 70% from the all-time highs reached after it launched the first GLP-1 weight-loss shot. That has pushed the dividend yield up to 4.5%, well above the pharmaceutical industry average of 1.3%, despite a strong payout ratio of roughly 33%.

Wall Street may not be giving Novo Nordisk enough credit, especially given the strength of its new GLP-1 pill. Here's why more aggressive investors may want to jump on this high-yield opportunity.

There's no way to sugarcoat it: Novo Nordisk flubbed the launch of its Wegovy GLP-1 shot. The product was very well received, but the drug maker couldn't keep up with demand. That allowed generics to enter the market early, creating a major competitive headwind. When Eli Lilly (LLY -0.30%) introduced a more effective GLP-1 shot, Novo Nordisk quickly became the number two player. Investors' early enthusiasm quickly turned into deep pessimism.

That pessimism persists, even after Novo Nordisk's successful launch of a Wegovy pill. Consumers prefer pills over shots, for obvious reasons. That helps explain why Novo Nordisk's pill launch has been even more successful than its GLP-1 shot launch. In fact, in the third quarter, the company estimates that the Wegovy pill captured eight out of 10 new patients starting oral GLP-1 weight-loss treatments.

The company is executing so well that it increased its full-year 2026 sales and earnings guidance when it reported third-quarter results. From this perspective, Wall Street may be overly negative about the business.

But there's another problem that investors are grappling with. Novo Nordisk is moving to a volume-driven business model. That involves lowering prices, which has been a headwind for the top and bottom lines. In fact, while the company updated its guidance, the sales and revenue midpoint is now flat. That's a massive improvement from the previous midpoint of down 3% for both metrics, but it is understandable that investors would remain concerned.

Essentially, Novo Nordisk needs to prove not only that it remains an innovative drugmaker but also that its volume-driven model is working. And given the intensely competitive healthcare sector, it will likely see additional GLP-1 competition at some point. For example, Pfizer (PFE -0.51%) is hard at work on an acquired long-acting GLP-1 drug it purchased after the company dropped an internal GLP-1 candidate. That said, the global obesity market is very large, so there's likely to be plenty of room for more than one player. And there are also opportunities for companies to fill different niches within the still emerging GLP-1 market.

For more aggressive dividend investors, the risk/reward balance is likely tilted toward reward with Novo Nordisk. The value opportunity here isn't just highlighted by the lofty yield. The stock's price-to-sales ratio of 3.3x is well below its five-year average of 8x. Its price-to-earnings ratio of roughly 10x is far lower than the five-year average of 23x. And Novo Nordisk's price-to-book ratio of 5.2x is a fraction of its 15x longer-term average. Notably, all of the ratios are lower than they were before the launch of the Wegovy shot. For reference, Eli Lilly's P/E ratio is nearly 45x.

Given the success Novo Nordisk is seeing with its Wegovy pill and the improved revenue and earnings outlook, it seems like management is executing well as it shifts to a volume-driven model. Add in the lofty yield and attractive valuation, and the story gets even more attractive. If you can handle a little uncertainty, this well-run drug maker is probably worth a very close look right now.

#3033. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Global stock markets remain volatile amid persistent geopolitical tensions and concerns about the sustainability of AI trade. Given this backdrop, investors seeking steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking recommendations from top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick attractive stocks, as these experts assess a company's ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is a dividend king. The company has increased its dividend for 39 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share (annualized dividend of $7.12 per share), CVX offers a yield of 3.14%. Chevron will announce its third-quarter results on October 30.

In an earnings preview note on U.S. oil majors, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Chevron with a price target of $241. The analyst is focused on the company's international growth prospects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa, and the Eastern Mediterranean.

Specifically, Mehta highlighted management's commentary on solid execution in Venezuela, with gross production expected to more than double to nearly 600 kbd (kilo barrels per day) by 2031 via three joint ventures. He also noted a recent oil and gas condensate discovery in offshore Angola and is awaiting commercial updates on the West Qurna 2 and Nasiriyah projects in Iraq.

Furthermore, Mehta expects CVX to report improved productivity and capital efficiency across domestic shale assets. He expects the improvement mainly in the Permian Basin, where Chevron projects advanced chemical treatments to drive about a 10% recovery uplift in new wells and bring down capital spend per barrel by about 25% year-over-year. Overall, Mehta expects Chevron to report Q3 2026 EPS of $4.95, above the consensus of $4.89.

"While unfavorable timing effects and volatile commodity prices may present quarterly earnings headwinds, we highlight healthy U.S. refining margins and strong asset utilization as partial offsets," said Mehta.

Mehta ranks No. 381 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Chevron KPIs on TipRanks.

Kinetik Holdings (KNTK) is a fully integrated, pure-play, midstream company operating in the Delaware Basin. Earlier this month, the company declared a dividend of $0.81 per share, payable on October 30, 2026. With an annualized dividend of $3.24 per share, KNTK offers a yield of 5.82%. Kinetik will announce its Q3 earnings on November 4, with a conference call scheduled the next day.

Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, Goldman Sachs analyst John Mackay reiterated a buy rating on Kinetik stock and slightly raised his price target to $58 from $57. He expects the company to report Q3 2026 EBITDA of $279 million, higher than the Street's expectation of $270 million but lower than the second quarter's EBITDA of $281 million.

The 5-star analyst expects Kinetik's Q3 performance to gain from higher gas volumes after notable curtailments in the second quarter. Mackay also expects the quarter to benefit from higher production and steady commodity margins, even though some lower-margin contracts and weaker Waha spread marketing gains could weigh on results.

Overall, Mackay increased his Q3 2026 estimates to reflect higher volumes, driven by rapid Permian egress pipeline commissioning, stronger throughput at Kings Landing I, and higher M2M commodity prices, offset by increased costs.

"As natural gas takeaway constraints ease across the Permian, investor focus centers on KNTK's YE26 volume trajectory and durability of its multi-year growth," said Mackay.

Mackay ranks No. 569 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.9%. See Kinetik's Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving to Crescent Energy (CRGY), an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian, and Uinta Basins. With a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share (annualized dividend of $0.48 per share), CRGY's dividend yield stands at 3.56%. Earlier this month, Crescent announced an agreement to acquire Eagle Ford assets from Devon Energy (DVN).

Heading into Q3 earnings on Nov. 2, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reiterated a buy rating on Crescent stock and slightly lowered the price target to $17 from $18, citing dilution from new shares.

"CRGY's recent acquisition of DVN's Eagle Ford assets marks a strategic, growth-oriented move, potentially enhancing cash flow performance," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst noted that the acquisition of the Eagle Ford assets at a net purchase price of $3.85 billion will add about 68 MBoe/d of net production and over 600 net Tier 1 locations. Richardson added that a solid track record of synergies from past deals in the Eagle Ford region increases confidence about Crescent achieving efficiencies with this deal.

Additionally, Richardson noted that the financial implications of the acquisition seem impressive, with a projected increase in cash flow per share of about 9% for 2027 to 2029.

The analyst believes that Crescent is not just expanding its business through acquisitions but positioning itself as a more durable company, regardless of market conditions.

Richardson ranks No. 478 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Crescent Energy Statistics on TipRanks.

#3031. Dutch Bros vs. Celsius: Which Stock Is the Better Buy as the Market Keeps Falling?

Both companies are growth stocks, but one stands out more than the other.

By Jack Delaney – Updated Nov 21, 2026 at 5:16PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Dutch Bros ›

Long-term investors view broad market sell-offs as offering discounts on growth stocks. That's because, over the long run, stock prices tend to go up. So even amid some general uncertainty now, there could be good deals. Two such stocks we'll look at today are Dutch Bros (BROS -4.01%) and Celsius (CELH -7.36%).

Dutch Bros operates a rapidly expanding network of drive-thru coffee shops, while Celsius markets functional energy drinks through global retail channels. Both companies are disruptors in the competitive beverage stocks landscape. This comparison looks at their growth trajectories and financial health to help you decide which stock fits your strategy.

Dutch Bros operates a high-growth model focusing on drive-thru shops that serve a variety of hand-crafted beverages. The menu includes coffee, tea, lemonade, and the popular Dutch Bros Rebel energy drink. As of June 2026, the company managed over 1,200 locations in nearly 30 states. Customer concentration is a factor to watch, as the company's brand recognition is somewhat limited.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue reached nearly $2 billion. This represented a revenue growth rate of roughly 30.7% compared to the prior year period. The company reported a net income of approximately $102 million during this same timeframe. This growth is driven by both new shop openings and steady traffic at existing locations.

Celsius creates and markets functional beverages that have gained significant popularity in the U.S. and international markets. Its portfolio includes brands like CELSIUS and Alani Nu, sold through convenience stores, fitness centers, and large retailers. The company is substantially reliant on PepsiCo (PEP +1.11%) as its primary distributor in the U.S. and Canada. This single customer accounted for over 43% of revenue in 2025, and such high customer concentration adds a layer of risk to the business.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue for the company reached roughly $3.2 billion. This reflects significant momentum with revenue growth of approximately 49.1% over the preceding year. During this timeframe, the company generated net income of nearly $230 million.

Dutch Bros faces intense competition from established coffee chains and other quick-service restaurants. Its high geographic concentration in the Western U.S. remains a concern, as most of its shops are located in that region. Rapid expansion also carries risks of identifying suitable real estate and maintaining its unique corporate culture. Regulatory changes regarding labor laws or potential drive-thru bans in specific municipalities could impact its operational model.

Celsius is heavily dependent on its distribution agreement with Pepsi, which provides the backbone of its retail reach. This relationship creates potential conflicts of interest since Pepsi holds a significant ownership stake and board representation. The company also faces fierce competition from global players such as Monster Beverage (MNST -0.57%) and Red Bull. Additionally, integrating recent acquisitions like Alani Nu and Rockstar presents ongoing execution risks for the management team.

Celsius appears more attractively valued than Dutch Bros, as it offers a lower earnings multiple despite maintaining a significantly faster revenue growth rate.

For investors taking the long view, broad market dips offer buying opportunities. After all, Dutch Bros opened at $35.50 on Nov. 16 and closed at $32.78 on Nov. 20. Celsius also saw its stock price dip, opening at $32.47 on Nov. 16 and closing at $28.46 by Nov. 20.

Choosing between the two growth stocks for a portfolio, however, I would rather own Dutch Bros.

One of the key reasons is that, as a growth stock, Celsius appears to be increasingly relying on acquisitions. In the third quarter of 2026, revenue for its CELSIUS brand fell by approximately 6%, and U.S. retail sales fell by 7%. It saw strong growth through its acquired Alani Nu brand, with revenue climbing 24% and retail sales growing 57.9%.

In comparison, in its third quarter, Dutch Bros grew revenue by nearly 30%, same-shop sales increased by 5.4%, and customer traffic grew by 1%. Granted, it's not an apples-to-apples comparison, given the differences in how the businesses operate. Still, it shows that the growth opportunities for Dutch Bros come through its core offerings, as its stores are attracting more customers, selling more, and have future sales growth opportunities through new store openings.

While Celsius is buying growth, Dutch Bros is creating it. That makes Dutch Bros the stock I would want to buy for the long term, using sell-offs to pick up shares at a discount to where they could be trading in the future.

#3025. Kraft Heinz vs. Coca-Cola: Here's the Stock to Buy

Kraft Heinz's net loss and Coca-Cola's net margin tell very different stories, and the valuation gap may surprise you.

By Jack Delaney – Updated Nov 21, 2026 at 5:18PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Kraft Heinz ›

Should everyday investors favor a turnaround value play or a reliable beverage leader? This match-up explores whether Kraft Heinz (KHC +1.89%) or Coca-Cola (KO +0.94%) is the better buy for your portfolio heading into 2027.

Kraft Heinz focuses on household food staples and condiments, while Coca-Cola dominates the global non-alcoholic beverage market. Both companies provide essential products that often resist economic downturns, yet they follow very different growth paths and valuation models today. This comparison breaks down their latest financial results and potential risks to help you make an informed decision.

Kraft Heinz produces and markets a massive global portfolio of branded foods and condiments, ranging from its namesake ketchup to Philadelphia cream cheese. As one of the prominent consumer staples stocks, it sells its products in more than 40 countries through retail and foodservice channels. Its largest customer is Walmart (WMT +1.51%), which accounts for approximately 21% of net sales, and this customer concentration adds a layer of risk to the business.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 26, 2026, revenue reached nearly $24.8 billion, representing a slight decline of approximately 1.5% compared to the prior period. The company reported a net loss of roughly $3.5 billion during this timeframe, and its net margin was approximately -14%. These results reflect a period of adjustment as the company manages its global portfolio and navigates changing retail demand.

Coca-Cola is a global beverage powerhouse that owns or licenses hundreds of brands across categories like sparkling soft drinks, sports drinks, and juices. The company utilizes a vast network of independent bottling partners, including Coca-Cola Europacific Partners (CCEP +0.72%) and Coca-Cola HBC (CCHBF +0.67%), to distribute its products in over 200 countries. It recently secured an exclusive pouring rights agreement with KFC U.S. starting in January 2027.

In the 12 months ended Oct. 2, 2026, revenue reached approximately $51.1 billion, which represents a healthy growth of roughly 7.1% over the prior year. The company delivered net income of $14.6 billion, resulting in a strong net margin of about 28.7%. This profitability highlights the strength of its brands and its ability to maintain pricing power in various global markets.

Kraft Heinz faces intense competition from both major branded rivals, such as PepsiCo (PEP +1.11%) and Nestlé (NSRGF +1.24%), and low-cost private-label products. The company must constantly adapt to rapidly changing consumer preferences and is dependent on key retail customers, who may reduce shelf space or pricing leverage. Additionally, supply chain volatility and potential future impairments of its intangible assets remain significant concerns for the business.

Coca-Cola is exposed to risks from unfavorable global economic and geopolitical conditions that can impact its international operations. The company also faces health-related concerns regarding sweetened beverages, which often lead to increased regulatory scrutiny and shifts in consumer habits. Furthermore, Coca-Cola depends heavily on independent bottling partners and must navigate challenges like water scarcity and maintaining its brand image in a competitive landscape against PepsiCo and Keurig Dr Pepper (KDP +1.25%).

Kraft Heinz currently looks cheaper based on its much lower forward price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio and price-to-sales (P/S) ratio, suggesting it is priced as a value stock compared to Coca-Cola.

Between the two, I favor Coca-Cola more for a simple reason: Kraft Heinz is executing a turnaround, while Coca-Cola is just executing.Looking at its recent performance for the third quarter of 2026, unit case volume grew 5%, organic revenue grew 7%, comparable earnings per share (EPS) grew 11%, and operating income grew 12%. Overall, that shows not only consumers buying more, but also Coca-Cola converting demand for its products into greater profits.

Also promising in those quarterly results was that volume growth wasn't tied to just one geographic area, giving Coca-Cola multiple markets and helping reduce reliance on just one market.

In addition, it's seeing success with its classic offerings, with Coca-Cola volume growing 5% in that quarter. But it's also seeing even larger volume growth with tweaks to its classic formulas, with Coca-Cola Zero Sugar volume growing 16%. To finish out the year, Coca-Cola's management expects 5% organic revenue growth and 11% comparable EPS growth.

While Kraft Heinz needs to show investors it can earn their confidence back, Coca-Cola has already earned the confidence. It just needs to keep executing from here.

#3019. Oklo vs. NuScale Power: Which Nuclear Stock Is a Better Buy?

Two nuclear upstarts with very different paths to market.

By Jack Delaney – Updated Nov 21, 2026 at 5:19PM EST

As data centers hunt for power, Oklo (OKLO -4.75%) and NuScale Power (SMR -11.81%) have emerged as two of the most prominent contenders in the nuclear space.

Both companies aim to build smaller reactors that can sit closer to the point of use. Oklo emphasizes fuel recycling and has unique partnerships, while NuScale focuses on a modular design already certified by federal regulators.

Oklo designs fast-fission power plants intended to serve specialized customers like data centers, industrial factories, and military installations. The company is currently targeting major players like Meta Platforms (META -5.93%), Equinix (EQIX -4.89%), and Diamondback Energy (FANG -0.58%) for long-term power agreements. Because its business model revolves around a few massive potential contracts, customer concentration adds a significant layer of risk to the enterprise.

Oklo does not yet report meaningful revenue, as it has not yet brought its first powerhouses online. As of Sept. 30, it had total cash, cash equivalents, and debt securities of $3.1 billion. For the first nine months of 2026, it recorded a net loss of $123.3 million.

NuScale Power focuses on small modular reactors designed to be safer and easier to deploy than traditional large-scale nuclear plants. The company relies heavily on an exclusive strategic partnership with ENTRA1 for global sales and development. Key collaborations include work with the Tennessee Valley Authority (TVC +0.17%) and other project engagements.

Like Oklo, NuScale does not report meaningful revenue. It ended the third quarter of 2026 with $1.9 billion in cash, cash equivalents, and investments. For the first nine months of 2026, its net loss sits at $135.2 million.

Oklo faces significant risks due to its lack of commercial operating history and the absence of binding power purchase agreements. The company is developing first-of-a-kind technology that requires complex regulatory approval from both the Nuclear Regulatory Commission and the Department of Energy. Furthermore, securing a scalable supply of specialized nuclear fuel remains a major hurdle for its future growth and operational success.

NuScale Power faces substantial risks related to ongoing securities class action litigation involving its disclosures about project prospects and competitive advantages. The business depends almost entirely on its partnership with ENTRA1, and any termination of that agreement could be devastating for its commercialization efforts. NuScale may still need to secure significant future capital to reach its commercial delivery targets.

As neither company generates meaningful revenue nor is profitable, it is difficult to rely solely on traditional metrics to make an investment decision.

I could see the case for adding both Oklo and NuScale to a portfolio. That way, you can still make money from the upside of the nuclear energy market while not being tied to the success of either company. Still, between the two, I would rather own Oklo because of its unique setup. NuScale's success will primarily depend on selling its reactors, while Oklo has multiple revenue stream opportunities it can eventually tap into.

It's a unique business model in that it attempts to develop reactors, sell energy and heat, recycle fuel, and engage in isotope production simultaneously. That does introduce more execution risk, trying to achieve multiple objectives at once, and it also makes it difficult to estimate the future value of what all that would look like when it's up and running. But the main takeaway is that, because of those opportunities, its sales-generating potential could be greater than that of other nuclear players.

As mentioned earlier, Oklo already has some buy-in from big names, such as Meta Platforms. Oklo plans to develop a 1.2 GW power campus in Ohio that will support Meta's data centers in the region, and the agreement allows Meta to prepay for power and offer funding to advance the project.

What Oklo could fully accomplish is still years away from fruition, so this should remain a small, speculative position in a portfolio.

#3017. November 22, 2026: Markets Special Report

LIVE NOVEMBER 22 at 7 PM ET. Watch The Motley Fool team discuss the current market conditions, what investors need to know now, and how we should all be thinking about our investments in this environment.

#3015. Shopify vs. Uber Technologies: Which Technology Stock Is a Better Buy?

Shopify's revenue surge and Uber's free cash flow make for a sharp contrast, but one future opportunity may settle which stock to own.

By Jack Delaney – Updated Nov 21, 2026 at 5:20PM EST

Choosing between Shopify (SHOP -6.25%) and Uber Technologies (UBER -3.25%) involves weighing two different tech stocks. Both companies have become essential to how we shop and move.

Shopify acts as the digital backbone for millions of online merchants, while Uber manages a massive logistics and transportation network. This comparison evaluates their financials and business models to see which is a better pick for 2027 and beyond.

Shopify provides the essential digital tools that allow companies to build their own online storefronts and manage physical retail locations. The company serves millions of merchants in multiple countries, providing an all-in-one ecosystem for everything from inventory management to payment processing. By focusing on giving brands total control over their customer relationships, it has become a central player among tech stocks that support the broader e-commerce market.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue reached nearly $14.2 billion, representing an increase of approximately 32.5% compared to the prior twelve-month period. This robust growth was accompanied by net income of $2.1 billion.

Uber Technologies operates a massive technology platform that connects people to transportation, food delivery, and logistics services. Its network spans over 70 countries, serving a growing number of monthly active consumers who use the app for ridesharing or ordering from local merchants. The company leverages partnerships with major retailers like Best Buy (BBY -1.33%) and Tractor Supply (TSCO -0.77%) to expand its reach beyond traditional restaurant delivery into a broader marketplace.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue reached nearly $56.5 billion, which is a growth rate of approximately 13.9% over the previous twelve months. The company generated net income of around $3.5 billion during this period.

Shopify faces intense market competition from established platforms and niche providers that could limit its growth or force pricing changes. The company depends heavily on third-party cloud infrastructure from Alphabet (GOOGL -2.70%) and payment processors like PayPal Holdings (PYPL -5.46%). New risks involve the use of artificial intelligence, which could lead to inaccurate outputs or regulatory challenges.

Uber Technologies deals with ongoing legal and regulatory challenges regarding how it classifies its drivers, which could fundamentally change its business model. It competes globally in mobility and delivery against well-capitalized rivals such as Lyft (LYFT -2.54%) and DoorDash (DASH -2.88%). The platform is also vulnerable to macroeconomic shifts, as inflation or rising fuel prices can impact consumer spending on rides and deliveries. Finally, the company relies on the mobile marketplaces of Apple (AAPL -1.90%) and Alphabet to reach its vast user base.

Uber Technologies appears more reasonably priced as it trades at a much lower forward price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio and price-to-sales (P/S) ratio than Shopify.

Both are well-run companies. But if I had to pick only one stock, it would be Uber. It's not so much about anything Shopify is doing wrong; it's more about Uber growing its current business and the opportunities ahead.

Despite Uber being a well-established brand, its recent 2026 third-quarter earnings results showed that monthly active users increased by 14% to 215 million. Trips also increased by 16% to 4.1 billion, and gross bookings climbed 20% to roughly $60 billion. That shows not only that more people are signing up to use its platform, but that users are also booking more rides and deliveries.

For its sales growth opportunities, the focus should be on robotaxis, which could be a huge market. According to research from Goldman Sachs (GS -6.71%), the global robotaxi market size is expected to reach $415 billion by 2035. By the end of 2026, Uber expects to have 700 fully driverless vehicles on its platform, with more than 2,000 in 2027.

Shopify will continue to benefit from the growth of e-commerce sales. But for long-term investors, understanding that the roads over the next decade will be filled with more autonomous vehicles, Uber can be a centerpiece in that market, which should only grow in value.

#3007. Robinhood shares fall as crypto slump weighs on profits, offsetting gains in new businesses

Robinhood shares were down around 8% in pre-market trading on Wednesday as investors digested weaker-than-expected third-quarter earnings despite growth in options trading and prediction markets.

Here's how the brokerage performed in its quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with Wall Street estimates, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Robinhood's net income fell to $468 million, or 51 cents per share, from $556 million, or 61 cents per share, a year ago. Revenue represented a 12% increase from $1.27 billion in the same quarter last year.

Despite that gain, transaction-based revenue of $867 million came in below Wall Street estimates from StreetAccount of $879 million, putting focus on whether Robinhood can sustain momentum across its trading businesses and reduce its reliance on volatile market activity.

Robinhood has been diversifying its business as it tries to expand its brokerage app into a broader financial services platform. Options trading and event contracts have become increasingly important sources of transaction revenue, while the company has continued to invest in wealth management, subscriptions and other offerings.

Transaction-based revenue grew 19% from the same period last year to $867 million, driven primarily by a more than 500% increase in event contracts revenue. Additionally, options revenue grew 31% to $399 million and equities revenue rose 41% to $121 million.

Those gains were partially offset by cryptocurrency revenue, which fell 55% to $120 million.

Chief Financial Officer Shiv Verma told CNBC event contracts on crypto movements did well in the quarter, offering investors another way to express a view on crypto despite the slump in prices and spot trading.

Net deposits on the Robinhood platform totaled $15.7 billion for the quarter, equal to a 17% annualized growth rate based on total platform assets at the end of Q2 2026. Over the past year, net deposits totaled $71 billion, representing 21% growth compared with total platform assets at the end of Q3 2025.

—With reporting from CNBC's Robert Hum and Kate Rooney

#3003. In the Last Tech Bust, the S&P 500 Lost Money for a Decade. History Says Stocks With the Highest Yields Gained 5.4% a Year.

The S&P 500 lost money from 2000 to 2009, but the highest-yielding dividend stocks gained 5.4% a year. Here's what the last tech bust says about yield, and what it doesn't.

By Matt DiLallo – Nov 21, 2026 at 5:31PM EST

10 stocks we like better than S&P 500 Index ›

The AI trade seems to be unwinding fast. The Nasdaq Composite (^IXIC -5.28%) was recently down more than 15% from its early October peak, while the S&P 500 (^GSPC -3.70%) was approaching correction territory, with a roughly 10% slump from its all-time high. Nobody knows how this downturn will play out.

However, history offers a useful reference for what held up the last time a technology boom ended. From 2000 to 2009, a period that featured the dot-com crash, the S&P 500 posted negative returns, averaging a 0.8% annual decline. High-yielding dividend stocks, on the other hand, delivered an average annual total return of 5.4% over the same period, according to a Wellington Management study published by Hartford Funds. Here's what investors can learn from this history lesson.

The dot-com bubble burst in March 2000. It led many stock investors to return to fundamentals, focusing on metrics such as a company's price-to-earnings ratio and dividend yield instead of future monetization potential. Companies that generated real revenue and profits, and returned a portion of their income to investors via dividends, delivered the best returns during this period.

Wellington sorted dividend-paying stocks into five groups by yield, from the top 20% of payers to the bottom 20%. In the 2000s, the highest-yielding group gained 5.4% a year, while the lowest-yielding payers lost 1.7% a year. To put this in dollars, $1,000 compounding at 5.4% for 10 years grows to about $1,690, while $1,000 shrinking at 0.8% per year ends up near $923.

The decade before the dot-com bust looked very different for high-yielding dividend stocks. In the 1990s, the lowest-yielding payers kept pace with the S&P 500, while the highest-yielding group trailed it by a meaningful amount. Meanwhile, the last six years look a lot like the late 1990s. Since 2020, the lowest-yielding payers have beaten the market, while the highest-yielding group has trailed.

The highest-yielding dividend stocks have outperformed the market in five of 10 time periods from 1930 to 2025. That's the same percentage as the lowest yielders. The best performers were companies with higher dividend yields, though not the highest. These companies prioritized dividend payments (and growth) without sacrificing financial flexibility. This cohort outperformed the S&P 500 in six of those 10 periods, including in the 2000s (4.4% annualized return). They also delivered solid performance leading up to the dot-com crash (15.6% returns in the 1990s).

Here are three practical tips investors can employ in their own portfolios based on the data on dividend stocks:

A high yield can be as much a warning as a reward. A stock's yield rises when its price falls, and sometimes the market is pricing in a dividend cut. The data on cutters show how costly that can be. From 1973 through 2025, S&P 500 companies that cut or eliminated their dividends returned an average of -1% a year, compared with 10.2% for companies that raised theirs and 7.7% for the average stock, according to Ned Davis Research data published by Hartford. Cutters were also the most volatile group.

The last time a technology boom ended, the stocks that paid the most were the ones that held up best, while the lowest-yielding payers lost more than the market. That doesn't mean history will repeat itself, and the biggest yields carry their own risks, as dividend cutters show. However, the data makes a case for adding some yield to your portfolio before the next cycle turns.

Content costs are falling while the platform builds dedicated online creator tools and expands connected television advertising. Management is strengthening its subscription service and creator network while using artificial intelligence to accelerate output.

By focusing on disciplined investments and expanding revenue-sharing models, management aims to turn efficiency gains into consistent long-term cash flow.

#2991. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 stocks for the long haul

The ongoing earnings season, Middle East tensions and buzz ahead of the U.S. midterm elections have been keeping investors busy. Further, worries about elevated artificial intelligence spending are weighing on investor sentiment.

Investors looking beyond the short-term noise and focusing on long-term returns can track top Wall Street analysts to pick the right stocks, backed by strong research.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

This week's first stock pick is Datadog, an AI-powered observability and security platform. The company will announce its results for the third quarter on Nov. 4. Datadog is seeing strong demand for its offerings, thanks to cloud migration and rapid AI adoption. TipRanks' KPIs tool for DDOG shows the growth in its customer base over the past few years amid increasing market penetration and growing adoption of its products.

Heading into Q3 FY26 earnings, Bank of America analyst Koji Ikeda reiterated a buy rating on Datadog stock and raised his price target to $335 from $305. The analyst noted that industry checks and data indicate that infrastructure software demand is strengthening, with AI increasingly driving usage and growth.

Ikeda called Datadog and Atlassian his top picks, saying that companies that can show that AI is a tailwind to growth could see their valuation rerate, even at premium multiples. He added that the total addressable market for observability is growing faster than anticipated, driven by accelerated growth of enterprise AI and the rapid ramp of consumer AI agents.

The five-star analyst believes that focus will be on revenue growth, which he estimates to be 28.7% for Q3 FY26 versus the Street's consensus of 29.1%. Ikeda thinks that the market is underestimating the growth prospects of DDOG's security products. Meanwhile, he cautioned investors about concentration risk related to large customers.

"We think results will be supportive of our long-term thesis that Datadog is a strong share gainer in the rapidly expanding observability opportunity," said Ikeda.

Ikeda ranks No. 673 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 55% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.9%. See Datadog Statistics on TipRanks.

The next pick, semiconductor giant Nvidia, is also benefiting from AI-driven demand for its products. After attending Nvidia's GTC Berlin conference, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reaffirmed a buy rating on NVDA stock with a price target of $300, calling it a top pick in the semiconductor space.

The five-star analyst said that the conference supported his thesis that Nvidia continues to effectively navigate bottlenecks in the supply chain, land/power/shell, and funding. In fact, Moore is "convinced that all 3 aspects are opportunities for NVDA."

While Nvidia's GTC Berlin offered no major updates, Moore noted the conference reflected that the company continues to add to its significant advantages heading into 2027. The analyst said that Vera Rubin is gaining momentum, though details on next year's Rubin Ultra were lighter than expected.

With demand outpacing supply, Moore said that focus is on closing the gap. In this regard, he highlighted the supply chain deals on substrate, packaging and testing. While the analyst continues to view TSMC 3 nm wafer supply as the biggest challenge, he expects it to ease over time.

Moore ranks No. 142 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.4%. See Nvidia Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Aerospace company SpaceX recently announced solid third-quarter results, with its AI revenue surging to $7.56 billion from $813 million in the prior-year quarter.

Impressed by the results, Needham analyst Ryan Koontz reiterated a buy rating on SPCX stock with a price target of $250. Koontz highlighted that SpaceX's Q3 results significantly exceeded expectations, proving that the company's vertically integrated business model is driving massive scale.

Furthermore, Koontz emphasized the 195.2% sequential growth in SpaceX's AI revenue, fueled by wholesale spot compute deals. He noted management's increasing focus on its AI strategy, given that it is optimistic about adding 10 gigawatts of incremental capacity during 2027.

"With increasing confidence on Starship re-use, we meaningfully increase our estimates," said Koontz.

Koontz ranks No. 357 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 42% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.7%. See SpaceX Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2984. Federal Reserve Board releases results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 10:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Friday released the results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families.

The data provide a representative picture of what Americans own, how and how much they borrow, and how they bank. The current version of the survey has been undertaken every three years since 1989 and provides the public with an extensive time series of thorough information on the economic conditions of American families. Alongside the survey data, the Board issued its summary report entitled Changes in U.S. Family Finances from 2022 to 2025 (PDF), which examines changes in income, net worth, assets, debt, and financial vulnerability.

Key findings from the 2025 survey include:

In addition to the summary report, an interactive chartbook displaying changes in these financial characteristics over time is available here. Additional data from the survey, including extensive data examining other economic conditions of American families, is made available to researchers and the public here.

The survey is conducted for the Board by NORC, a social science research organization at the University of Chicago. Participants in the study are chosen at random from 119 geographic areas, including metropolitan areas and rural counties across the United States, using scientific sampling procedures that aim to produce a sample representative of American families.

#2977. Federal Reserve issues FOMC statement

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 2:00 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Open Market Committee approved the following statement for release by a 10 â 2 vote:

The Committee decided to maintain the target range for the federal funds rate at 3-3/4 to 4 percent, in support of the Federal Reserve's dual mandate. The Committee is continuing its policy of maintaining ample reserves in the banking system.

Economic activity is expanding at a strong pace. While uncertainty remains elevated owing, in part, to geopolitical developments, domestic spending has been resilient. Productivity growth is strong, and capital investment is robust. Job gains have kept pace with the workforce, and the unemployment rate has changed little.

Inflation remains elevated. The accumulation of more information will help the Committee determine the policy stance appropriate to ensure a timely return of inflation to the Committee's 2 percent goal. The Committee will deliver price stability.

Voting against the monetary policy action were Lisa D. Cook and Beth M. Hammack, who preferred to raise the target range for the federal funds rate by 1/4 percentage point at this meeting.

#2974. Gritty road trip was enough to give Knicks their mojo back

The Knicks left for their four-game road trip a bit unsettled, with questions circling them due to a rocky start to the season. After going 3-1 on the trip and beating potential playoff teams in the Raptors (twice) and Timberwolves, they return as a side rounding back into form and a better team than when they left.

"I feel like we're getting into a good rhythm offensively, defensively," Josh Hart said after the team's 102-99 win over the Timberwolves on Thursday night in Minneapolis. "I think you'll see some of the defensive rotations were on point. Obviously, we can be better. But we're continuing to learn, gain experience. Every year is different, every team is different. So I think we're doing that, and I think we're getting better.

"Whenever you win in the league, you continue to build confidence."

It is notable that they won in different ways: through free-flowing offensive showings; through grittier defensive efforts; and on Thursday, a combination of both. None of the three wins were blowouts — each game was on the line in crunch time.

New York Knicks center Karl-Anthony Towns looks on after an NBA basketball game against the Minnesota Timberwolves in Minneapolis, Friday, Nov. 20, 2026. AP Photo/Ellen Schmidt

"It shows that we're able to win in different ways," Hart said. "And that's what you have to do in the league. Some days, you're gonna shoot the cover off the ball and you can win by 10, 15, 20. Other games, it might be a possession game and offensively it's not really clicking, not knocking down shots, but you're getting stops defensively and you're able to pull out stops like that. I think it shows that this team can win games in different fashions. And that's the most important thing in the league."

A road trip like this can be beneficial, particularly early in the year as a new group gels. Despite all the continuity the Knicks have, they have been adamant that chemistry has to be reestablished.

They begin a five-game homestand Saturday, in which they'll host the surprisingly strong Trail Blazers, get a rematch with the Pacers (who could have back injured Tyrese Haliburton), play the Cavaliers for the third time already this season, see Giannis Antetokounmpo and the Heat for the first time and square off with the Hawks, who have started this season well after the Knicks eliminated them from the first round of the playoffs.

"For us to have a great road trip like this and win in so many different ways, it [says] a lot about our team," Karl-Anthony Towns said. "It's great when you can find a way to win. At the end of the day, it's hard to win in the NBA. I've learned that. It's hard to win. And you just have to find a way to win every single night. For us on this road trip to continue to find ways to win, it speaks to this team, and it's the most important thing when you play."

The Knicks' injury-free run came to an end.

New York Knicks forward OG Anunoby dunks during the first half of an NBA basketball game against the Minnesota Timberwolves, Thursday, Nov. 19, 2026, in Minneapolis. AP Photo/Ellen Schmidt

Jordan Clarkson (right plantar fasciitis) is out for Saturday's game against the Trail Blazers at Madison Square Garden. OG Anunoby (left calf tightness) is listed as questionable.

They might also get a break as Blazers star Deni Avdija is listed as questionable with a left foot contusion. He has missed the Blazers' last two games. Ja Morant remains out with a hamstring injury.

#2971. Anthropic raises $93.5 billion total as underwriters scoop up ‘greenshoe’ overallotment

Anthropic underwriters have officially exercised their overallotment of shares in the historic initial public offering, bringing the total raised to $93.5 billion, according to a release Friday.

The artificial intelligence company raised $81.3 billion on Wednesday and netted an additional $12.2 billion through the overallotment, surpassing SpaceX, which held a record IPO in June.

Anthropic said it sold an aggregate of 1,127,000,000 shares of its Class A common stock at $83 per share in the offering. It sold 980 million shares on Wednesday and its underwriters had the option to purchase up to an additional 147 million shares by Friday as part of the overallotment, which is commonly referred to as the "greenshoe."

Shares of the Claude maker opened trading at $111.50 on the Nasdaq on Thursday, but slid throughout the day and closed at $97.17. The stock sank 12% in premarket trading on Friday during a broad tech sell-off.

In addition to the sale of its Class A common stock, Anthropic closed a concurrent sale of its Class N common stock after a group of tech companies, including Advanced Micro Devices, Google, Microsoft, Nvidia and others, agreed to participate in a $26 billion private placement at the IPO price. Anthropic said Friday that aggregate gross proceeds from its IPO, including the private placement, totaled $119.5 billion, before deducting offering expenses.

Anthropic dodged an unforgiving series of obstacles on its road to the Nasdaq on Thursday, as investors brooded over Jensen Huang's shocking ouster at Nvidia, voters' stinging rebuke of the tech during the midterms and whether rising interest rates would stymie industry plans for a multitrillion-dollar AI infrastructure buildout.

CEO Dario Amodei co-founded the company in 2021 after he and a handful of other researchers, including his sister Daniela, quit their jobs at OpenAI, now Anthropic's chief rival. Anthropic is best known for developing a family of AI models called Claude, which have captivated the corporate world and fueled the company's historic rise.

The siblings celebrated the IPO as a "milestone" in an interview with CNBC on Thursday, but said there's more work ahead for Anthropic.

"For us, it's like one stop on the journey," Daniela Amodei said. "We think about this as, 'Wow, this is an incredible moment for Anthropic for our mission, but we're going to wake up tomorrow and we're going to be the same company.'"

#2970. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for consistent income

Democrats' midterm election win has put the artificial intelligence trade in question, as voters clearly expressed opposition to the rapid buildout of data centers. The stock market could remain volatile amid AI trade concerns, macro uncertainties, and geopolitical tensions.

In this scenario, investors can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios to earn steady income. However, the extensive universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Top Wall Street analysts' recommendations can help investors shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as these experts assign ratings after in-depth financial analysis.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Viper Energy (VNOM) is a subsidiary of Diamondback and owns and acquires mineral and royalty interests, primarily in the Permian Basin. The company reported upbeat third-quarter earnings, supported by recent acquisitions and continued development activity from Diamondback and third-party operators. Viper announced a base cash dividend of 50 cents per share for Q3 2026, payable on Nov. 19. Based on the base and variable dividends announced over the past 12 months, VNOM's dividend yield stands at 5.7%.

Following the Q3 results, TD Cowen analyst Aaron Bilkoski reiterated a buy rating on Viper Energy stock with a price target of $61. The analyst noted that the company delivered solid Q3 results, with production, including oil and natural gas liquids, exceeding both TD Cowen and consensus estimates.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that the key update was the announcement of two royalty acquisitions for $809 million in cash, including deferred consideration, and $82 million of newly issued shares.

Given the recently announced base dividend hike, Bilkoski expects Viper to direct incremental free cash flow toward buybacks. While the acquisitions increase leverage, the analyst believes that Viper has enough capacity for opportunistic buybacks.

Bilkoski noted that Viper has historically delivered one of the highest production per share growth profiles in his royalty coverage, but still trades at a notable discount to the royalty peer average and the company's historical FCF yield range. "In our view, its above-average growth warrants a higher valuation," said the analyst.

Bilkoski ranks No. 525 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.80%. See Viper Energy Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Exxon Mobil (XOM) reported robust Q3 2026 profits, as its U.S. refineries boosted production to capitalize on the high diesel fuel prices sparked by the conflict in the Middle East. The oil and gas giant announced a quarterly dividend of $1.07 per share, payable on Dec. 10, reflecting a 3.9% increase. This marks Exxon's 44th consecutive year of dividend increases. At an annualized dividend of $4.28 per share, XOM offers a yield of 2.53%.

Impressed by the Q3 print, TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Exxon stock and raised his price target to $193 from $180 to reflect higher downstream earnings, with the expectation of increased mid-cycle margins.

Gabelman noted that the strength in Exxon's refining business continues to offset Middle East-related headwinds. He added that the company increased its ownership in LNG projects, which should reduce its Middle East concentration over time from 75% to 50% and lower the likelihood of diversification-focused mergers and acquisitions. Beyond LNG, Gabelman expects Exxon's growth in the 2030s to come from Guyana, Kashagan, Permian, and possibly Venezuela.

Gabelman noted that Exxon announced a dividend hike of nearly 4% and plans to maintain buybacks at $20 billion for 2027. "The decision to maintain reduces the pro-cyclicality nature of the buyback," said the analyst.

Gabelman ranks No. 347 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.90%. See Exxon Mobil Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Williams (WMB), which owns and operates energy infrastructure to deliver natural gas, reported strong third-quarter results. The company announced a quarterly dividend of $0.525 per share, or $2.10 per share annualized, payable on Dec. 28. WMB offers a dividend yield of 3.09%.

In reaction to the Q3 2026 results, CIBC analyst Robert Catellier reaffirmed a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $82. The analyst noted that Q3 results were essentially in line with expectations and reflected continued strength in execution across WMB's infrastructure portfolio.

Catellier said that Williams' Q3 performance was driven by higher transmission, storage, and power generation revenues, as well as contributions from the Momentum Midstream acquisition.

He noted that Williams maintained its full-year EBITDA guidance of $8.3 billion to $8.5 billion. In fact, results are tracking toward the higher end of the guidance range, backed by contributions from recently completed projects and the Momentum Midstream deal. WMB continues to target long-term EBITDA compound annual growth rate of at least 11% through 2030.

Catellier expects WMB's long-term targets to be supported by incremental transmission and storage opportunities, including Transco's Southeast Supply Enhancement II project and an 84 MMcf/d expansion of the Garden Connector project.

Catellier ranks No. 667 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 9.60%. See Williams Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2966. Lakers survive in ugly game to down Kings

Another sloppy game saw the Lakers struggle to the finish line before coming away victorious against the Kings on Friday, 106-104.

LA remained in a funk not dissimilar from Wednesday's loss to the Jazz, trailing by as many as 11 points. The bench helped spark a run in the third quarter to get back into the game.

A clutch three from Quentin Grimes late pushed LA ahead by three with a minute remaining in the game. Zach LaVine had a chance to win it for the Kings at the buzzer but missed.

Matisse Thybulle got off to a hot start with two triples. Everyone in the starting lineup, minus Ziarie Williams, scored a bucket. Keegan Murray and Domantas Sabonis combined for all 12 of Sacramento's first points.

LA struggled with turnovers early, committing four. Luka Dončić started scoring and was already close to double figures with nine points. Sabonis and Murray were still leading the Kings with eight points each.

At the end of the first, the game was tied. Los Angeles was shooting better than the Kings, 52% to 44%. Luka ended the quarter with 13 points.

Walker Kessler started the second period strong, scoring four points. Zach LaVine started to heat up for the Kings, scoring four points. It was Sabonis, though, who continued to keep Sacramento in the game with 15 points.

Quentin Grimes missed his first four shots in the half, but finally scored on a layup. He had six points, mostly thanks to free throws.

The game was tied again with six minutes left in the half.

Cameron Carr had an immediate impact off the bench, scoring five points. Los Angeles struggled with their offense. They were 7-20 from the field in the quarter. Sacramento went on a 9-0 scoring run.

As a result, the Kings were up by eight at halftime.

De'Andre Hunter scored first to start the third period for Sacramento. The Kings seemed to have all the momentum as Acuff Jr. also drained a triple. LA, though, had some life as they scored six in a row, forcing Sacramento to call a timeout.

As soon as it looked like the Lakers were gaining momentum, Sacramento struck back almost immediately. Thybulle drained a three to make it a one-point game before the Kings responded with a 6-0 run.

LA started gaining more defensive momentum thanks to Jarred Vanderbilt's extra effort plays. That included a sequence in which he got three rebounds in a row that resulted in Sandro Mamukelashvili converting on a three-point play.

At the end of the third, the purple and gold were only down two.

The final frame started with a 3-pointer from Kessler that received a nice crowd pop. Collin Sexton also began to take over offensively, scoring four points. Los Angeles had kicked up their defense, and in return, their offense was flowing more smoothly.

Thybulle knocked down his fourth triple of the game, giving the team a five-point lead with 7:36 left.

Williams finally got a 3-pointer to fall after several games of struggling from behind the arc. Sexton was now the only player off the bench in double figures with 10. Hunter continued his strong play for the Kings, converting on a three-point play. He had 25 points.

At the 3:30 mark, the purple and gold were up by five.

The teams swapped big shots to either tie or take the lead. Luka was poked in the eye by Murray, but remained in the game.

The Lakers went up by two after Grimes made a pair of free throws. Hunter responded with a jumper on the other end to tie it again.

With 1:12 left, Grimes knocked down a much-needed three to put the team up by three.

Sacramento missed a shot. Luka then had the ball and turned it over. The Lakers intentionally fouled up three to put LaVine on the line and he split the pair. Kessler was then fouled after rebounding the miss but missed both free throws.

LaVine had the opportunity to win with a deep three and missed.

Luka finished with 25 points, four rebounds and eight assists. Kessler ended with 15 points and three blocks. Thybulle pitched in with 14 points. Grimes logged 13 points with five rebounds and seven assists.

Carr had five points off the bench. Vanderbilt scored four points with four rebounds and two assists. Mamu finished with nine points, nine rebounds and two assists. Sexton and Jake LaRavia combined for 16 points.

The Lakers' next game will be against the Utah Jazz on Monday at 7:00 PM PT.

You can follow Karin on Twitter at @KarinAbcarians.
