#2962. Federal Reserve Board announces pricing, effective January 1, 2027, for payment services the Federal Reserve Banks provide to banks and credit unions, and changes to streamline and adjust their check services

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 1:30 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday announced pricing, effective January 1, 2027, for payment services the Federal Reserve Banks provide to banks and credit unions, such as the clearing of checks, automated clearing house (ACH) transactions, instant payments, and wholesale payment and settlement services.

By law, the Federal Reserve must establish fees to recover the costs, including imputed costs, of providing payment services over the long run. The Federal Reserve expects to recover 101.5 percent of actual and imputed expenses in 2027, including the return on equity that would have been earned if a private-sector firm provided the services. Overall, price changes for 2027 will result in an estimated 1.4 percent average price increase for established, mature services.

In addition, the Federal Reserve Banks announced changes to streamline and adjust their check services. As described in the request for information on check services issued last year, the volume of checks processed by the Federal Reserve Banks is declining, but checks remain an important payment instrument for many consumers and businesses. The changes were informed by public feedback and ensure that the Federal Reserve Banks continue to provide check services efficiently. The adjustments include optimizing check processing windows during low-volume periods, increasing fees that banks and credit unions pay to process checks, and other operational updates. These changes do not impact consumers' and businesses' ability to pay with or receive checks and only impact how banks and credit unions process checks with Federal Reserve Banks.

The entire 2027 fee schedule for all of the Federal Reserve's payment services is published on FRBservices.org®. Additional information on the Federal Reserve's payments services, including service usage statistics, is available on the Board's website.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call 202-452-2955.â¯â¯

#2961. Phoenix Suns vs Utah Jazz: Recap and Final Score

The Utah Jazz are back to their losing ways, falling short against the Phoenix Suns with a final score of 111-101.

The final score depicts a game that was more competitive than reality. Utah's successful trip to Los Angeles was a bit of fools gold; once again, the Jazz were slow in their offense, failed to bring intensity on defense, and had lapses of focus that are unacceptable for a team that can no longer use its youth to justify its mistakes.

The Jazz missed 13 free throws. They turned over the ball 14 times. They spent multiple possessions walking up the court, entering their offense late, dribbling the air out of the ball, and taking a shot without passing the ball. Look no further than Keyonte George for a primary offender: his four turnovers, ill-advised shots, and lackadaisical effort led him to 14 points on 4-17 shooting. The Jazz's shooting struggles from three returned, finishing the night 10-29 from three as a team. The combination of George/Peterson/Bailey ended the night an astonishing 0-21 from three.

Darryn Peterson led the Jazz with 20 points, 10 rebounds, and eight assists. Peterson and Josh Green were the only Jazz players to finish with a positive plus/minus, sharing a plus-4 each. Importantly, Peterson appeared to tweak his leg after landing on a drive to the basket, although he appeared to finish the game without issue.

For the Suns, this wasn't the prettiest win. Phoenix shot only 45% from the field and 29% from three as a team, led by Devin Booker's 23 points on 9-22 shooting. That said, they hustled and played with an intensity that was lacking from Utah all night.

The Jazz return to action on Sunday, November 22nd to host the Dallas Mavericks.

#2958. Agencies extend comment period for proposed third-party risk management guidance

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 4:15 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation, the Federal Reserve Board, the National Credit Union Administration, and the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency today announced that they will extend, until December 16, the comment period for proposed guidance on managing risks associated with third-party relationships. Comments on the proposal were originally due November 16.

The proposed guidance reflects the agencies' supervisory experience and lessons learned from examining financial institutions' third-party risk management practices. It is intended to help banks and credit unions tailor their risk management practices to the risks associated with individual third-party relationships. The proposed guidance takes a principles-based approach and, like all supervisory guidance, is non-binding.

When finalized, the federal bank regulatory agencies plan to rescind existing third-party risk management guidance and replace it with the finalized guidance to promote consistency and prudent innovation in the banking industry. NCUA also plans to rescind its existing third-party risk management guidance and will conduct its own reviews to update related guidance as needed.

Also today, the Federal Reserve Board separately announced that it will extend, until December 16, the comment period on a proposed third-party risk management guide specifically for Federal Reserve-supervised traditional community banks.

Federal Register notice: Proposed Third-Party Risk Management Guide for Traditional Community Banking Organizations

#2957. Trump says he discussed Jensen Huang with Xi; Bessent tells CNBC Nvidia chip export policy is unchanged

President Donald Trump on Friday said he and Chinese leader Xi Jinping discussed their shared affinity for former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang during their talks in China this week. Earlier in the day, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said the ousted leader was not mentioned in separate talks he had with Chinese officials.

"I did discuss that with President Xi, and he said, 'Oh, brilliant, brilliant! He's a brilliant man.' He is," Trump said at a White House press conference.

Asked earlier Friday on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" if Huang, a close ally of Trump's, had come up in "any of your discussions with the Chinese," Bessent said "no," without elaborating.

A senior Trump administration official later said that both Trump's and Bessent's remarks were correct, and that both were addressing their own conversations. The official spoke on condition of anonymity to discuss those conversations.

Trump also reiterated that he would like to see Huang — who was replaced by interim CEO Colette Kress after resigning amid allegations of an extramarital affair with a Chinese spy — be restored as Nvidia's CEO.

"I would love to see Jensen be given a second chance," Trump said. "You know, we're a country of second chances, and I'd love to be able to see him. That's a very important company to us, and he put us in a position where we are literally 10 years advanced over other companies."

Nvidia's board is not considering bringing Huang back, a person familiar with the matter told CNBC earlier this week.

The president's comments came amid growing scrutiny of advanced U.S. chips, including those made by Nvidia, ending up in China either legally or by being diverted via third countries.

Both Trump's and Bessent's remarks are potentially an indication that the Trump administration may not focus on cracking down on China's efforts to acquire advanced chips.

Bessent suggested that U.S. policy allowing the sale of Nvidia's powerful H200 AI chips to China remains unchanged following the talks in Shenzhen this week.

"We've already agreed that the ... H200s are the threshold level of chips that could be sold to the Chinese, and, you know, that continues as planned," Bessent said.

"I wouldn't anticipate any change in the policy," he added after CNBC's Sara Eisen noted that Nvidia said it had stopped selling H200s to China.

The administration ignoring the subject of Huang's affair with a possible Chinese spy — whose Singapore-based company Megaspeed has been accused of acting as a cutout for Beijing — contrasts with the importance Trump has placed on beating China in the race to artificial intelligence dominance. It could suggest that despite his support for the U.S. AI industry, Trump is less concerned with addressing China's ongoing efforts to acquire U.S. semiconductors.

Trump has also defended Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick, who has come under fire from multiple directions for reportedly slow-walking export restrictions on Chinese chip sales. "I understand both sides of it," Trump said on Monday when he also said he's like to see Huang back as CEO.

Bessent's appearance on CNBC came after Trump's return from a tour through Asia that saw him meet with Xi and Russian President Vladimir Putin in Shenzhen, China.

The meeting was historic as the first time the leaders of the three countries had met without other nations' counterparts. But the trilateral summit appeared to yield few deliverables on any number of the most pressing global issues related to the countries, including the Ukraine and Iran wars or the ongoing AI race.

Bessent told CNBC on Friday that there was a "robust discussion" between Trump and Xi on trade policy and AI.

They "agreed that humans should always be involved in any military decisions that SI makes, and the SI should not be allowed to be autonomous for major military actions," he said, abbreviating the term "super intelligence," Trump's attempted rebrand of AI.

In a CNBC appearance last week, Bessent lamented the "leakage" of U.S.-made AI chips to China and blamed the Commerce Department, led by Lutnick, for slow-walking export controls on suspected Chinese cutouts like Megaspeed.

Multiple Trump administration leaders have long sought to impose export restrictions on Megaspeed, but Commerce "has the pen" and "you will have to ask them about their internal processes," Bessent said in that interview.

On Friday, however, Bessent was much more tight-lipped.

Asked if there is a dispute between him and Lutnick over how aggressively the U.S. should be pursuing Chinese export controls, Bessent said, "I think we're all committed to following President Trump's instructions."

"I think we are going to see that the ... proper chip restrictions are in place, and the remote access has been curtailed," he added. Commerce recently took steps to close one of China's paths to accessing AI processors.

Correction: Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent spoke to CNBC's "Squawk on the Street." A previous version of this story misstated the name of the show.

Editor's note: This story and headline have been updated following additional information provided by the Trump administration about the nature of Bessent's and Trump's meetings in China.

#2955. Harden’s 51 points leads Cavs over the Pacers

James Harden put on a vintage performance, dropping an electric 51 points, Donovan Mitchell added 20 of his own, and Mario Hezonja put up a double-double off the bench to lead the Cleveland Cavaliers to a 128-119 victory over the visiting Indiana Pacers in an NBA Cup matchup. Cleveland sits just behind the Miami Heat in NBA Cup Group Stage play, while the Pacers fall to 1-2.

Harden finished 15-26 from the floor, including 7 for 11 from beyond the arc, to go with six assists and six rebounds. Oh, and in classic fashion, he was also 14-14 from the free throw line.

Cleveland now sits just over .500 at 9-8, beating an Indiana team that was still without star guard Tyrese Haliburton. The Cavs did not have forward Evan Mobley, who is undergoing treatment for a great toe sprain. Peyton Watson returned from a one-game absence due to a back injury, and provided 15 points, four rebounds, and three assists.

The two teams were neck and neck in the first half, with the Cavs holding a slight one-point advantage at the break. But it was the 37-year-old Harden who took over in the third quarter, helping Cleveland outscore Indiana 43-23 in the frame. Harden's 51-point effort, the 26th of his career, puts him past Kobe Bryant for third on the NBA's all-time 50-point game list.

"My idol, the person I watched growing up," Harden said in a postgame interview when told he passed Bryant on the all-time list.

Harden has been playing much better of late following his slow start, something that the former MVP has built into his conditioning strategy of playing into game shape. Mitchell's 20 points mostly came in the second half, but he helped apply pressure in that "Cavalanche" of a third quarter that allowed Cleveland to pull away.

While most of the attention will go toward the 50-point effort, Hezonja's ten points and ten rebounds off the bench continue a stretch of solid play. When the Cavs play well, and with energy, chances are that Hezonja is part of the reason why. He has made smart passes, rebounded the basketball well, and seems very comfortable on offense getting to his spots around the paint. It won't be the headline of this game, but Cleveland must be pleased with their signing of Hezonja this past offseason.

The Cavs also moved to 2-1 in NBA Cup play and put themselves in position to advance out of the Group Stage, just behind the Heat. Cleveland will not play this weekend, with their next game coming on Monday in a rematch against the Chicago Bulls. Tip-off is at 8:00 p.m EST from the Windy City.

#2954. Three Things I’m Looking at in the New York Jets vs Los Angeles Chargers Game

The New York Jets are headed to the City of Angels for a football game where stadium seats cost less than a cheeseburger in that stadium. A rare Sunday where I get to wait until 4pm to be utterly disappointed instead of the classic 1:00 devastation. I figured the Jets hadn't been to SoFi too often, but I was shocked to learn that they haven't been there since two trips in 2020.

The first trip should sound fairly familiar: a Week 11 contest between two terrible teams that had only two combined wins. The Jets employed an old journeyman QB with no tangible long-term future with the team, and the Chargers had Justin Herbert. Had the Chargers not won this past Monday, this would've been the exact description of both Jets vs. Chargers games at SoFi Stadium.

The second trip was much more memorable: the 0-13 Jets faced a talented 9-4 Rams team and shocked the world by finally winning their first game of the season. We all remember this as the game that shifted the Jets' odds of having the first overall pick when every poor fanbase was looking to 'Tank For Trevor'. The Jets won the following week and finished 2-14, securing the second overall pick and failing to properly tank.

For those of you who value tanking so much, the Jets have an opportunity to repeat history by winning a game in SoFi Stadium and dropping further down the draft board. But don't fret: if history repeats the first trip to SoFi, it would mean the Chargers winning a shootout against the Jets, and you get what you want.

For what it's worth, I hate the toxicity of tank culture. For 16 years, the Jets have been losing time and time again, getting high draft picks, and the team isn't any better. How about we root for wins? Especially this year, with the Colts having released Daniel Jones and the Jets having two additional first-round picks, why do you care so much whether the Jets' specific pick is 6th or 11th? The flexibility to move anywhere on the board will be there regardless.

We keep saying late wins don't matter, we keep saying let's get the highest pick, isn't that the definition of insanity?! We're literally yearning for the same thing every year and expecting a different result. Clearly, the high draft picks alone aren't enough to save this team. Especially when they miss (Zach Wilson, Mekhi Becton, Olu Fashanu?).

Anyway, let's get back to this game. Regardless of which outcome you're rooting for, let's discuss what's worth looking at:

Can someone tell the New York Jets that the FAA has resumed flights? They must still think they're grounded. In each of the last two games, the Jets have scored exactly zero touchdowns and four field goals for a total of 12 points in each game. With all the firepower at their disposable, this is a pretty inexcusable and mind-numbing stat. We talked last week about specifically scoring in the red zone, but now they can't even throw a TD bomb from outside the red zone. Their last touchdown was a 21-yard run by Braelon Allen in the 3rd quarter against the Raiders.

Someone on social media said it's Breece Hall's fault they haven't scored a touchdown in the two games they've played since his return. Seeing how Braelon only scored two touchdowns in his five-game break out, I can't co-sign that claim, but perhaps decreasing his workload to just eight carries per game is hurting the offense overall. Allen's still averaging a healthy 4.5 yards per carry with Breece back. Hall is averaging 3.9 yards per carry and has had 26 carries to Braelon's 16. It's definitely interesting to consider what the rushing totals would be if it were Braelon who had 26 carries and Breece who had 16.

While the Chiefs defense has been good, they had allowed a TD in six of the seven games they played before the Jets, despite facing bottom-feeders like the Dolphins, Raiders, and Colts during that time. The Bills had allowed seven of eight, with the exception being Joe Fagnano's first (and perhaps only) NFL start ever. Sure those are two good teams, but neither defensive unit is giving the '85 Bears a run for their money.

How about this hypothesis: Geno Smith isn't doing enough to get this offense going. We mentioned last week how three Jets receivers sit atop the league-wide ranks for average separation score. Geno is not getting people open, that's a combination of the receivers themselves and Frank Reich. Geno's job is to hit people when they're open, or at least not completely covered and he's failing to do that.

A quick look at Geno's numbers from game-to-game tell the story. He's been afraid to throw interceptions, so instead he makes a safer throw or holds onto the ball too long and takes a bad sack. After a three-week stretch where he aired out the ball more freely but threw an interception in each game, it seems apparent that his fear has returned. He stopped throwing picks, but had a huge uptick in sacks (again).

In these past two weeks of safe Geno, he is throwing 58.5% completion, 0 touchdowns, 0 interceptions, 6 sacks in 10 quarters, and a paltry 5.9 yards per attempt. To compare that to the previous 8 games, he was throwing 69% completion, 12 touchdowns, 3 interceptions, 23 sacks in 32 quarters and 8 yards per attempt. The issue is that now he's not hitting those "safe throws" either – and the offense as a whole is suffering.

You could see pretty clearly the stark difference of Cade's time in the game. He moved the ball down the field, was absolutely fearless, and while he was much less accurate than Geno he was still able to make plays due to his willingness to trust his playmakers to make plays – which they did. Geno is the more accurate option, he obviously is better at pre-snap recognition at this point in their careers, but he needs to find Cade's level of fearlessness if the offense is going to take flight again.

This week is the latest in a weekly tradition of telling you guys to buckle up for a thrashing by the opposing team's running back. It was looking like the Jets would finally get a break from facing a star running back, as Omarion Hampton was struggling(!) the first 8 weeks of the season. In his first seven games, he was only averaging 44 rushing yards per game on 3.5 yards per carry and three touchdowns in that span. Terrible numbers.

In the last two games, however, he must've remembered he was a first round pick last year because now he's averaging 81.5 yards per game on 4.7 yards per carry and one touchdown in that span. He has been explosive and a huge part of the reason why a team that was only averaging 14.1 points per game with 0 wins is now averaging 22.5 over it's last two and split 1-1.

What can the Jets do to stop him? Smart money would say 'nothing', and bet the over on Hampton's yards. FanDuel currently has his rushing yards at 62.5, are they kidding? The Jets are on a streak of allowing 80+ rushing yards to the opposing RB1, and almost had bonafide scrub Corey Kiner reach 62.5 (he had 57) in just 11 carries in garbage time in Foxborough. I won't go back and keep talking about Kyle Monangai's performance, let's keep it to the last five games:

Why include that last column? To make it clear that for this ineptitude, game script is irrelevant. The Jets could be down multiple scores, up multiple scores; it could be a one-score game. The only constant is the opposing leading rusher dominating our hopeless group. Our hopeless group is now 30th in rushing yards allowed per game, facing a team that's surprisingly 10th on the year despite being winless until Monday. Their game plan is clear: Run it early and run it often.

Let's make money together: Hampton over 62.5 yards + Hampton touchdown. If you're a risk-taker like me, you can alt Hampton's rush yards to 80+, add Herbert 150+ pass yards, and add Geno +175 pass yards. Let's turn $20 into $100 to dry our tears if we don't get our way on Sunday.

I'm super curious to know how many people reading this article have ever watched an episode of The Dukes of Hazzard. It was a great show whose influence on pop culture still resonates today in several ways. Unfortunately for us, I'm fairly confident the phrase "a great show" will never describe the Jets' defense this year. Despite offseason improvements at every position, the team has been absolutely horrid at stopping other teams when it's not raining.

They allow 15 points per rain game and 28.3 points per game in clear skies. That 28.3 number would rank 2nd worst in the NFL if it were the full-season average. Perhaps this utter failure is why Aaron Glenn, who famously said defensive play-calling is his superpower, decided to give up play-calling to a guy who had never called plays in the NFL before. Yes, Brian Duker, the first-time defensive coordinator who was hired due to familiarity with Glenn, was suddenly calling the shots against arguably the best team in the NFL.

I can write an entirely separate piece on what that means for the big picture of the Aaron Glenn Error and how that reads as a symbolic waving of the white flag, but instead I'll focus on this game. Is he calling the plays for this game? If so, how does the defense respond?

The team finally caught their second interception in 27 games, and maybe they can thank their playcaller, Brian Duker, for that. This Sunday, they face a quarterback who threw an interception in every single game until this past Monday. Can Duker get Herbert feeling jittery? We also saw a brief Will McDonald sighting last week after weeks of silence. With him wanting a bigger role and guaranteed to remain a Jet this season, can Duker get more out of him than Glenn did? I will be watching with bated breath. Let's go Jets!

The Federal Reserve Board on Friday released results from a survey of senior financial officers at banks about their strategies and practices for managing reserve balances. The Senior Financial Officer Survey is used by the Board to obtain information about banks' reserve balance management strategies and practices, their expectations around the size of their balance sheets and deposit balances their activity in money markets, and their views on other matters, such as operations on weekends and holidays.

The survey was conducted in collaboration with the Federal Reserve Bank of New York between September 18, 2026, and September 28, 2026, and includes responses from banks that held around three-fourths of total banking system reserve balances at the time of the survey.

#2951. Quentin Grimes' late three-pointer lifts Lakers to narrow win over Kings

The Lakers just finished a stretch during which they played four games in six days, a stretch that included back-to-back games this week, a stretch in which their mental and physical fortitude was tested.

They were not able to hold up during Wednesday night's defeat to the Utah Jazz, but the Lakers were able to bounce back with a 106-104 win over the hapless Sacramento Kings on Friday night at Crypto.com Arena behind another winning shot from Quentin Grimes.

Even though the Lakers were facing one-win Sacramento, they had to fight to the final few precious seconds to improve to 14-3 and 2-1 in NBA Cup play. They only secured the win after guard Zach LaVine missed a three-pointer just before the buzzer.

Read more: How the Lakers' Quentin Grimes wants to maintain his hot shooting, even off the bench

Lakers coach JJ Redick said he had some choice words for his players at halftime.

"I feel I did a decent job of regulating at halftime. They may have a differing opinion of that," Redick said. "Can't repeat every word I used. The main message was, 'We've got to be tougher. We've got to bring a little bit of energy. Certain nights, whether it's the team, how we're playing, the atmosphere in the arena, you can just be a little flat.' And I thought we just had to be tougher and pick up our energy. They accepted the challenge."

The score was tied 103-103 when the Lakers forced Kings rookie guard Darius Acuff Jr. into a turnover.

Grimes then hit a step-back three with 56.3 seconds left to give the Lakers a 106-103 lead.

It marked the second time this week Grimes made a winning three, the first coming off a banked-in trey Sunday at Phoenix with 32.7 seconds left — the day he said, "I called game."

Lakers center Walker Kessler scores off an offensive rebound against the Sacramento Kings on Friday. (Gina Ferazzi / Los Angeles Times)

"I think so," Grimes said, smiling. "Luckily, didn't have to bank it in, it just kind of went in pretty good. But yeah, it feels good to get another one of those games in, for sure."

Grimes didn't have the best shooting night. He was two for seven from the field, one for five from three-point range. Grimes missed his first three-pointer in the fourth quarter.

But he was unfazed with the game on the line, hitting a 26-foot jumper.

"Yeah, it don't matter. [You] shoot the next one. I mean, [it's a] make-or-miss league," said Grimes, who scored a career-high 47 points against Charlotte on Tuesday. "You gonna make it, you gonna miss it. Don't matter if you go 0 for 10 or you can go 10 for 10. Next one, get it up, and then luckily that was the biggest shot in the game and the shot of the night."

After Acuff missed a three-point attempt, Luka Doncic turned the ball over, giving the basketball back to the Kings (1-14) with 23 seconds left.

Lakers star Luka Doncic drives to the basket against Sacramento Kings guard Zach LaVine (8) and forward Keegan Murray in the first half Friday. (Gina Ferazzi / Los Angeles Times)

LaVine made one of two free throws to pull the Kings to within 106-104.

Then Walker Kessler, who had 15 points and seven rebounds, missed two free throws with 5.3 seconds left, leaving the door open for the Kings.

But LaVine missed his three-point try as time expired, allowing the Lakers to escape on a night when Doncic had 25 points and Matisse Thybulle had 14.

So, it appeared that whatever Redick spewed at his team at halftime resonated.

"Yeah, I mean he yells," Doncic said. "But I'm used to it. So, got us all fired up. So, sometimes you get to yell."

Austin Reaves missed his fourth consecutive game because of a left heel bruise, but Redick said his star guard is making progress. The Lakers next play Monday night at home against the Utah Jazz and then again Friday at San Antonio. The hope, Redick said, is to get Reaves back for one of those games. Redick said Reaves had a "great day" on the court Friday and "made a lot of progress."

"We're trying to manage his pain," Redick said. "So, hopefully he'll be back in one of the next two games."

#2950. Warsh wants Fed officials to talk less. Instead, they’re talking more

In his first six months as Federal Reserve chairman, Kevin Warsh has diligently avoided signaling where he sees monetary policy and interest rates headed. His colleagues have been only too happy to fill the void.

Fed officials have been rushing to the microphones lately, voicing sometimes conflicting opinions on how the central bank should be handling an inflation bug that the economy can't seem to shake.

Most agree that rates should be raised — and soon. Some are willing to hike at a measured pace as conditions play out, while others, such as Governor Christopher Waller and St. Louis Fed President Alberto Musalem, entertain the thought of more aggressive hikes in a shorter period of time.

Largely on the sidelines in the debate is Warsh, who has expressed disdain for that sort of forward guidance and argued that it handcuffs policymakers long before they actually have to make a decision. But that hasn't stopped most of the other 18 officials who sit in on Federal Open Market Committee meetings.

In fact, according to a Bank of America analysis, there has been more so-called Fedspeak in the past two weeks than in any similar period since the start of 2025.

"Warsh's refusal to guide markets might be encouraging the rest of the FOMC to speak more, not less," Bank of America economist Aditya Bhave said in a note Thursday.

"There is always a range of views across the 19 FOMC participants. Typically, the Chair would weigh in, indicating to markets whether the hawks or doves are in ascendence," he added. "Absent this leadership, however, both sides might feel incentivized to speak more often and more forcefully."

The increasingly vocal debate comes at a critical time for the Fed and has myriad policy implications.

Bhave noted "a deepening divide" on the FOMC. The Fed's decision not to increase its benchmark rate at its October meeting "increasingly looks like a mistake," and as a result "the hawks have been very vocal of late," he wrote. Leading the charge is Waller, who has raised the possibility of hiking by half a percentage point when the committee next meets in December.

Minutes from the October meeting hint at the tension, particularly when it comes to Warsh's refusal to provide guidance.

The summary related that some officials who were looking for a hike in October "could have supported no change in the federal funds rate provided that it was clearly understood by market participants that further tightening was likely" — a statement that points toward the forward guidance that Warsh has insisted the Fed avoid.

The minutes "revealed that some officials favoring a hike would have accepted a hold if the Fed had clearly communicated further tightening," market veteran Ed Yardeni wrote Thursday. "Warsh's communication philosophy may thus be limiting the Fed's flexibility precisely when it needs it most."

Cleveland Fed President Beth Hammack, who voted against the October pause in favor of a quarter-point hike, tiptoed around the forward guidance issue during a recent public appearance.

Prefacing some observations she made about where she sees policy headed, Hammack noted that, "Those are not promises. Those are not commitments. They're not forward guidance, which is a hot-button topic. But what they are are a conditional set of expectations of where I think the economy can go, based on what I'm seeing."

Hammack's comments illustrate a needle that Fed officials have been trying to thread: continuing to be transparent about their thinking without appearing locked into a future policy that changing economic conditions could render obsolete.

Speaking Thursday, Chicago Fed President Austan Goolsbee framed the argument around credibility.

"I am sympathetic to the view that [Warsh] has expressed that let's not be so, in in our language, forward guidance-oriented, saying here is what I think rates are going to be at the next meeting," Goolsbee said, calling such pronouncements "falsely precise."

However, he quickly separated forward guidance from transparency.

"I think central banks should be careful about doing anything that undermines credibility," Goolsbee said. "That said, I also think that it's pretty important [that] each person who sits around that table explain how they see the world. What do you see in business conditions? You don't want to surprise everybody."

Taking a slightly different tack, Philadelphia Fed President Anna Paulson, in a speech Thursday on policy communication, insisted that markets can figure out where policymakers are going without specific guidance so long as they understand how economic developments will influence decisions.

Paulson also defended the variety of voices coming from the Fed.

"But volume is not the same as clarity," she said. "The measure is not how much we say. It is whether we are understood." The mixture of voices "guards against any single communication becoming the only one that matters."

Financial markets have taken the plethora of Fed communications and interpreted it in a fairly hawkish way.

Traders are implying a 100% chance of at least a quarter percentage point hike in December, with a very small probability of a half-point move, according to the CME Group's FedWatch. From there, odds are high for a follow-up increase in January, then perhaps one more before the end of 2027.

While current inflation indicators are running above 3%, compared to the Fed's 2% target, market measures of longer-term inflation have declined.

Five-year forward expectations, as indicated through the Treasury market, are at their lowest since April. That suggests investors could be less concerned about inflation over the longer term, even as policymakers warn that current price pressures remain elevated.

Still, a sclerotic communications strategy from the Fed amid volatile economic and geopolitical currents could prove dangerous ahead.

"The result of this back-and-forth is undue rates volatility in already turbulent times," BofA's Bhave said.

For his part, Warsh has been advocating for a "family fight" atmosphere inside the Fed, arguing that competing viewpoints deserve a full airing as the committee works towards decisions.

"There are benefits to shaping the market's future expectations. Perhaps this is part of the 'family fight' that Warsh keeps referencing in his pressers," said Tom Graff, chief investment officer at advisory firm Facet. "That family fight seems to be happening out in public."

#2949. Tokenization platform Securitize gains 10% postmarket on Q3 results, move into asset-backed finance

Tokenization platform Securitize climbed about 10% postmarket after reporting third quarter growth alongside increased operating expenses, and announcing an expansion into asset-backed finance through a strategic acquisition.

Miami-based Securitize, which made its public market debut in July, operates in the relatively new corner of the crypto equity universe involved in institutional tokenization infrastructure. Tokenized assets on the platform grew 9% in the third quarter, to $4.8 billion, from the same period a year ago. Aggregate transaction volume rose 20% from a year ago, to $4.3 billion. Revenue of $11.5 million was up 28% from last year.

The growth came as bullish sentiment around tokenization gained traction throughout the third quarter, ahead of the Security and Exchange Commission formalizing the long awaited Innovation Exemption on Sept. 17, which created a temporary pathway for trading of certain tokenized stocks and strengthened the regulatory outlook.

Profitability is still meaningfully below year-ago levels, due in part to costs associated with going public over the summer, as well as the scaling up of the tokenization business at an active time for the entire sector, Securitize CEO Carlos Domingo told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

Securitize reinforced "certain areas of the company like finance, compliance, legal [and] cybersecurity," which increased corporate costs "compared to a year ago when we were privately held," Domingo said. "We're in in a very good position. The adjusted EBITDA … is improving significantly from previous quarters."

Securitize reported an adjusted EBITDA loss of $2 million, compared with positive adjusted EBITDA of $3.6 million a year ago, better than a $5.5 million loss in the second quarter. The company posted a 183% increase in operating costs and expenses.

Also on Monday, the Securitize board authorized a $50 million share buyback and announced the acquisition of River One, an institutional asset-backed financing platform developing technology to connect stablecoin capital with traditional lending markets. Terms were not disclosed.

Securitize sees an opportunity to modernize the $9 trillion market for asset backed finance by using stablecoins to finance real world assets, generate yield and tokenize investment positions and credit products, Domingo said.The acquisition also marks an expansion beyond Securitize's core niche markets at a time when tokenization's competitive landscape is growing, with traditional asset managers like BlackRock and Franklin Templeton, and established financial market infrastructure providers like DTCC and Broadridge, entering the industry.

"We have been very active in tokenized funds, treasuries [and] yield … And we've been obviously recently very active in tokenized stocks, so this adds another piece of the puzzle in the emerging tokenization space for us to capture revenue growth."

#2948. Federal Reserve announces College Fed Challenge winners

An official website of the United States Government

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 4:15 p.m. EST Share

The University of California, Los Angeles won the 23rd Annual National College Fed Challenge.

The University of California, Los Angeles (UCLA) won the 23rd annual national College Fed Challenge on Friday, a team competition that encourages undergraduate students to learn about the U.S. economy, monetary policymaking, and the role of the Federal Reserve System. Teams analyze economic and financial conditions and formulate a monetary policy recommendation, modeling the Federal Open Market Committee. The team, from Los Angeles, California, represented the San Francisco District and included Aarushi Maheshwari, Annika Chan, Atul Prasanth, Caitlyn Cox, Finn Karish, Eli Brooks (alternate), Kennedy Yang (alternate), Manasi Vartak (alternate), Ronojoy Borpujari (alternate), and Sahil Sudani (alternate). The team's advisers were Chris Surro and Ali Haider Ismail.

"I am very happy to congratulate the students who competed in this year's College Fed Challenge, especially the winners and finalists," said Federal Reserve Chairman Kevin Warsh. "They took on the same big questions we policymakers face. I'm impressed by their talent, their rigor, and their eagerness to think hard about monetary policy and the economy."

This year's College Fed Challenge included 141 schools from 34 states across the nation, the most in the competition's history. Teams submitted video presentations or participated in local competitions last month. Twenty-one semi-finalist teams then participated in question-and-answer sessions held earlier this month, and seven finalists were invited to the Federal Reserve Board for the national finals. The other national finalists were the University of Chicago in second place and the University of Maryland, College Park in third place. Teams with honorable mentions include Cleveland State University, Harvard College, Pennsylvania State University, and St. Lawrence University.

Teams were evaluated on their economic analysis, responses to the judges' questions, teamwork, and presentations.

#2947. Raiders Mailbag: How will Fernando Mendoza look in 2nd career start against Denver Broncos’ defense?

Fernando Mendoza had an up-and-down performance in his first career start against the Seattle Seahawks last week, and his second start comes against the Denver Broncos' defense, which led the NFL in sacks during the 2024 and 2025 campaigns. So, there's a lot of talk about the Las Vegas Raiders' Week 11 matchup and the rookie quarterback in this week's mailbag. Let's get to it!

A: The Broncos' defense definitely isn't as dominant as we've seen over the last two years, ranking 27th in yards per game allowed (355.0) and 13th in EPA per play (-0.05) allowed, per Sumer Sports. Granted, the latter is just outside the top third of the league, but that's below the standard Denver has set recently. So, I think Mendoza can have more success and should have an easier time moving the ball this Sunday compared to last week.

The biggest area he needs to improve on is handling pressure. Broncos defensive coordinator Vance Joseph is different from Seahawks head coach/defensive play caller Mike Macdonald, in that Macdonald leans more on creating confusion through varying pre-snap looks and simulated pressures (showing blitz but rushing four).

Joseph will line up and play more, letting Denver's excellent pass-rushers pin their ears back and do their jobs. But he isn't afraid to bring the heat either, ranking 11th with a 33.7 percent blitz rate, according to Sumer. Especially after watching last week, I'd expect Joseph to dive deep into the playbook and continue to throw the kitchen sink at the rookie quarterback making his second career start.

So, it's going to be important for Mendoza to stay poised under pressure and be faster at finding his answers/check downs. I have confidence that he'll be able to make an adjustment; poise and toughness in the pocket were two of his biggest strengths last year at Indiana. However, of course, it's a whole different ball game at this level, and he needs to prove it on the field.

The good news is that it should be all downhill from here as far as facing strong defenses with very good pass-rushers for the rest of the season. Well, until the Raiders play the Broncos again in a few weeks, LOL!

A: I'm definitely on board with you that Klint Kubiak has to reverse script and use the passing game to set up the rushing attack. Also, I think you're onto something about that being the strategy last week, where the first two plays of the game were play-action bootlegs. The next four plays were runs (one three-yarder and three four-yarders) to keep the offense on schedule, and then another play-action bootleg.

As you mentioned, Brock Bowers going down changed the strategy, or at least the effectiveness of it. Also, Macdonald started giving Mendoza the confusing pressure looks toward the end of the second quarter, making the problem even worse.

It sounds like Bowers will at least be out there on Sunday (he's currently listed as questionable) to make the Broncos account for him, so I'd expect Kubiak to use a similar strategy. Hopefully, the 'football robot from heaven' is healthy enough to be effective and can stay on the field to give us a full game to see how using the pass to set up the run works with Mendoza behind center.

Q: When you think about QB skill sets and what can or can't be developed effectively, where does pocket presence fall? Not going to lie, Mendoza's pocket presence troubles me a bit going back to Indiana.

A: Mendoza's feel for pressure was/is definitely one of my biggest concerns about his transition to the NFL. Where it can get complicated is that it's tied to his fearlessness under pressure. He's tough as hell and has no problem standing tall and making throws while taking a hit in the pocket. But the downside is that he will stay in the pocket too long and take sacks, some of those being big hits from free rushers. I flinched on both of Devon Witherspoon's sacks last week. LOL!

So, it's one of those things where Fernando is going to have to find the balance between one of his biggest strengths and one of his biggest weaknesses. You don't want him to become timid and turn into a 'Checkdown Charlie'. However, six sacks a game will lead to a short career.

Ultimately, the only way to find the balance is to get live reps in games since the defense isn't hitting the quarterback in practice. Jordan Love had a similar problem when he first became the Packers' starter, taking 29 sacks with a 15.2 percent pressure-to-sack ratio, per Pro Football Focus. In year two, Love's figures went down to 14 and 8.9 percent, and then 21 and 10.9 percent last season.

Granted, this season he's back up to 25 and 15.8 percent, but 12 sacks came in two games against the Vikings. That's just par for the course against Brian Flores, especially with how that defense has been playing this year. I'd be willing to bet Love's pressure-to-sack percentage comes down by the end of the year.

In other words, it's something that can be improved. Mendoza just has to get more reps and make an adjustment to have a better feel for pressure. Personally, I think it's easier to coach a quarterback to get the ball out faster than it is to force a scared quarterback to be braver in the pocket. One is a timing issue, and the other requires a rewiring of the brain, so to speak.

Quick break to let you guys know about a new release from Homage. Follow this link to get your Raiders Blackout Starter Jacket!

HOMAGE x Starter Raiders Blackout Breakaway Pullover Jacket

Q: It might be way too early for this question: Xavier Gilliam, Zereoue Williams, Jackson Harris, and Will Echoles are there for a first-round pick. What's your choice? The team needs WR and IDL talent badly, but may also need to look at a future replacement for Kolton Miller soon.

A: You're scratching the surface on an offseason topic that will likely be heavily talked about: Is it time to move on from Miller?

I wouldn't say he's been bad by any means, but the 31-year-old has certainly regressed this season to the point where finding a long-term blind-side protector will be on the table. So, someone like Williams or Jordan Seaton (if the Raiders are picking high enough) could be an option in the first round. However, right now, I'm willing to give Miller the rest of the campaign before closing the book on him and creating a new need in Las Vegas' trenches.

On that note, the Raiders need to get better on both sides of the trenches before worrying about adding a wide receiver, in my opinion. The only caveat to that would be if they're in range for Jeremiah Smith (highly unlikely) or Charlie Becker (fairly unlikely). Outside of those two, there isn't a receiver in this class who stands out enough to push me away from a lineman. I think there should be good/better value at wide receiver in the second round this year.

So, it comes down to Gilliam or Echoles. To be honest, I'd be pretty happy with either, but I lean toward Echoles. Gilliam has undoubtedly been the better player this year and has the potential to be an impact player against the run and as an interior pass-rusher. However, he is a one-year wonder, so Echoles has the bigger body of work, and I trust him against the run more.

The Ole Miss product definitely has to be more consistent as a pass-rusher, but the flashes are there to show some potential to grow in that department. But, again, I'd be pretty happy if the Raiders managed to land either of those defensive tackles.

Q: What is the difference between this year's Ashton Jeanty and last year's Ashton Jeanty?

A: To me, Jeanty doesn't have the same lateral movement skills and shiftiness that he showed as a rookie. Granted, those were never his biggest strengths, but I feel like his jump cuts and ability to make defenders miss have regressed this season. The straight-line speed and burst are still there, as seen in last week's 38-yard catch. However, Pro Football Focus had him with 61 missed tackles forced in 17 games last year (about 3.6 a game), but that's down to 11 MTFs in 10 games (1.1 per game) in 2026.

I think another good example is that Jeanty's yards per carry on outside zone runs has dipped from 4.0 (109 carries for 438 yards) to 3.2 ypc (29 carries for 92 yards), per Sumer. That's where being able to cut and move laterally really comes into play, and his effectiveness/efficiency has taken a hit this season. Maybe that's tied to the late-training camp ankle injury and it never fully healed, but running backs are never going to be fully healthy in the NFL. So, this is something to monitor over the next couple of months.

Q: What do you think is the main problem with the running game?

A: It's still the offensive line and, more specifically, the guard spots. Trey Zuhn III has been solid and looks like a good fit in Klint Kubiak's wide zone scheme. However, he only has 66 run-blocking snaps in the three games since becoming a starter, per PFF, so it's still too small of a sample size to definitively say the rookie can be a long-term option.

Meanwhile, I'm going to be pretty upset if Spencer Burford gets re-signed in the offseason. Take this for what it's worth; PFF has Miller (80.5), Tyler Linderbaum (77.7), DJ Glaze (72.6) and Zuhn (70.8) graded in the 70s or higher as zone run blockers, and Burford is sitting at 58.9. That's not the end-all-be-all and, again, it's a small sample size from Zuhn. But it's pretty telling to me that the same grading/evaluation system has four out of five linemen executing Kubiak's system at a high level and one guy who isn't.

Looking at the big picture, something that worries me is that the Raiders might have a zone-blocking offensive line and gap-running backs. I think Jeanty can execute zone runs and was decently effective at executing them last year, all things considered. However, that hasn't been the case in 2026, and Mike Washington Jr. doesn't have the vision and agility to be a consistent zone runner, in my opinion.

Kubiak's offense can still work where Washington is the change-of-pace back, but the team is going to need at least one back who can execute zone runs at a high level. Hopefully, that's Jeanty, and we'll see how this plays out down the stretch, but this could be another storyline heading into the offseason.

A: I think I've gotten this question (or some variation of it) in each of the last three weeks, and it's completely justified! To be honest, I don't think there is a "valid" reason, other than the coaching staff — Kubiak, more specifically — is attached to Burford and thinks he's playing better than what everyone else perceives. Meanwhile, it feels like they've soured on Jackson Powers-Johnson. It was pretty telling that when Zuhn got a concussion in San Francisco, Caleb Rogers was the one to replace him.

To be fair, JPJ is a questionable fit in a zone-running scheme. I thought he could make it work because he showed impressive athleticism at Oregon despite how big he is, but that just hasn't been the case this season. For perspective, he's the only lineman who has a worse PFF run-blocking grade on zone runs (50.5) than Burford does.

I'm with ya in that I think Powers-Johnson should get a shot at left guard; I just don't think we'll see it happen. Hopefully, I'm wrong, and something changes in the next few weeks.

A: I'll start my answer here with a little teaser from my 'Key Matchups' column:

"In Weeks 1-5, [Malcolm Koonce] was tied for 21st among edges with 16 pressures and sixth with a 19.4 percent win rate. In the five games since then, he's tied for 64th in pressures (eight) and tied for 65th in win rate (10.0 percent). Clearly, the Raiders need the complementary rusher who got off to a strong start to show up on Sunday and not the guy they've had over the last month."

At some point, a change on the depth chart has to happen if Koonce doesn't start picking it up. Seeing as Kwity Paye is an even less effective pass-rusher, the next man up should be Slone. The problem is the coaching staff has already used the undrafted rookie's three free practice squad call-ups, so elevating him means waiving someone else, barring an injury.

When will that happen? Your guess is as good as mine, but Koonce's clock should be ticking. I could see a situation where, if he doesn't start playing better, the Raiders waive the veteran during the bye week or shortly thereafter and give Slone a few games to show what he can do. Koonce is on a one-year contract and, as it currently stands, I don't think it would make sense to bring him back in the offseason. Then again, I said the same thing last year…

A: I could see that being another post-bye adjustment, especially since Jermod McCoy has been getting more playing time recently. I thought he played pretty well in garbage time against the 49ers, coming up with a couple of pass breakups and making a play or two against the run. If you're interested, I did a Tape Don't Lie breakdown on his performance (starts around the 42-minute mark).

The problem with McCoy right now is he's a very different corner in zone versus man coverage. To me, it looks like he's thinking too much in zone, while the natural skills you mentioned shine through in man. But it's not like the other corners are getting the job done right now. Might as well give the most talented one of the bunch a shot down the stretch.

Q: I've seen an uptick in criticism of GM Jon Spytek regarding his failures in both the draft and free agency during his tenure. Jack Bech in round 2 and the Kwity Paye contract are borderline criminal, IMO. Do you believe year 3 for him is make it or break it?

A: I dove into this more in depth a few weeks ago, and my opinion hasn't changed since then. So if you're looking for a more detailed answer, click on this hyperlink.

At a high level, I definitely think the pressure is going to be on Spytek this offseason/next season. I'm not in favor of moving on from him at the end of the year, but I'm also not going to sit here and say that the returns he's gotten on his investments so far are acceptable.

Spytek definitely leans toward taking gambles on draft prospects who are more projects than complete players, which can be frustrating because it takes patience as those players develop. However, that also means next year will be a make-or-break type of campaign for the 2025 draft class, and simultaneously, for the general manager who drafted those players.

A: Absolutely. When you're a head coach, you shoulder blame for any issue the team has. Kubiak may not be the primary reason for the defense's struggles, but ultimately, he's the one who hired Rob Leonard, and I think you're correct that Kubiak's idea was to replicate Seattle's defense without Seattle's personnel. That isn't to say he should be fired over Leonard, but it's on the head coach to make a change in one form or another to get better results.

Maybe that's moving on from Leonard and/or changing up the scheme, or getting with Spytek in the offseason to figure out what players they need to bring in to execute the system the way it's supposed to be run. Regardless, barring a dramatic turnaround over the next couple of months, Kubiak has to make an adjustment this spring.

One of the big differences between being a coordinator and a head coach is that you can no longer wash your hands clean when one side of the ball isn't playing up to par.

That'll do it for this week's mailbag. Thank you all for submitting questions and, as your weekly reminder, if you'd like to have your questions answered in a future column, tweet them at me, @MHolder95, email them to SBPQuestions1@gmail.com or look for our weekly call for questions on the site. The latter will continue to publish on Thursdays.

#2946. Lakers squeak by lowly Kings to get back on track

The Lakers continue to look like a team in need of the benefits that are supposed to come from this stretch in Southern California.

Because after looking like one of the NBA's best teams for 3 ½ weeks regardless of the competition, they've struggled against some of the league's bottom-feeders during their four-game homestand.

Luka Dončić shoots a 3-point basket against the Sacramento Kings. NBAE via Getty Images

That was once again the case on Friday night, with the Lakers barely beating the lowly one-win Kings, 106-104, at Crypto.com Arena to get back on track after losing to the Jazz on Wednesday.

After Walker Kessler missed a pair of free throws with the Lakers up by two points with five seconds left in the fourth, Zach LaVine grabbed the defensive rebound and dribbled up the court before firing a 29-footer that hit the front rim to secure the victory for the Lakers.

Cameron Carr drives to the basket. NBAE via Getty Images

But until the final buzzer sounded, there was an unease about whether the Lakers were going to lose back-to-back games for the first time.

They trailed 62-54 at halftime, with a general malaise from Tuesday's overtime win over the Hornets and Wednesday's loss to the Jazz carrying over into Friday.

It showed defensively, with the Lakers allowing too many drives to the rim.

It showed with the seven first-quarter turnovers they committed, which directly led to 11 Kings points.

The Lakers continue to look like a team in need of the benefits that are supposed to come from this stretch in Southern California. NBAE via Getty Images

But the Lakers, led by Luka Dončić (25 points, eight assists, four rebounds and two steals), found enough of the groove they had been searching for in the second half to turn what was an 11-point deficit in the third into an eight-point lead in the fourth.

And they held on just long enough to ensure they entered the weekend with a 14-3 record before a two-day break before hosting the Jazz again on Monday.

#2943. Eagles News: Makai Lemon or Max Iheanachor?

Welcome to The Linc, the daily Philadelphia Eagles links roundup at Bleeding Green Nation!

Do you feel like the Eagles should've picked Max Iheanachor in their trade up to No. 20? Or do you stand by the team's decision to go with Makai Lemon? Lemon was clearly the higher-rated pick in terms of the consensus draft board, which the Eagles have leaned into in recent years. Lemon has been OK for the Eagles thus far, though it's not easy to get a great evaluation of him in this current lackluster situation. That being said, Iheanachor plays a very valuable position and his upside is intriguing. He was my pick for the Eagles in my final mock draft. Which player would you rather have on the Birds right now?

Let's get to the Philadelphia Eagles news and links …

An alternate reality where Max Iheanachor is on the Eagles, and everything else to know about Eagles-Steelers – Inquirer

With the Eagles at 2-7 and preparing to face the Pittsburgh Steelers on Sunday, it's a perfect time to hop into the Cold Tub Time Machine — I just made that up, I think — and head back to April 23. What if Makai Lemon didn't slide down the draft board? What if the Eagles didn't move ahead of the Steelers — who literally were calling Lemon — to select him? What would the Eagles have done with their pick? It's an interesting thought experiment, given what the Steelers did after realizing they couldn't draft Lemon. They took offensive tackle Max Iheanachor, who visited the Eagles and plays a position they needed, one that came more into focus four weeks into the season when Lane Johnson retired. But here are some numbers to chew on as Markel Bell, the team's third-round pick, prepares to make another start at right tackle. Iheanachor's pressure rate allowed is 7.83%, while Bell's is 7.14%, according to Pro Football Focus. Even Monroe Freeling, who was mocked to the Eagles by some and was drafted 19th by Carolina, is having a rough start to his NFL career with a pressure rate of 8.47%. Only Blake Miller (6.23%), picked 17th, is having a better season in pass protection than Bell among the tackles picked in the area of where the Eagles did. Caleb Lomu, who is a backup in New England, went seven picks after Iheanachor at No. 28, and then no tackle was selected until the Eagles drafted Bell 40 picks later. Bell's first season hasn't been perfect, and the circumstances haven't been ideal, but he's playing just as well as his peers drafted two rounds earlier. How would we all be viewing Iheanachor if he was the first-round pick? How would the Eagles? Would they still be entering the offseason wondering if they had the right tackle of the future in the building?

One interesting subplot in this game will be the RTs. Max Iheanachor and Markel Bell are both rookies and now starting. Had the Eagles not traded up, there is a good chance they would have taken Iheanachor. Instead the Eagles moved up for Makai Lemon and took Bell in the third round. Bell and Iheanachor are having similar rookie seasons. Who will play better on Sunday? Bell has to go up against TJ Watt so that could make for a long day. Pittsburgh wants to move closer to a playoff berth. The Eagles just want to win a game. Eagles players and coaches say they haven't given up on the season. They need to prove that with a good showing on Sunday.

Eagles-Steelers questions: Reflecting on the Makai Lemon pick, previewing 2027 NFL Draft – The Athletic

Berman: The context, of course, is that the Steelers were on the phone with Lemon when the Eagles traded up to acquire the wide receiver one spot ahead of them. Pittsburgh settled for Iheanachor. The Eagles would be better with a promising option at offensive tackle than at slot receiver, although it depends on their evaluation of Iheanachor and whether he would have been a markedly better long-term option than Markel Bell in the third round. Before the draft, I thought the Eagles should address the offensive line in the first round if the board allowed. They hadn't addressed the position in the first two rounds since 2022. I also thought they could get in position for Kadyn Proctor and/or Monroe Freeling, but it did not happen. Iheanachor was in a different tier than those tackles. I'd still lean toward building through the line of scrimmage, and a combination of Iheanachor and perhaps Williams or Chris Bell in Round 3 would be better than Lemon and Markel Bell. Given how Lemon has been used, I wonder if that's a role better addressed in a middle round, but it's too soon to determine that. So I'd say yes on the positions, TBD on the players.

What will make the 2026 Eagles season a successful one following the bye week? – BGN

The 2026 Philadelphia Eagles season won't be resulting in a Super Bowl championship. The playoff streak is also likely ending at five seasons. This season is a lost cause in terms of expectations from the summer. That doesn't mean the 2026 season can't be deemed a success. Bear with me here. Since the Eagles are 2-7 and aren't expected to make the playoffs this year, there are some factors remaining in this season that can carry over into 2027. The wins and losses aren't as important (well maybe the losses), but some aspects from 2026 can play into how the 2027 Eagles shape out. So what can make the remainder of this Eagles season a success? There are a few things to hope for in this lost season.

Lew The Coop: How Will Jalen Carter Handle The Return Of Ex-Eagle Lew Nichols? – PHLY Sports

Lew Nichols, still in the NFL years after he was initially named a Bo Wulf Camp Crush, comes to the LINC this weekend armed with vengeance in his heart against Howie Roseman, Nick Sirianni, Jake Elliott and the 2-7 Eagles. What does Deniz Selman make of this being the conceit of a show in November? Will Brandon Lee Gowton simp for Darius Cooper? Tune in to find out!

Bowen: Nick Sirianni stumbles upon a truth about the Eagles' offense under Sean Mannion – PHLY Eagles

"I think one thing I always value about him is, anybody can come and give a play and say, 'Hey, I want to do this, or I want to do that.' I think he just has a really good monotonous approach to figuring out everything that could potentially happen in that situation, what the downfalls are, what the upside is and whether we move forward with some of those things. I think that just speaks to the (attention to) detail that he has." I kept reading this quote over and over. "Monotonous approach"? I mean, it kinda fits. Sheil Kapadia noted after the Eagles lost to the Giants that the Birds have gone three and out on 43 percent of their offensive possessions, the highest rate for any team in the last three seasons, also highest percentage for any Eagles team in the 21st century. Pretty monotonous. But given that Sirianni did not fire Mannion or take away play-calling during the bye week, as had been widely anticipated, I don't think Nick was insulting Mannion. After thinking it over, I'm pretty sure Sirianni meant meticulous approach, which is a little different. But the malapropism sums up so much. For me, anyway.

Steelers EDGE Nick Herbig OUT vs. Eagles – Behind The Steel Curtain

The Pittsburgh Steelers have downgraded edge rusher Nick Herbig to out for Sunday's Week 11 game against the Philadelphia Eagles, per Adam Schefter. Herbig has been dealing with a rib injury and didn't practice at all throughout the week. This will likely give more opportunities to Jack Sawyer. Herbig signed a four-year, $100 million extension this offseason, keeping him in Pittsburgh for the long-term future. He has three sacks so far in the 2026 season, as well as seven tackles for loss and a forced fumble.

Edge Jonathan Greenard loves to makes plays, despite Eagles' standing – ESPN

It would be a nice story arc if Greenard were at full health and ready to play without limitation coming off the team's bye, but that's simply not the reality of his season. The left ankle injury he suffered while pressuring Matthew Stafford in Week 4 against the Los Angeles Rams lingers. It swelled up on him during a flight to Georgia last week as he returned home to take care of some things at his property, he said, and remains a limiting factor on the field. The physical demands of the NFL simply do not allow the healing time required to get to 100%. But there has been improvement, something that Greenard sensed against New York whether it was setting the edge against the run or effectively executing an inside pass rush. "Whatever move feels good we're going to find a way to get to that move and be physical, be myself as much as I can. I think slowly in that game I started getting that confidence back of, 'OK, I can do certain things with this move that I used to be able to do,'" he said. "I can finally run to the ball. With this foot I was only able to strike and make a tackle. I wasn't able to run for real in past times I've been in the game. But this past game it was like I can actually run more comfortable. It still bothers [me] but I can kind of do a lot more."

Roob's Observations: Why on Earth is Johnny Mundt playing more than Eli Stowers? – NBCSP

Someone has to explain to me why Johnny Mundt is playing more than Eli Stowers. Mundt is a 10-year veteran who has four catches for 16 yards this year and has not been close to the blocker we all expected when the Eagles signed him. For literally zero value, Mundt has played a ridiculous 220 snaps, or 24 ½ per game. Why? He brings nothing to the table. Then there's Stowers, a promising rookie who is finally healthy after dealing with a lingering hamstring injury. The Eagles invested a 2nd-round pick on Stowers, yet he's buried on the bench. In the three games since he came back healthy, Mundt has played 37 snaps and Stowers has played 32. There is absolutely no reason on Earth a 31-year-old journeyman with no receiving ability and minimal blocking prowess should be playing more than a 23-year-old rookie 2nd-round pick. None. Even if Mundt was good, the Eagles are 2-7 and this is a time to see what the young guys can do and get them as many reps as possible. Stowers got a few targets late in the Washington game and caught three passes for 53 yards, including a 34-yarder. He had more yards on that one 34-yard catch than Mundt has all year. And do you know when Mundt's last 34-yard catch was? It was in 2020. Six years and four teams ago. You're 2-7. What are we doing here? Play the young guys. Especially ones who have shown some promise. I don't know if Stowers will ever be a legit NFL tight end. But I do know we're not going to find out if he's not playing.

Fantasy football start/sit advice for Week 11 at QB, RB, WR, TE – SB Nation

SIT — DK Metcalf, Steelers at Eagles. The Eagles Quinyon Mitchell has played well this season and could follow Metcalf around. If he doesn't, Metcalf will see plenty of him. I expect this game will be a slog, as it has the lowest total of the week, the Eagles don't have a starting caliber QB and neither do the Steelers. And, Metcalf has been bad this year. Multiple drops and an old Aaron Rodgers throwing to him have led to him catching just 50% of his targets. On paper this matchup isn't bad, but this is a game I'd like to stay far away from this week.

SEPTA adding Broad Street Line service for Eagles-Steelers game amid Philadelphia Marathon road closures – FOX29

SEPTA is adding extra subway service to South Philadelphia on Sunday, Nov. 22, as fans head to Lincoln Financial Field for the Eagles' matchup against the Pittsburgh Steelers on the same day as the Philadelphia Marathon. #2939. Game Recap: Suns survive late Jazz rally to secure 111-101 NBA Cup victory

Man, this game was an emotional rollercoaster, especially late, but the Suns pulled this one out in a big way. For a team that started 1-5, they have turned things around quickly, winning three straight and only losing two in their last nine games. This is a good trend for a team still figuring things out on the fly with their starting lineup. It's another big win, as this was an NBA Emirates Cup game, which helped them bounce back after their first loss in Group A to the Denver Nuggets.

This game started hot for Phoenix, who took a commanding lead in the first half and held on to it through the third as a lot of free throws were made. In the fourth, the Jazz tried to climb back, cutting the deficit to four, but it was too little, too late as the Suns took care of business on the road. Even though the Suns allowed 17 OREB, their 19 points on 15 turnovers helped them early and led to this deserved win.

Devin Booker led Phoenix with 23 points, two rebounds, and three assists, while Miles Bridges and Dillon Brooks each had 18. Bench mob beasts Collin Gillespie and Jordan Goodwin sealed the win: Gillespie added 16 points, 4 rebounds, and 5 assists, while Goodwin grabbed 9 rebounds and 4 assists, doing much of the dirty work alongside Bridges.

For the Jazz, Darryn Peterson had 20 points, ten rebounds, and eight assists, which was close to a triple-double, while Jaxson Hayes caused havoc on the glass. He finished with 18 points and 14 rebounds, a big reason Utah stayed in this one. Brice Sensabaugh also got hot late, adding 18 of his own points with five threes to keep the Jazz thriving from behind the arc.

For the Phoenix Suns, coming out of the gate hot was needed to get to a three-game win streak. For the Suns, though, before they could score a basket, they had to win their first challenge call that night because of the refs. The Suns starters did look solid in their first stint, as they got Dillon Brooks going early, with Keyonte George guarding him for an easy four points. Phoenix got off to an early run, but the Jazz matched it and tied it up at 12.

Sadly for the Suns, they would have to use their second challenge just seven minutes into the game when Booker was hit across the face by a driving Ace Bailey. Even though Phoenix won both challenges, it was tough to lose them with 40 minutes of game time left. This allowed the bench to come in and resume where they left off. Jordan Goodwin and Collin Gillespie made it known when they got out there, with Gillespie making the first three of the night for both teams.

With Khaman Maluach and Ryan Dunn checking in, the Suns seemed more aggressive on the boards and limited the Jazz's second-chance points. The Suns gave up 12 OREB in the first half, and that cannot continue into the second half. With the Jazz finding some threes late, they brought the game closer to the end of the first quarter, but Booker made the last shot count, hitting a buzzer-beater off a full-court pass from Miles Bridges. The Suns led 26-23 at the end of the first quarter.

Heading into the second, we saw the bench continue to prove they are the best in the league, with Gillespie making some tough buckets and Luke Kennard getting an easy five points on two quick shots. Unfortunately, the trouble on the glass is an issue, as Jaxson Hayes is dominating and getting multiple tip-ins and one-and-one opportunities.

Though this was the case, Brooks and Bridges provided the Suns with the scoring to take their largest lead so far, 43-30, with a strong run and Bridges getting comfortable. Sadly, the Suns saw an injury when Ryan Dunn caught a knee to the thigh and went to the locker room. He provided solid defense and rebounding, allowing the offense to flow better with ball movement.

The Jazz, though, didn't want to go out quiet as Darryn Peterson started showing off his skills on both ends. Bailey secured a tip-in at the end, but the Suns saw some nice moves from Malauch in his second stint. The big caught a nice block and made some easy passes for putbacks and points. The Suns finished the first half up 61-49. Brooks and Gillepsie lead the way for the Suns with eleven points each, while Bridges. Booker and Maluach have eight. It's truly been a team game offensively, with the ball being shared, especially in the second unit. For the Jazz, Peterson has 12 points, while Hayes has 11, and Sensabaugh has 3 made threes. To win this game, the Suns have to win the rebounding battle and control the possession.

To kick off the second half, the Suns found out that Ryan Dunn would not return, leaving his minutes in limbo. That is where Koa Peat got some burn and, unfortunately, didn't get too much of it, as he picked up some early fouls. The Suns did a better job on the glass, limiting the Jazz's second-chance points, but the Jazz were still getting to the charity stripe.

This is where the game took a toll on me, as it dragged on with both teams finding opportunities to score only at the free-throw line. Brooks and Bridges, though, continued to show their offensive flow, with Bridges driving and delivering some nice paint scoring alongside some boards. The Suns got the lead back to 12, and Booker found a rhythm of his own, scoring 10 points in the quarter to bring his total to 18.

The thirteen Jazz turnovers really cost them in this game, and with the Suns doing a better job of limiting them on the glass, the Suns looked on track for an easy win if they closed out the fourth. Although this is the Phoenix Suns we are talking about, they always make your heart skip a beat, even when they play the worst teams.

The fourth quarter opened with Brice Sensabaugh getting red hot and quickly scoring four points. The Suns answered with tough shots of their own from Gillespie, but the Jazz weren't bowing out of this one. With more rim pressure from Hayes, the Jazz got back on the glass and caused some disruptions. Peterson also showed off his quickness to get to the cup and now had a double-double with some time left.

Bridges once again helped this team, alongside Booker, keep them afloat with some nice baskets. Brooks picked up his fifth, then eventually his sixth foul, and was fouled out of the game, which was not a good look as the Jazz rallied back. With more made threes, the Suns, who had a 16-point lead at one point, were down to just four with under a minute left.

Luckily for this team, Bridges was on tonight and made multiple nice inbound passes to Booker to get a guaranteed bucket, and here was another example of that. Booker then went on to get another three points by hunting his shots, and the Suns pulled off a 111-101 win. This is now the team's longest win streak this season, at three, and they just secured their first NBA Cup win this year. Overall, it wasn't the best win on paper, but it was solid nonetheless.

The Suns continue their road trip, heading to New Orleans to take on the Pelicans.

#2937. Buffalo Bills DT Ed Oliver questionable for Week 11 vs. Miami Dolphins

The injuries continue piling up for the Buffalo Bills ahead of their Week 11 home game against the Miami Dolphins. A surprise report originating from the team on Saturday afternoon revealed that Bills starting defensive tackle Ed Oliver is dealing with an oblique injury.

Oliver is now listed as questionable for Sunday's home game, though the news is not all doom and gloom. Matt Parrino provided some context to Oliver's injury, tweeting that "(p)er a league source, Ed Oliver notified Bills training staff this morning that he felt pain in his oblique. The staff checked it out and there's optimism that he'll play against the Dolphins."

Per NFL rules, Oliver's ailment must be accounted for on the injury report even at this late stage, which is why he's being listed as questionable. In the past, Oliver's situation would have likely had him tabbed as "probable" for game day. The NFL has since done away with that injury designation, which has seen the questionable term pick up the slack in filing injuries throughout the league.

Should Oliver be unable to go (or limited in some fashion) it would be a huge blow to Buffalo's defense, which already has to account for injuries to starters in cornerback Christian Benford and inside linebacker Dorian Williams. Both Bills defenders have been ruled out ahead of Week 11.
