#2931. NFL predictions: 3 bold takes on the Cowboys vs Titans game

The Dallas Cowboys got close to upsetting the San Francisco 49ers late in the game last week, but were unable to close the deal. That has led to chaos among the defensive coaching ranks.

The Cowboys are at home for this week's game against the 1-8 Tennessee Titans, and are 7.5-point favorites according to our friends at FanDuel. The over/under is 54.5 points.

Let's get into three bold predictions for the Titans game.

Tony Pollard returns to his old home as one of the main cogs in the Titans offense. Pollard has 628 yards rushing this year with six rushing touchdowns, along with 136 yards receiving an an additional touchdown.

Against Dallas, who has trouble containing running backs, especially dual-threat ones, Pollard will have a day. He will amass over 100 yards from scrimmage and will score twice.

This is more hope than anything else. The Cowboys defense needs something good to happen, and this week the Titans will oblige. While struggling with Pollard, they will frustrate Cam Ward and the passing attack with pressure and tight coverage on the Titans' short passing game. Things will go so well that they will actually score a touchdown on defense.

Last week, it was George Pickens who kept the Cowboys in the game against the 49ers. CeeDee Lamb came on late to help out, but spent much of the game on the wrong page with Dak Prescott. That gets smoothed out this week as Lamb goes off for over 150 yards receiving and a receiving touchdown.

#2929. Utah Jazz vs Phoenix Suns: Player Grades

By my money, this was the most disappointing loss of the year thus far. Riding high after two road wins, the team looked exhilarated for the first time all year and the home fans were excited to finally have something to latch onto. You could feel how badly the crowd wanted to root hard for their team, but instead, their team simply decided to not show up. The Jazz only lost by 10 points tonight, but if you could only analyze the game based off of body language and effort, you would guess that it was more than that. They didn't hustle after loose balls, they made no attempts to patch their poor decision making, and they looked defeated the whole night. This was a bad loss, and the grades will represent that – not a lot of positives you can take from this one.

Note: These grades are more-or-less relative to the player. An A grade for Darryn Peterson is different than an A grade for Tony Bradley.

It is a make or miss league, and Ace Bailey missed. Honestly, that isn't too surprising; it's been the status quo up to this point in the season. One of the most underrated skills in the league, fascinatingly enough, is actually making shots – a reputation alone doesn't help quite as much. To this point, Ace has a limit on his ceiling until his jump shots go in at a consistent rate, which has not been the case in his collegiate or NBA career. He will rarely go 0-7, but a better shooter will have nights like this even less frequently. Additionally, his defense has become less and less impressive as the year has progressed; tonight, he wasn't nearly as loud as he was at the beginning of the year. Without event creation on defense, self creation on offense, or the ability to hit open threes, Ace really isn't providing anything. Will Hardy recognized this, benching Ace for the closing stretch. This team's potential is very tightly knit to Ace's, and games like this just won't cut it.

After heaps of well-deserved praise after his recent stretch, Mo Bamba had a major regression-t0-the-mean game tonight. He was essentially invisible; the Suns were attacking him relentlessly and he was unable to use his size to make them pay. He looked comfortable taking threes…perhaps too comfortable; I think I prefer when he takes them with defenders sagging off completely, instead of in the face of solid closeouts. Ultimately, this harsh grade was given in the light of his recent games. These were the minutes I expected we'd get from Mo when the Jazz picked him up initially, but because he's shown me more, I've come to expect more. So, while I've loved the Bamba experience thus far, this game, hopefully, acted as a return to reality for those already championing Bamba as the Jazz's answer at the center position.

This was one of the worst offensive games that I can remember from Keyonte. The missed shots were one thing, but the lack of effort and body language were really discouraging. Keyonte has taken on the mantle of leader, but the responsibilities of that title are not relinquished during games where his threes are not going in. It's fun to be a leader when you beat the Lakers in L.A., but it's essential to be a leader when you are battling the Suns in a slog two nights later, and you are simply not doing that when you nonchalantly let opponents steal offensive rebounds from under your nose. I restrained myself from delivering an F due to some genuinely solid defensive contests, but I am placing the majority of this game's ugliness squarely on Keyonte's shoulders.

Josh Green is Josh Green. He can't dribble or pass, which is detrimental in lineups where he is playing alongside 2-3 other players who also can't dribble or pass. But he'll hit an open corner three or two, and he is easily our best defensive option against any star scorer, ranging from 6'4 to 6'8. Devin Booker ended with 23 points, but he wasn't particularly efficient, partially because Green understood that the reason he gets minutes is to make life difficult for the bucket-getter that he's lined up against. The final ATO was his worst coverage of the night, but that did not define his night – we did not lose this game because of Josh Green losing Devin Booker at the end of the game.

Very impressive night from Jaxson Hayes, where he reminded the fanbase that, until this season, he was a far more established player than Mo Bamba for a reason. One underrated skill of his is his timing on his rolls to the rim – he plays it at just the right pace where the ball-handler is able to hit him right in the most sensitive pocket of the defense, and tonight the Suns didn't really have an answer besides fouling. The missed free throws were frustrating, and I don't feel like Hayes ever plays as a 7 footer on defense (more of a shot blocker than a rim protector, and tonight he wasn't even blocking shots), but providing a consistent and efficient offensive outlet is something that shouldn't be undersold.

People aren't too quick to offer Svi praise these days, but he provided one of the better combinations of shooting and effort on the team tonight. I get it, I get it, his turnovers are frustrating, especially for a player that really shouldn't be dribbling, but tonight he understood that someone had to make something happen, which helps me give him a bit of a pass for his 3 turnovers tonight. At the end of the day, if Svi can make jump shots, he's a solid role player – let's hope that his recent efficiency continues on throughout the season.

Maybe I should've spent more attention on what Okogie was doing on defense, but it didn't pop off the screen tonight like it sometimes does. Okogie is somehow an even more simplistic player than Green – if he doesn't have a small, not-too-quick guard to pester, and the shot's not dropping, he really isn't providing too much to the team. He doesn't take much away – he'll shoot when open, and bring intensity at the very least – but he didn't contribute much of anything to the team tonight.

Interesting game for Peterson. He went 0-7 from three, which could make you think that I'd penalize him the same way I did Ace and Keyonte, but Peterson was able to get his fingerprints all over the game tonight in a way that his other prestigious youngster teammates could not. Some of his passes tonight were legitimately inspiring, and gave me hope that his long-term position might actually be point guard – his one hand dart to Green in the corner after a shifty drive was particularly impressive. Additionally, even though he struggled when matched up with a shifty point guard in Gillespie, I still think he gave some serious defensive flashes, at the very least on the defensive glass. Ultimately, I think much of a player's value is based on what they can provide to a team when their shot isn't falling, and tonight, Peterson provided a bit of everything while Ace and Keyonte did not. Some nights will be off nights, but that can be overcome with skills that are not dependent on the variance inherent to jump shots – Peterson seems to already have a strong understanding of that fact.

The defense wasn't awesome, and the decision making wasn't always great, but when he is making his shots, Brice provides so much value to this team because we simply do not have a surplus of players who can do what he does. He is a versatile shot-maker who can succeed even when faced with contests, and that cannot be said for a lot of the players competing for his minutes. Until we get some other player who can fill this niche on the Jazz bench, I can't get behind the prevailing sentiment, which is to call for his immediate dismissal from the consistently active Jazzmen. Brice brings an NBA skill that can completely alter a team's fortunes, which he did tonight – without his efficient shooting, I think the Jazz would've been dead in the water.

Who stood out for you? Which grades would you change? Sound off below!

#2928. What's ahead for AI financing after Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang departs

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's departure hasn't produced a broad shock to the credit market, but it is putting the company's role as a financier of artificial intelligence infrastructure under scrutiny.

Huang, who stepped down Thursday amid an espionage and adultery scandal, built Nvidia into an AI powerhouse in the 33 years since he co-founded the company. Chief financial officer Colette Kress has been named interim CEO as the company searches for a permanent chief executive.

Nvidia not only supplies its graphics processing units to hyperscalers and tech giants, it also provides financing to other companies to help the industry expand. That could all change.

"Although we expect the company to fulfill all of its previously announced commitments, we believe that going forward, it may be more difficult for the company to negotiate new multi-party investments among ecosystem partners at the same scale it has done up to this point," Goldman Sachs analyst James Schneider said in a note Thursday.

The news of Huang's resignation comes after investors were already concerned about the massive amount of debt being taken on to fund AI infrastructure.

Tech bond yields have risen and spreads have widened on credit default swaps, most notably in lower-rated names. Credit default swaps are contracts in which investors pay for protection in case the borrower can't repay its debt. Wider spreads indicate investors view the bonds as riskier.

On Friday, the yield on Nvidia's 4.5% bond due in 2031 sat at 5.717%, nearly 5% higher week to date. Bond yields move inversely to prices.

Tech stocks slumped Friday with the Nasdaq 100 closing down 1.2% as markets continued to digest the Nvidia news. Yields on hyperscaler bonds ticked slightly higher on Friday, signaling the broader concern about AI debt lingers.

It's the smaller AI companies that could ultimately see an impact if Nvidia makes any changes to its AI financing. The company had $36 billion in off-balance-sheet AI cloud agreements, or backstops, for neoclouds, according to its last quarterly financial filing in July.

Neoclouds CoreWeave, Nebius and IREN are all in Nvidia's portfolio. Their shares were down sharply Thursday.

CoreWeave, which has become the poster child for AI debt fears, saw its bond yields retreat after its quarterly earnings on Tuesday eased investor concerns. The yield on its 9.75% bond due in 2031 was 14.79% on Friday after climbing to 16.7% last week. The spread on its five-year CDS also dropped more than 17% week to date, although it remained above 1,000 — which is generally considered a sign of distress.

It remains unclear how Nvidia's support for those companies may change, said CreditSights analyst Andy Li.

"We don't know what the ultimate strategy for Nvidia is going to be coming out the other side," he said.

"However, I think directionally, just given the fact that Jensen was so crucial in selling the Nvidia story and being a very aggressive deal maker, it's likely that a lot of this creative deal-making and circular financing and equity and credit support is going to moderate," he added.

CoreWeave CEO Michael Intrator told CNBC Wednesday, before Huang's departure, that Nvidia "is not one person."

"There are thousands of people over there that interface with thousands of people at my company every day. They integrate on the technology. They integrate on the physical infrastructure. They integrate on the networking," he said in an interview with "Squawk on the Street." "They have been a great partner for us for years. They will continue to be a great partner for us."

Bob Lang, founder and chief strategist at Explosive Options, doesn't see any big changes in terms of Nvidia's relevance or its ability to finance for now. As CFO, Kress was involved in the financing deals, and the search for a permanent CEO is going to take some time, he noted.

The real issue is just how much money the company will want to lend for AI financing, rather than, say using its hundreds of billions in free cash flow for other purposes, like share buybacks, capex, or acquisitions.

"Big companies like Nvidia and Meta, they're holding all the cards," he said. "When they decide that the game is over, that's it — it's going to be curtains for some of these smaller companies."

— CNBC'S Kif Leswing and Isabel O'Brien contributed reporting.

#2924. Chiefs-Cardinals: 5 Things to Watch in Week 11 matchup

The (7-2) Kansas City Chiefs will host the (3-7) Arizona Cardinals this week in an interconference matchup between AFC West and NFC West teams. The game kicks off at noon at Arrowhead Stadium in Kansas City, Missouri, and will be broadcast on CBS.

With a 35-13 blowout win over the Atlanta Falcons, the Chiefs found themselves back in the driver's seat in the AFC West, taking a slight advantage over the Denver Broncos due to tiebreakers.

The Cardinals found themselves on the opposite end of the stick, losing 34-6 to the Los Angeles Rams to fall to dead last in their division.

The Chiefs are heavy favorites heading into this week and need a win, with much tougher matchups on the horizon.

Right tackle Jaylon Moore (ankle) and right guard Trey Smith (concussion) have been ruled out; Moore missed last week as well, and Smith went out in the first quarter.

Josh Ezeudu will likely start his third game at right tackle, while Mike Caliendo should make his third start at right guard.

Against the Falcons, the Chiefs' offensive line issues didn't matter, but they could limit how the offense calls plays.

Patrick Mahomes and the skill players overcame the injuries up front last week, but it will be interesting to see whether Arizona targets the right side this week when the Chiefs drop back to pass.

The Cardinals defense ranks 17th in the NFL in sacks this season with 17 total sacks. Cardinals defensive coordinator Nick Rallis was retained through the head coaching change from Jonathan Gannon to Mike LaFleur, and it will be interesting to see how he prepares to face the beat-up Chiefs offensive line.

This week, the Chiefs announced they signed veteran offensive tackle Fred Johnson to the team's practice squad. Johnson has 22 career starts, so it will be interesting to see whether the team shifts the line further if performance issues arise or more injuries occur.

Head coach Andy Reid commented on the line during his press conference on Friday.

"Well, we've got a variety there, but they're all going to have a shot to get in there and go, so we'll see how it all goes," Reid said. "But I think everybody has a lot of trust in Mike (Caliendo), if he's the one in there playing."

Despite a slow start to the season, the Chiefs' pass rush has delivered back-to-back solid performances, led by stars Chris Jones and George Karlaftis.

Karlaftis leads the team with 5.5 sacks this season and has at least one in the last three games. Jones has three this year, but has come alive with 2.5 through the past two games.

The slow start getting home hurt the team, but both have played their best football so far in November.

The defensive line has also seen the emergence of defensive end Ashton Gillotte, who has recorded at least one sack in the last two weeks, along with multiple pressures.

The Cardinals have allowed 28 sacks so far this season, tied for sixth-worst in the NFL, and will start multiple backup-caliber players on their offensive line on Sunday.

Rookie quarterback Carson Beck is unlikely to play, meaning journeyman backup Josh Johnson would start.

If the Chiefs pass rush can turn in another solid performance, they should be able to shut down the Cardinals offense.

General manager Brett Veach's latest in-season acquisition started off his tenure in Kansas City on a positive note despite playing limited snaps and receiving only five targets against Atlanta.

In only ten snaps, Josh Downs caught four passes for 53 yards and hauled in an impressive touchdown to end the first half.

In that limited action, he showed off his route-running ability and the sure hands needed in the Chiefs system. With a big game in the middle of next week against Buffalo, Downs likely won't see every snap out wide, but I expect to see him get around 25-30 as he gets more comfortable in the Chiefs system.

In his first start, rookie running back Emmett Johnson exploded for a massive game, compiling 170 total yards on the ground and through the air and scoring two touchdowns off passes from Mahomes.

With Kenneth Walker (groin) slated to miss his second consecutive game, Johnson will once again get the call to start.

With the touchdowns, Johnson took the lead for rookie touchdowns and has moved himself into the conversation for the Offensive Rookie of the Year award.

Johnson has impressed with a tremendous combination of vision and cutting ability that has helped him find running lanes and break tackles at a high rate on so few touches.

With 519 total scrimmage yards this season, Johnson has quickly become a difference-maker for the Chiefs offense, and even when Walker returns, Johnson should still see his role expand. I would expect to see Johnson in heavy doses on Sunday.

The Chiefs have once again found elite cornerback play in Mansoor Delane and Nohl Williams.

A former third-round pick, Williams has quickly become one of the NFL's best corners and has been among the league's most effective players in man coverage.

Opposite Williams, Delane has been equally impressive and is making a run at a potential defensive rookie-of-the-year campaign.

Critics questioned the decision to trade All-Pro corner Trent McDuffie in the offseason, but so far the trade has paid off.

Delane and Williams are both on rookie deals and appear headed toward bright careers. Against a stumbling offense and a journeyman quarterback, this week will give each player a chance to pad their stats and look to add to their interception totals.

#2923. Data center construction costs soar 13% in 2026

A version of this article first appeared in the CNBC Property Play newsletter with Diana Olick. Property Play covers new and evolving opportunities for the real estate investor, from individuals to venture capitalists, private equity funds, family offices, institutional investors and large public companies. Sign up to receive future editions, straight to your inbox.

The demand for large-scale data centers in the U.S. this year, driven by the need for computing power for artificial intelligence, grew well beyond the supply of powered land and the labor to build these structures. It also pushed timelines and risk for contractors.

All that resulted in costs rising 13% year-over-year to more than twice the global rate, according to a new report from Turner & Townsend, a global project management company. The gain this year was about three times the last ten-year average.

The report looked at over 500 live or recently completed projects in 62 global markets. It also included a survey of 400 experts in the global data center industry.

While there was some stabilization in cost inflation this year, it was not enough to offset the huge growth in not just demand but speed of construction and delivery.

"More aggressive schedules tie to contractors in particular and subcontractors baking in more risk allowances in their pricing," said Paul Barry, North America data center lead for Turner & Townsend. "So whether that's acceleration allowances, whether that's more overtime and labor attraction allowances, even just higher fees for taking on the risk of delivering at a faster pace than what they would traditionally take on."

All of those things, combined with the spike in demand and the strain on the supply chain, translate into higher costs, he added.

Capital expenditure on global data centers is projected to rise from about $800 billion this year to $1.8 trillion per year in 2050, according to a recent report from PwC. The U.S. is expected to capture almost half of all global investment in AI infrastructure, which includes both data centers and chips.

Power is, of course, key to all data centers, and 56% of those surveyed by Turner & Townsend globally said it was a major obstacle to delivery. Just over half said utility connections typically take between one and two years, and almost a third reported timeframes of over two years. Costs for power vary regionally — from $15.01 per watt in Silicon Valley to $10.51 per watt in Dallas, based on a typical 60-megawatt design.As with every type of construction in the U.S., labor continues to be a significant headwind. Just over one quarter of respondents said their contractor capacity, that is, the ability to manage construction backlogs, was sufficient.

Going forward, Barry said he expects costs to moderate somewhat, with cost increases in the 6% to 10% range in the next year. A lot of the major projects have already started or are starting now, so cost risk has been baked into those contracts.

"If this demand continues, then there'll be more continued flow of project starts and so on, but I think we've hit a big surge in 2026, especially of new big major projects that have been procured," said Barry.

CNBC's Property Play with Diana Olick covers new and evolving opportunities for the real estate investor, delivered weekly to your inbox.

#2921. Nets’ Moritz Wagner disappointed after losing to brother in ‘one of the more conflicting games’

Brooklyn's Moritz Wagner had been in the NBA for nine years, alive for 20 more, and never played against his brother Franz in a game that counted.

This first one stung, having to watch his younger brother torch his team for 35 points, including a game-winner with 2.8 seconds left in a game the Nets had to have.

"I don't care about him. I wanted to win the game. It's one of the more conflicting games I've ever been a part of," Moritz, who scored three points in 22 minutes, said afterward. "That is weird, but we've got enough problems going on. I can't worry about nothing on the other side. I'm trying to win the game, and that one hurts."

Brooklyn Nets center Moritz Wagner (13) defends against Orlando Magic forward Franz Wagner (22) in Brooklyn, N.Y., Friday, Nov. 20, 2026. Robert Sabo for NY Post

The Nets had already dropped three straight, including Wednesday's humiliation against Chicago that prompted a team meeting.

Brooklyn jumped to a quick 17-11 lead against Orlando, then coughed up a 27-8 run.

The younger Wagner tore up Brooklyn's defense, including 14 points in the third quarter alone. But it was his 15-footer that won it with 2.8 seconds remaining.

"He's a great player. He's gonna be an All-Star many times in this career. And great players make great plays," Moritz said. "I don't think we lost the game on that play. There are plenty of opportunities. We turned it over twice down the stretch. We took some questionable shots, didn't make enough plays, and that's what happens. Great player, make great plays."

Moritz played for Michigan and was a 2018 first-round pick by Orlando, where he'd spent his entire career until inking with the Nets this summer.

Center Moritz Wagner reacts during the second half of the Nets' 113-111 loss to the Magic on Nov. 20, 2026, at Barclays Center. Robert Sabo for NY Post

"To be honest, it was one of the toughest things I've ever had to feel on a court. Too much going on, I was very anxious first half, clearly (not) focused on the game. I didn't expect it would be this difficult," said Moritz. "I'm glad we got this one out the way. I'm not sure how I'm gonna deal with that; to be determined. But we did it, and now it's not a big deal anymore."

Franz said: "My whole NBA career I played with him. The first time in my life playing against him. But as weird as it (was) … it's a blessing to share the court with your family. Not a lot of people get to do that so that's how I look at it."

Orlando forward Franz Wagner (22) celebrates during the Magic's win over the Nets on Nov. 20, 2026, at Barclays Center. Robert Sabo for NY Post

#2916. Trump vows to stop U.S. companies from building data centers abroad, threatens 'tremendous tax'

President Donald Trump on Friday said U.S. companies will be banned from building data centers in other countries and will face a "tremendous tax" penalty if they do so.

"We're going to fight very hard for the data centers," Trump said at a White House press conference before announcing the new policy — which apparently came as news to his own staff.

"I don't know if that's a breaking story," Trump said. "I haven't even said that to my people. But I'm telling them now they're here."

Trump's latest push for more data-center construction in the U.S. came despite Americans repeatedly stating — and showing with their votes — that they dislike the structures.

CNBC's All-America Economic Survey found that 64% of registered voters opposed an AI data center within 10 miles of their home, while just 13% of respondents said they would support one.

If Trump follows through on his threat to clamp down on overseas data center construction, he will have to contend with the fact that numerous U.S. tech giants — including Alphabet, Amazon, Microsoft, Meta and Oracle — have already built them in other countries.

International expansion represents a growth opportunity for the top cloud infrastructure providers, which want to serve clients from data centers that are nearby, sometimes to meet regulatory requirements.

Trump said Friday, however, that if companies want to continue building the centers outside the U.S., "they can, but we're going to put a tremendous tax on them."

"We have a lot of places that want them badly, and they're great places. They have energy, and they have everything. They're tremendous assets," Trump said.

Representatives at cloud providers Amazon, Google, Microsoft and Oracle did not immediately respond to requests for comment.

Trump's remarks came a day after Trump met with Chinese President Xi Jinping and Russian President Vladimir Putin in China for a trilateral summit that appeared to produce few concrete deliverables on an array of pressing global issues.

Xi "congratulated me on how well we're doing" in artificial intelligence, Trump said, using his preferred term "super intelligence," which he said the Chinese president also likes better.

— CNBC's Jordan Novet and Annie Palmer contributed reporting.

#2912. Game Preview: Knicks vs Trail Blazers, November 21, 2026

As we all know, Madison Square Garden isn't just the most glorious gymnasium on the planet and revered in the world of sports; it's also famous for the long list of famous musicians who have performed under its unique bicycle-wheel ceiling. Now the Portland Trail Blazers are dropping by for a quick game of hoops on Saturday night, but they might actually be the one franchise that would be adept at bringing out the microphones as well. I mean, who wouldn't want to hear the newest rendition of all-timers like "Bust A Bucket" or "Rip City Rhapsody"?!

Not to disappoint anybody, but the days of Terry Porter and those shiny lycra dance outfits of the Blazers cheerleaders that would have made Cory Everson proud are long gone. Today's Rip City Rascals (coming in at 10-5) have their own way of entertaining a crowd, though, which is more than many would have expected before the season. With the return of Damian Lillard and a highly questionable move for "Pistol Petey" Ja Morant, the Blazers looked to be about five years late to the modern NBA party but atleast early it turns out they are anything but.

New head coach Micah Nori is making one of the more funky-looking rosters in the Association work so far, and he does it with a very modern, analytical approach. The Blazers shoot and make way more threes than their opponents (outscoring them by about 12+ points from beyond the arc), defend downtown better than 25 other teams in the NBA, and make sure Tony Brothers and his brothers-in-arms in the striped shirts have a living by drawing the second most fouls in the league.

Most of those foul numbers might be attributed to Deni Avdija. Only Luka from the Lakers and that feaky Greek in Miami stand at the charity stripe more frequently than the Israeli All-Star from Portland, leading to some fans questioning the ethics in his basketball bravado. But make no mistake about it, he is a serious talent, big-bodied and confident with a well-rounded game that can give every opponent problems. If he will be of any concern to the Knicks is not clear yet though; Avdija missed Portland's last two with a foot injury and figures to be a game-time decision.

But even without Avdija, Portland (road record 7-3) will present a very interesting matchup for your Knickerbockers. Damian Lillard seems to be rejuvenated after his Achilles injury, and a lot about his game feels a bit more wholesome in the jersey in which he always belonged. Behind him you have a Jrue Holiday coming off the bench, probably one of the more professional, classy guys you can find in the NBA these days. Cheekily, he also still packs quite a punch in his game, giving Nori first-class playmaking, tough D, and leadership in spades. If you think rattling these two old wily pros might get you anywhere, think again.

You could have more luck with some of the younger Blazers, but many of them bring certain elite skills to the table, too. Belgian baller Toumani Camara is one of the best defenders in the entire league, long, lanky, and extremely athletic. Seemingly plodding big man Donovan Clingan is getting more mobile by the minute and still hasn't lost his knack for gobbling up boards like a motivated Moses Malone did back in the day. Sidy Cissoko is another promising young wing, and former number two overall pick Scoot Henderson, while having a nightmarish season overall, can still break out for a big one as he did against Milwaukee the other night with 27 points.

What all those Blazers have in common is that they buy into Nori's system so far and operate with a clear-cut plan on the shiny hardwood. They extend their defense, making it hard to find anything downtown but also possible to do some damage in the midrange. On offense, they move the ball well, play five-out as much as they can (using Clingan as a high screen setter), and rely on balanced scoring night in and night out. It's a good formula, especially without Ja Morant, who has missed all but four games with a hamstring injury so far this year. The Knicks will have to touch the paint plenty to get that Portland defense moving, and they have to attack Lillard whenever possible on that end. Their superior depth could be a factor as well.

If the Blazers then throw a curveball and try to actually upend the Knicks' three-game winning streak by dragging them into a singing competition after all? No need to worry – we still got Tyler Kolek!

Who: Portland Trail Blazers (10-5) at New York Knicks (9-6)Date: Saturday, November 21, 2026Time: 7:30 PM ETPlace: Madison Square Garden, New York, NYTV: MSG, NBA TVFollow: @ptknicksblog and bsky

#2905. Rockets sputter late in Denver 133-124

Tonight featured the Rockets third NBA Cup game, which was essentially a must-win after their opening NBA Cup round loss to Dallas. The Rockets would be playing in Denver, against a Nuggets team that seems to be finding its way after initially scuffling this season.

Many have mocked and derided the NBA Cup, and sure, it's a gimmick, and it's artificial, and so forth. But the sad truth is, professional sports are a gimmick, and are artificial, too. This is just a new wrinkle in the existing structure, not some sort of attack on the traditional and pure nature of NBA basketball. But even if you think it is some sort of assault, don't you want to watch players trying their hardest, during the regular season? Because they do try hard. These games actually are extra, because hyper competitive NBA players like being hyper competitive. It took a few years, but the players are there, they mean it (at least in the early games). By the time a 14 year old NBA fan today is in their mid 20s, the NBA Cup will always have been part of the NBA for them.

Anyhow, the Rockets won't be moving on in the NBA Cup this year. It's possible to move on with 3 wins, but not with maybe 2. Those who don't like this added competition are free to celebrate this.

As for tonight, there are two ways of looking at it.

The Rockets were on their fifth consecutive road game, which happened to be an NBA Cup game, at altitude in Denver. Denver is where good road trips go to die, and if they don't die there, it'll happen in Salt Lake City (you can look it up).

On top of the schedule, and the location, a bunch of player who had previously been kind of bad for Denver so far this season had excellent night. Aaron Gordon, Cam Johnson, and Christian Braun went 20-29 and threw in another 11 points from free throws, which they shot at 10-11.

The Rockets on the other hand, missed 12 free throws out of 35 attempts, shooting 66% to Denver's 93%.

The Rockets bench played 75 minutes and managed all of 12 points.

The Denver bench played 75 minutes as well, and contributed 37 points.

The game featured over 40 lead changes, and was anyone's game until the mid 4th quarter.

Maybe the altitude told on the Rockets starters, and Denver's stars, Jokic and Murray, whom the Rockets had held down most of the first half, came alive.

The duel between Sengun and Jokic was pretty much a dead heat individually.

Jokic went 10-15/2-3 3pt/7 rbs/11 ast/4 To for 26 points.

Sengun went 9-16/1-2 3pt/11 rbs/10 ast/2 TO/1 stl/1 blk for 28 points.

This one was painful, but not unexpected.

The second way is a little more alarming:

The Rockets held a narrow lead towards the end of a high scoring game. They couldn't keep scoring, and lost it.

As the game got tighter late, Denver clamped down on defense, and the Rockets simply couldn't execute offense.

The Rocket with the most shots was Fred VanVleet. This wasn't due to setting up Fred for shots, it was due to Fred often finding no passes, no one really doing much of anything, and being forced to take a late clock attempt.

FVV did this pretty well, but he also shot the most shots on the team, at 21, and was part of the Rockets two guards (including Amen) going 4-11 from the free throw line.

Kevin Durant took 9 shots, wasn't assertive and the Rockets couldn't get him the ball late, anyway.

Jabari Smith was on fire, but only got 8 attempts, of which he made 7.

The late game collapse, and it was a collapse in the final minutes, felt very familiar. Unable to get stops. Unable to execute on offense. There was no plan, no special action to get the Rockets best offensive players tonight, Sengun, and Jabari Smith, looks late. It was, again, a lot of Fred VanVleet dribbling under pressure, and often making a difficult midrange shot with no time remaining.

The level of competition was high, the level of intensity of that competition was high, so it's not unreasonable to have concerns about this outcome. Denver might be a first round playoff opponent, for example.

One game doesn't make a trend, and isn't proof of anything, but I saw in this game a painful reminder of painful games last season. That could just be bad memories, and sensitivity to certain sorts of issues. The season, and especially the playoffs, will tell us.

#2901. AI is changing software engineering — and some developers miss the struggle

When Gemma Rodgers began her computer science degree in 2002, software engineering was seen as a future-proof and lucrative career. It appealed to people who enjoyed getting into the weeds of code, tracking down bugs and building programs from scratch.

It's an industry that has always moved fast, Rodgers, a software developer based in the U.K., told CNBC. But the arrival of artificial intelligence has accelerated the pace, with AI tools now part of the daily work of every software engineer she knows. In a 2026 Stack Overflow survey, 83% of software developer respondents said they use at least one AI tool.

As AI coding tools help engineers write, debug and test code faster than ever, some are mourning the job they used to have. Rodgers has always loved the sense of accomplishment that comes from piecing together different parts of code to find a solution, particularly when she's spent days, or even weeks, trying to fix a stubborn bug.

"The sense of joy and fun that I used to experience by having a challenge and then having to work that out, it's been removed somewhat," she said. "Now you can sometimes do it in a few prompts, and it's great — but it's a breadcrumb of joy, not a full meal of joy."

Rodgers is not alone in feeling nostalgic about the not-so-old days. Software engineers have taken to social media to describe missing their old way of working, when they could get into a flow state with code for hours at a time.

Still, AI tools can be useful and have lowered the barrier to entry significantly. "It's becoming an industry where a lot of people have been able to thrive who wouldn't necessarily have been able to thrive 10 years ago, five years ago, even three years ago," Rodgers said.

It's also important that the core skills engineers have traditionally learned on the job are not lost, she said. AI tools and internet services can go down unexpectedly, and engineers still need to know how to investigate problems and work through solutions themselves.

"The sorts of skills that we were taught years ago are kind of being lost because [engineers are] having to just churn work out," Rodgers added.

The job itself is changing, too. James Broadhead, chief technology officer of AI decision-making company Taktile, told CNBC that engineers who might previously have spent three weeks building a prototype can now generate 10 in an afternoon. The hardest part of that, he said, is "having the judgment to decide which of those are any good."

"The [AI] agents are spitting out so much code that [engineers] haven't read it all anymore," Broadhead said. "It used to be you'd build up a sense of comfort and awareness of how the whole code base was structured, but now the whole thing changes all the time."

Joshua Wöhle, CEO of London-based AI training company Mindstone, told CNBC he sees a growing divide between workers who know how to use AI effectively and those who don't.

The industry used to talk about "10X engineers," he said, who were dramatically more productive than their peers. Now, AI is widening that gap even further to produce "100X engineers."

Wöhle believes companies will increasingly have to decide whether to help employees catch up with AI or prioritize those who can get the most out of it. But he also understands why some engineers might struggle to adapt.

The people who once excelled at the job were often those who loved writing and working through code, he said, and those are not necessarily the current top performers.

"That's the grief that is very, very real," Wöhle said. "The thing you love doing is no longer the thing the company should be paying for, to a degree, because it's no longer adding the value to the company that it used to."

But not every engineer sees the move away from hands-on coding as a bad thing. Chris Sees, a software engineer of more than 20 years and CEO of London-based virtual office company Hoxton Mix, told CNBC that AI gives developers more time to tackle interesting problems. He views AI as a capable junior engineer that still needs supervision.

"The important thing is knowing what to delegate and when human judgement is needed," Sees said. "We'd be silly to think AI gets everything right, and I know I get a lot of satisfaction out of proving and spotting where it has gone wrong."

Even so, Sees worries about what happens to the next generation of engineers if AI takes over the routine coding and debugging through which they traditionally learned. Those tasks are "where you learn lessons and build scar tissue," he said, and companies need to make sure AI becomes part of the learning process, rather than a shortcut.

For Rodgers, what matters is having control over how she uses the technology. As a self-employed developer, she can use AI to automate the parts of a project she finds tedious, while keeping the more interesting problem-solving for herself.

She believes there's something important about working through a difficult problem rather than immediately outsourcing it to technology. Friction and discomfort, she said, are vital parts of the human experience.

"As humans, we need to feel accomplished in something and part of accomplishment requires challenge," she said. "Unless we are able to work that through in so many different aspects of life, then you never get the full force of the good feeling on the other side."

#2899. Lakers needed a little bit from everyone in win against Kings

LOS ANGELES — If Friday's 106-104 win against the Sacramento Kings was any indicator, it's as if the Lakers have played bad teams so often over the past two weeks that they've started to become one.

"That was a—a win's a win," Lakers center Walker Kessler said as he labored into his postgame presser.

But just as the trillions of cells in the human body help fight a cold, Los Angeles got (and needed) a little bit of everything from everyone to get over this Sacramento flu.

Whether it was Kessler's first 3-point attempt and make of the season, seven assists from Quentin Grimes, who yet again hit a game-sealing three, five combined offensive rebounds from Jarred Vanderbilt and Sandro Mamukelashvili off the bench or 14 points from Matisse Thybulle, the Lakers needed every bit of it.

"Certain nights, whether it's the team, how we're playing, the atmosphere of the arena, you can just be a little flat," head coach JJ Redick said. "And I thought we just had to be tougher and pick up our energy. They accepted the challenge."

Friday marks the end of a stretch of four games in six nights for the Lakers, who improved to an NBA-best 14-3 record and are one of six teams to reach the 17-game mark. Los Angeles is also tied with three other teams for most back-to-back games thus far at four.

They haven't been absent of casualties during this early war of attrition. Austin Reaves missed his fourth consecutive game with a left heel bruise he suffered against Milwaukee. That the Lakers have certainly felt the impact of playing without their second-best player is no revelation, but Luka Dončić has perhaps felt his absence the most.

Dončić, the league's leading scorer, extended his shooting slump on Friday, scoring 25 points on 2-of-9 shooting from three in another game where he was the focal point of the opposing defense every second he was on the floor. Dating back to the Milwaukee game, his shooting percentages have dipped to 41.4% from the field and 30.6% from beyond the arc.

The double-teams and blitzes he saw earlier in the season were less effective when Reaves was there to make the defense pay. But without him, the weight of the offense is much heavier on Dončić.

That's where Los Angeles's role players come in. Just as inconsistent as their roles have been so far, their impact on winning has been just as consistent. Redick said postgame that their plan in the offseason was to surround Dončić and Reaves with complementary players, and that despite having played three-quarters of a game with both of them available over their last seven games, the Lakers are 6-1.

"It's a credit to the group [for] sticking with it," Redick added. "Basketball's a game of mistakes. We played through some mistakes tonight, just like we did the other night against Charlotte. Good win. We'll take it."

The locker room is beset by these shooting slumps. Dončić, Mamu, Ziaire Williams and Jake LaRavia, among others, are simply going through one of those stretches. Redick plans to use the Lakers' forthcoming two-day break — a rarity so far this season — to ensure the team continues to stay sharp and get shots up.

After Monday's rematch against Utah, the team will enjoy another three days off before clashing with San Antonio. Just as Grimes broke through a rough start to the season, Redick believes others will come around, no matter how much a slump can weigh on someone.

To that point, as Redick recently stayed up late on the iMovie app on his phone, cooking up a two-minute highlight video of six-time major champion golfer Rory McIlroy to show his players — a video he humbly thought was "well-produced and edited" — one of McIlroy's quotes stuck with him during this trying time:

"When you're not playing well, it feels like your good game is so far away. And then once you start playing well, you realize it was never that far away to begin with."

You can follow Connor on Twitter at @cmorenoNBA.

#2897. Europe's cyber weak spot is the everyday businesses nobody thought to protect

Cyberattacks on refrigerators in French supermarkets in October spurred Swedish chain Coop to act. But it wasn't fast enough.

Attackers hit refrigeration systems in seven of its stores in early November. The affected stores had been scheduled for security upgrades just two days later, a company spokesperson told CNBC.

Mundane but essential equipment, used by everyday businesses, has become a favorite target of cyber saboteurs in recent weeks.

Experts are warning that a combination of known vulnerabilities, basic security failures and ambiguity over whether suppliers or their clients are responsible are making things worse, as AI makes cyber threats faster and more sophisticated.

Guy Segal, CEO of cybersecurity firm Sygnia, told CNBC that his company handled 285% more incidents in October than a year earlier, with demand forcing it to turn away some businesses seeking emergency assistance.

"It's going to get worse before it's going to get better," Segal said.

The recent cyberattacks targeted refrigeration systems across hundreds of European supermarkets, remotely altering temperature settings and forcing retailers to discard spoiled food.

France has since accused a cyber unit with links to Iran's Revolutionary Guard Corps of the attacks, an allegation Tehran denies.

Fredrik Blix, a cybersecurity researcher at Stockholm University, told CNBC that thousands of industrial control systems remain accessible over the public internet, sometimes despite manufacturers' warnings.

Segal said that basic cyber protection measures such as multi-factor authentication and basic network segmentation are more important than ever.

He said risks to major civic infrastructure like the electrical grid were front of mind and so "usually pretty well protected."

"There are always those civil supply chain components that are way, way less protected. Could be supermarkets, could be gas stations, could be… the screens on the Tube," Segal said, referring to London's underground rail system.

The supermarket attacks showed "this is the new normal, and definitely it's a wake-up call for every company out there," Segal added.

Segal said that inexpensive AI tools mean technical expertise and resources are no longer needed to launch attacks. He described one recent incident in which clients suffered breaches within six hours of a vulnerability becoming known, as perpetrators used AI to act faster — a process that might have taken weeks a year earlier.

Segal, whose firm helps companies respond to breaches and strengthen their defenses, warned that businesses without prearranged incident-response support could struggle to secure emergency assistance, as overwhelmed cybersecurity firms prioritize existing clients.

In one case, he said, Sygnia had to turn down a company because no response teams were available. Segal added that the company in question had already been turned down by ten other providers.

Jacob Silverman, CEO of risk advisory firm Kroll, told CNBC earlier this month that the company was operating at full capacity in its incident-response business amid what he described as "the most rapid uptick in threat activity that we've ever seen."

Segal argued that retailers should treat cybersecurity risks with the same seriousness as fire safety and food hygiene, including when outsourcing maintenance.

When Ahold Delhaize stores in Belgium and Luxembourg were attacked on Nov. 2, it exposed how different independent store operators had responded to the warnings following the earlier attacks in France.

Retailers had been warned about the vulnerabilities following earlier attacks in France, and those that implemented the recommended protections were spared, CEO Frans Muller told investors in an earnings call earlier this month, but smaller operators with fewer technical resources had struggled to make the necessary changes.

Coop, whose stores are owned by regional consumer cooperatives or operated by franchises, told CNBC that public attention on the recent attacks was the main driver of security improvements. It said the vulnerability at the affected stores had since been addressed.

The financial consequences of cyberattacks can be substantial. Ahold Delhaize told investors in November that a separate October cyberattack on its online operations and supply chain would reduce fourth-quarter sales by 200 million euros ($219 million).

Danish shipping giant Maersk, which suffered an estimated $250 million to $300 million in losses from the 2017 NotPetya cyberattack, has spent nearly a decade strengthening its defenses.

CEO Vincent Clerc told CNBC earlier this month that the experience had prompted significant investment in security capabilities, but warned that AI was enabling attackers to become more effective.

"Everybody is in a race" to boost defenses, he said.

Research by cybersecurity firm Censys found that the number of internet-exposed refrigeration controllers made by Danfoss and Copeland exposed to cyber hacking fell 53% globally between Oct. 9 and Nov. 5.

However, more than 3,100 remained exposed, and Censys said it could not determine whether the decline reflected permanent security improvements or equipment simply being taken offline.

A Copeland spokesperson confirmed that some of its refrigeration systems were affected by the vulnerability and said it had contacted customers to reinforce cybersecurity guidance, including keeping devices off the public internet and updating software.

Danfoss didn't respond to CNBC requests for comment.

In the U.K., a covert cybersecurity operation has been underway to protect tens of thousands of refrigeration systems at supermarkets, warehouses, restaurants and other businesses, the BBC reported on Friday. After researchers discovered a major vulnerability in late October, cybersecurity specialists, IT teams and the U.K.'s National Cyber Security Centre worked to alert dozens of companies and secure their systems before hackers could exploit the flaw.

The EU's Cyber Resilience Act will also impose stricter security requirements on manufacturers of connected products from December 2027.

But the legislation does not require all existing equipment to be made compliant with the new standards.

Blix told CNBC on Nov. 6 that European authorities should take a more proactive approach to reducing vulnerabilities by identifying exposed industrial control systems and contacting their operators before attacks occur.

"What worries me is that we don't really do anything," he said, adding that "we are shuffling a lot of papers" and "doing a lot of risk assessments" but failing to take meaningful action.

#2896. Falcons Draft 2027: Five Prospects for Falcons Fans to Watch for Week 12 of College Football

College football's regular season is in its waning moments, and every game counts as we approach the final season of the 12-team college football playoff. This week is full of trap games where teams fighting for their playoff lives meet teams who want nothing more than to play spoiler, and players who want nothing more than to boost their draft stock against some of the more prominent prospects in the 2027 NFL Draft class.

Here are five prospects who Atlanta Falcons fans should keep an eye on in this intriguing Week 12 slate of college football games.

Arkansas has a chance to put the nail in the coffin for Texas, which LSU simply couldn't do last week, and if they do, it'll start with a big performance from their standout receiver, Chris Marshall. At 6'3", 210 pounds, I see a clear vision of adding him to the Falcons' receiver room and immediately adding some verticality to the passing game. He will have to get past Texas cornerback Bo Mascoe and safety Jelani McDonald to pull off the upset.

The Falcons need a franchise right tackle and Jawaan Taylor simply isn't it. Drew Azzopardi already has the size and technique to be a good blindside protector of Michael Penix or a solid right tackle who can win in pass pro in general. However, he still has a way to go in the run game, but he has the athleticism to do it. With Bill Callahan in tow, I think Azzopardi would be a solid day-two prospect to invest in. Against Indiana, he has another chance to show off his pass protection against one of the Big Ten's best edges, Tobi Osunsanmi.

This safety class is absolutely electric and Oklahoma's Peyton Bowen is no exception. A versatile safety who plays the run well and can get his hands on the ball, if Oklahoma is going to make good on their late bid for a playoff spot, it will be behind this Oklahoma defense. The Sooners should make light work of Texas A&M, as long as the Oklahoma front can get past Mark Nabou Jr. and this Aggie offensive line.

There is a lot of talk about how this quarterback class has not lived up to the billing, and for the most part, it hasn't. With most of the highly touted guys potentially going back to school to be a part of the 2028 class, including Drew Mestemaker and Arch Manning, it opens the door for guys like Darian Mensah to play into QB1 territory. Mensah is a highly accurate passer who has shown he knows how to use his talent, especially with top target Malachi Toney. The question lies in whether he can continue his high level of play against higher competition outside of the ACC.

Despite an up-and-down 2026, Jacarrius Peak is still on my radar as a potential day-two tackle the Falcons could be in on. A smooth mover in the run game, great feet in the pass game, he is extremely raw in terms of technique, but there is so much athletic upside. Peak has a great opportunity to showcase how well he can keep LaNorris Sellers upright against NFL-caliber talent as he goes up against Chris Cole, Raylen Wilson, and this Georgia defense.

#2895. A massive bearish trade just happened in the chip stocks

So much for buying the dip.As the S&P 500 entered correction territory and the Nasdaq-100 flirted with bear-market territory, down 19% from highs as of midday Friday, the single biggest options trade of the day was a near $100-million bet that the VanEck Semiconductor ETF (SMH) has a long way more to fall.Around 12:30 E.T., someone spent $93 million buying 20,000 380-strike puts expiring Sep. 17, 2027, according to trading data reviewed by SpotGamma and Volland, options-analytics platforms that both show the trade as a likely opening position, with volume exceeding existing open interest in the contract. The trade bets that the index will fall below $380, a nearly 8% decline from its current level. The SMH is off 40% from its recent all-time highs.

"That trade stands out big-time," wrote Jason DeLorenzo, founder and owner of Volland. "It is either a hedge or speculative, but either way it isn't a vote of confidence."The puts were bought for $46.50 a piece, an arguably expensive choice for a downside bet with volatility in the ETF trading at 70.

"You're paying a metric ton of Vega for that thing," added SpotGamma's Brent Kochuba, referring to the contract's sensitivity to changes in volatility of the underlying chip ETF. "Maybe they were a forced buyer, or maybe they're actually selling this, which can still be the case even though the price shows it happening closer to the ask."

The semiconductor index headed for a 9% drop this week alone.

#2894. Is a bottom in for the Nasdaq-100? Charts to watch

(See the video above for Frank's complete chart breakdown.)

Is the Nasdaq-100's recent bounce a sign that the bottom is in? We're going to look at several charts to help answer that question.

First, let's remember how we got here. In September, the NDX broke out from a bullish cup-and-handle pattern, producing a 33,200-upside target. That breakout quickly failed, however, and gave way to a bearish pattern that broke down aggressively two weeks ago.

The decline brought the index relatively close to its 26,350 downside target before the current rebound began.

Zooming out, it's telling to see where the bounce originated. Three important forms of support converged in the 25,000-26,000 area:

This confluence of support helped encourage buying after a roughly 15% decline.

For perspective, we also see what larger drawdowns of 20%, 30%, 40%, and 50% would look like … a lot worse.

The following chart tracks NDX drawdowns from 52-week highs going back to just after the financial crisis. Only a handful of declines exceeded 20%, and three produced relatively quick reversals: late 2018, the Covid lows in 2020 and April 2025.

Historically, buying into declines of approximately 15%–25% has generally paid off, even when additional downside occurred first (yellow highlights).

The chart also highlights previous declines of 10%–16% in green, putting the current pullback into perspective.

The major exception was 2022, when the NDX eventually declined nearly 36% over approximately 10 months ... a much worse outcome, which many investors are concerned could happen again now.

That raises the key question: Are we experiencing another routine correction, or the early stages of a larger bear market?

One important clue will be how far this bounce progresses.

Any rally that fails to reclaim the previous highs could establish a significant lower high. Additional lower highs would provide stronger evidence of a developing, longer downtrend.

We saw this in March 2022, when that first sharp rebound effort failed to reach the previous high before a much more severe decline followed.

The bottom line: The current bounce is encouraging, particularly given where it originated and knowing how well buying prior 10-20% drawdowns has paid off.

From here, it's all about the follow through.  We don't need to see new highs right away, but any rally from here that's met with another wave of strong selling could reignite downside momentum.

Frank Cappelleri is the founder and president of CappThesis, an independent research firm that helps active investors through time-tested chart and statistical analysis. Prior to starting CappThesis, he spent 25 years on Wall Street as an equity sales trader, technical analyst, research sales specialist and desk strategist.

#2891. Chicago Bears vs. New Orleans Saints: Week 11 Players to Watch

Though the Chicago Bears have won 4 of the last 5 games, including three straight prime-time matchups, the BYE week couldn't have come at a better time as they embrace the much-needed opportunity to rest their wounded and prepare for the final stretch of the 2026 season.

That starts in earnest this Sunday as Caleb Williams and Co. host the New Orleans Saints. A team that has struggled mightily this season despite being one game behind in the race to win the putrid NFC South.

Make no mistake, this is as winnable a game as the Bears will see the rest of the season, but a conference matchup in such a tight race atop the NFC is also a must-win at this point in the season, and it also happens to be against Bears' defensive coordinator's former team, so there will absolutely be some added emotions this week. Either way, here are a couple Saints players to keep an eye on. Starting on offense.

The number 8 overall prospect selected in the 2026 NFL and the 2nd WR taken, the former Arizona State Sun Devil has been inactive for most of the season dealing with some nagging soft-tissue injuries. While Tyson was only targeted 5 times in his first action as a pro two weeks ago against Cleveland, he led the team with 13 targets (more than doubled the next most player) last week against Carolina and converted 11 of those into receptions for 80 yards.

Tyson is an explosive play-maker who might not be quite as physical after the catch but reminds me of Luther Burden III with his burst in and out of cuts and how he can make defenders miss in the open field. Tyson is the type of receiver who can give our corners fits, so I will be eager to see how Dennis Allen plans to slow him down while not losing track of the big-play weapons like Chris Olave, Barion Brown and fellow rookie Bryce Lance. With a depleted offensive line, I expect much more quick passes from QB Tyler Shough, so Tyson figures to once again receive the bulk of the targets. If Chicago can limit his YAC, they should force New Orleans to turn the ball over and score enough points on offense to put this one away. That is, if they can slow down the Saints pass rush.

With two players in the top 5 of the current NFL sack leaders, the Saints boast as potent of a pass-rush threat as any team in the league. Carl Granderson holds the slight edge with 8.5 sacks to Chase Young's 8.0, but both players will be key in containing Caleb Williams and forcing errant throws like we saw in Chicago's last action against the Tampa Bay Buccaneers. Not only does Granderson lead the team in sacks, but he also leads the team in tackles for a loss with 10.5, and that is despite missing a game this season already.

While I have the utmost confidence in Chicago's OL and how Ben Johnson schemes up protection, fending off these pass rushers will be a primary focal point this week. As our own Mongo Peanut pointed out, Bears Left Tackle Theo Benedet will have his work cut out for him this week. If Chicago can limit their production and prevent them from making any game-wrecking plays, the Bears should march up and down the field and put up points in droves. If not, New Orleans has a chance at an upset in Week 11.

#2890. Why Anthropic's blockbuster IPO couldn't escape the stock market correction

No one can say the Anthropic initial public offering wasn't a success for the company. Deal size and pricing were signs of what IPO experts say was insatiable demand for "ANT" shares. But what came within a day is something that investors better get used to: anxiety in the stock market about valuations being assigned to artificial intelligence revenue and business models that should keep Anthropic shares volatile in the period ahead.

The Anthropic IPO reinforces those fears rather than running counter to them, says Avery Marquez, director of investment strategies at Renaissance Capital, which researches IPOs and manages the Renaissance IPO ETF (IPO).

"The volatility in the AI trade we've been seeing for the past few months stems from valuations and whether we are in a bubble, and that's been heightened and highlighted by the Anthropic IPO," Marquez said on "ETF Edge" on Friday.

And in the case of Anthropic, which finished trading Friday down 9%, the valuation anxiety becomes "so much more prevalent," she added, because it is a new stock.

It is not just any new stock, but one that has Wall Street adding downside risks to research notes likely never seen before, and which may be the greatest understatement (or most subtle equities research joke) in the history of earnings analysis. "Anthropic could develop AI systems which become fully autonomous and wipe out humanity which may lead to a serious decline in earnings," Seaport Research Partners analyst wrote Jay Goldberg wrote on Nov. 18.

"It was challenging company to value ... and it's very richly valued," Marquez said. "We're seeing something more reasonable and investors grappling now with where should this company actually be valued," she added.

The fact that Anthropic pulled off the deal in this market and was able to command the price it did was an achievement, according to Yuri Khodjamirian, chief investment officer at Tema ETFs, which offers multiple funds that invest in pre-IPO shares of companies and held Anthropic in its Tema Photonics & Optical ETF (LAZR) well before IPO day. "We have to remember they pulled off a very large IPO in the middle of a very severe market with the volatility in the tech sector specifically," Khodjamirian said on "ETF Edge."

Khodjamirian says the investor demand for AI remains, but amid a reassessment of returns. And he says an equally hard task has now begun for Anthropic. Investors are unwinding positions in everything from tech stocks to gold and bitcoin, and repositioning for 2027 based on their new outlooks. "We are in a bit of vacuum," he said.

"From here on the market is trying trying to figure out what direction to go in," Khodjamirian said, whether that is based on Fed policy, geopolitics, the midterm elections results, or the AI trade. "In many cases, the chief investment officers I speak to, and the clients, say 'I'm just gonna take some risk off and wait and see until I get more info.' And with the Anthropic, the revenue picture and how the AI adoption curve progresses will be a vital stat to watch," he added.

Plenty of value has already been wiped out in tech, according to Khodjamirian, but investors need new signals to buy. That doesn't just go for Anthropic but for much of the market's tech leadership. "One of the things most difficult for investors to understand right now is some of the leadership in the market that has been taken by semiconductor companies," he said, adding that the peak was 22% of S&P 500 earnings. But these companies, like Nvidia and Micron, have a very different earnings profile from the rest of the market.

The VanEck Semiconductor ETF (SMH), which was down over 10% on Friday and around 15% for the week, was on pace for its worst week since September 2001, according to CNBC data on Friday afternoon. It also lost 15% in one week of March 2020. The tech sector of the S&P 500, tracked by the State Street Technology Select Sector SPDR ETF (XLK), turned in its worst weekly performance since the Covid crash.

Khodjamirian said while these stocks may look cheap on a P/E basis right now after the heavy selling, that requires faith in the earnings estimates, which he says will be more important as results are released for the next few years. "Right now it looks cheap, but will companies like Micron and Nvidia actually deliver?"

Nvidia shares fell 14% on Friday, adding to its worst stretch of weekly performance since 2008. What's more, the single biggest trade in the options market on Friday was a near $100-million bet that the VanEck Semiconductor ETF has a long way more to fall.

And the same caution about the outlook goes for Anthropic, he said. "That's why we need to watch Anthropic's annual recurring revenue number, because it is a key part of puzzle," he said.

Marquez said there is always hype in names like SpaceX and Anthropic when they go public, and it is hard to know whether the pricing of the deal is based on fundamentals, "but we just need more time and lots price discovery," she said. "It is not going to happen overnight," she added.

As an IPO researcher, she says this "discernment" is a healthy thing for the markets. There are few cases to look to as comps for Anthropic, other than SpaceX, and before that, way back to Facebook's IPO in 2012. And neither example is one of quick recovery. SpaceX shares remain well below their first-day closing price more than five months after its IPO. Facebook (now Meta), meanwhile, "did not do well for the first year after going public and we see where it is now. It's just the nature of these disruptive tech companies that come to market," Marquez said. "They lend themselves to more volatility and a longer period of price discovery for investors."

All eyes will next be on Anthropic's rival OpenAI, which has suggested it will be ready to go public in 2027. Marquez said the reaction to the Anthropic deal may be a signal that it was "a good idea to wait, allowing themselves to build more quarters of better financials and improve fundamentals a bit before taking on a market that is really quite discerning."

Tema does not own shares of OpenAI in any of its ETFs. But, "I can't say it wont be," Khodjamirian said. "They exist out there in the secondary market, and we will look at it, but we haven't made any decision. It depends on the ETF fit and if the portfolio manager wants to invest. We are always looking out for these interesting businesses but never just for the sake of private exposure," he added.

Tema does own Databricks, Stripe, Kalshi and Polymarket across its funds. It also was an investor in SpaceX pre-IPO through its Tema Space Innovators ETF (NASA).

Khodjamirian said demand from public market investors to access these "generational businesses" will continue to be the case, despite the market volatility, and will more often come before IPO day. In fact, he pointed to the filing from the world's largest asset manager and one of the biggest ETF companies in the world, BlackRock, earlier this week. It wants to bring out a new kind of exchange-traded product that would bring the ETF and interval fund structures together as a way to increase exposure to private companies, with shares trading on an exchange while the fund makes monthly repurchase offers.

#2889. Game Preview: San Antonio Spurs vs New Orleans Pelicans

Basement dwellers for seemingly the zillionth season in a row, it's tough not to feel for fans of the New Orleans Pelicans. They still have Zion Williamson on the roster and he's had a solid start to the season. However seven years into his career, the oft-injured Williamson, once seen as the NBA's next sure-fire superstar, has been unable to drag New Orleans out of irrelevancy. They've also dealt with injuries up and down the roster in the early going this season, as well as some roster drama involving a trade request that was made public last Friday from former San Antonio Spurs guard Dejounte Murray. With so much inner turmoil and unfortunate luck, the path forward for New Orleans is as murky as it's ever been.

Meanwhile for San Antonio, their generational draft pick has already made good on his upside, but there is still much work to do for Victor Wembanyama and the Spurs. Without Dylan Harper for the immediate future, they're coming off a win against another mess of a team in the Los Angeles Clippers. That win came with some concerns, namely the season-high 19 turnovers they accumulated and a 17/42 three point shooting performance that kept Los Angeles in the game until the 4th quarter. Warts and all though, San Antonio has continued to take care of business as they approach the 20-game mark. They'll need to bring that professionalism once again tonight against an opponent that is this far out in the weeds.

San Antonio Spurs (13-3) vs New Orleans Pelicans (5-12)November 21 2026 | 7:00 PM CTWatch: DAZN | Listen: WOAI (1200 AM)Line: San Antonio -16.5

Spurs Injuries: Tobias Harris, (out — calf), Dylan Harper, (out — hip)

Pelicans Injuries: Dejounte Murray (out – ankle), Bennedict Mathurin (out – foot), Trendon Watford (out – ankle), Bryce McGowens (out – elbow); Trey Murphy III (questionable – knee).

With San Antonio's decision to roll with the three guard line-up to start this year, Stephon Castle has sacrificed some of his game and has spent a lot more time this season off-ball to help accommodate both Harper and De'Aaron Fox when all three are sharing the court and it's been admirable to watch. As a result though, he's been relegated to being a spot-up shooter at times, being an outlet rather than being the one to get downhill to try and bend the defense like he did all of last year with the starters. With Harper out, expect to see a little bit more of the Castle from last season, though hopefully without some of the turnover woes he was prone to at times. Ironically, he had a season-high 8 giveaways in the win over the Clippers, the first game without Harper.

Zion Williamson remains the focal point of this struggling Pelicans team. He's putting up solid numbers and has been efficient doing so, relying on a still-explosive first step to get in the paint and either get shots off close to the rim or draw fouls that lead to him getting to the line. The effectiveness of that plan of attack though will be put to the test tonight with reigning DPOY Victor Wembanyama patrolling the paint with his 8-foot wingspan. The stellar San Antonio defense has been the best team in the league so far this season at not fouling and keeping opposing team's off the free throw line. Whether or not that will hold true tonight against an athlete like Williamson, who is still so strong and explosive, is arguably the main thing to watch for tonight.

Dylan Harper being out doesn't only mean more opportunities for Castle, it also puts Julian Champagnie back into the starting role he occupied all of last year. Champagnie is one of San Antonio's most versatile team defenders and is their best shooter. 3-point shooting in particular is arguably the biggest weakness of the 3-Guard lineup, as all three go to their jump shot when they need to but are at their best putting the ball on the floor and trying to get into the paint. Having to account for a shooter like Champagnie means one less guy that's playing with a foot near the paint clogging up the lane for a Wemby that is already making it a habit to dunk everything this season.
