#2887. An early breakdown in the polar vortex is threatening already depleted gas stocks

An unprecedented early breakdown of the stratospheric polar vortex and a record-breaking El Niño event has put energy traders on alert this winter, with analysts warning the weather and climate phenomena could have an outsized impact on Europe.

Meteorologists are currently observing a record early breakdown of the polar vortex, a large-scale circulation of strong winds that typically keeps extremely cold air over the North Pole.

Changes in the strength and shape of the polar vortex can influence weather patterns much lower down in the atmosphere, potentially bringing in colder weather to parts of Europe and North America.

This year, however, the collapse of the stratospheric polar vortex, or SPV, is taking place at the same time as an unusually powerful El Niño, a climate event that can boost global temperatures and disrupt rainfall.

The collision of these two events raises the risk of a prolonged cold spell that could lift heating demand and force European countries to draw down already-low reserves faster. That would leave the continent more reliant on imported liquefied natural gas (LNG) at a time when supplies from the Middle East are disrupted.

To be sure, Europe's natural gas storage levels started the winter at their lowest level in more than a decade.

The International Energy Agency warned earlier this month that low European storage levels and limited storage options in several LNG-importing countries in Asia and South America could intensify the competition for cargoes and put upward pressure on prices during periods of high demand.

Simon Lee, a lecturer in atmospheric science at the U.K.'s University of St Andrews, said the combination of a sudden stratospheric warming (SSW) with an El Niño event is not necessarily unusual, but meteorologists had been expecting a polar vortex breakdown in February — not as early as November.

The early SPV disruption is an "extremely rare" event that could influence winter weather in northwest Europe and the eastern U.S., Lee said, increasing the chance of colder conditions although likely not to the extent that a polar vortex event caused parts of Niagara Falls to partially freeze in January 2014.

"Geopolitics and weather are the two key volatility drives for energy and now they seem to be happening at the same time — and that's always the biggest risk for markets," Florence Schmit, senior energy strategist at Rabobank, told CNBC.

For months, meteorologists expected an El Niño event would result in a milder winter in Europe and relatively muted heating demand, Schmit said, which was broadly seen as welcome news given ongoing concerns over the continent's tight regional supplies for gas and crude oil.

The prospect of a colder-than-expected winter due to an early breakdown of the polar vortex could be "incredibly difficult" for Europe to navigate, Schmit warned. She noted that the region's gas storages have seen "very, very strong withdrawals" over the last two weeks, "which is quite unusual for this time of year."

"If you continue to see the same storage withdrawals over the next couple of months, that means energy prices will spike again — and they won't spike because of geopolitics. This time it will be the weather driving that spike and that volatility," Schmit said.

European gas prices have soared to levels not seen since the 2022 energy crisis in recent weeks, notching 103.9 euros ($113.99) per megawatt hour in late October. European benchmark Dutch TTF futures for December delivery stood at around 87.28 euros on Friday afternoon.

"We have never seen an early breakdown of the polar vortex and a super el Nino in the same winter so we have no historical precedent on how this will impact global gas demand," David Lewis, principal research analyst of gas and liquified natural gas at Wood Mackenzie, told CNBC by email.

El Niño is commonly associated with milder winters, while a SSW event typically leads to colder temperatures and higher snowfall, Lewis said, noting this could materialize in December and January within Europe.

"So far, the start of the European winter heating season has been quite cold but more importantly has seen low wind speeds across Northern Europe," Lewis said.

"The drawdown in stocks in the first half of November has been amongst the highest on record. With inventories starting the winter at their lowest level on record, any further deterioration in temperatures or wind speeds will have an outsized impact on European balances and lead to higher prices across Europe," he added.

#2885. Harden passes Bryant for 50-point games

James Harden scored 51 points to move third on the all-time list for 50-point NBA games as the Cleveland Cavaliers beat the Indiana Pacers 128-119 in the NBA Cup.

The 37-year-old made all 14 of his free throws as his recorded his 26th 50-point game to move above the legendary Kobe Bryant.

Harden, who also made six rebounds and provided six assists at Rocket Arena in Cleveland, had not passed 50 points since scoring 55 for the Los Angeles Clippers in November 2025.

Legendary centre Wilt Chamberlain holds the NBA record ⁠with 118 50-point games, while Michael Jordan is second with 31.

"When you talk about just basketball, obviously Wilt was elite, but you got Mike and Kobe - shooting guard position, the best to ever do it - so just to be in that conversation, it's very humbling and it's definitely special," said an emotional Harden.

Aged 37 years and 86 days, he is the fourth oldest player to score 50 points in a game.

"What excites me a lot is even being in my 18th year learning how to still be impactful," said Harden, the ninth highest scorer in NBA history with 29,633 points.

"It's easy when you're young - 23, 24, you run around, you jump high and can move fast and get by. Now it's like, you're not as fast.

"That's the evolution of James Harden and figuring that out.

"Tonight was a confidence-booster that I can do it at a high level still."

The Cavaliers travel to the Chicago Bulls in the NBA on Tuesday.

#2884. LSU football's Ty Benefield ejected for targeting vs Tennessee

KNOXVILLE, Tenn. — LSU football senior safety Ty Benefield has been ejected due to targeting late in the first half against No. 9 Tennessee.

Benefield was making a tackle on a Volunteer receiver DaSaahn Brame on a pass play over the middle and appeared to lower his head and lead with the crown of his helmet while making the stop.

The Tigers were leading Tennessee, 6-3, at the time of his ejection with less than 20 seconds left in the second quarter. Three plays later, the Vols scored the game's first touchdown and took the lead for the first time Saturday, Nov. 21 inside Neyland Stadium.

Officials reviewed the play after it was not initially ruled targeting and came out of it ruling that Benefield targeted while making the tackle.

Benefield will sit the rest of this game and it'll be a big loss for LSU's defense as he's one of the top players on that side of the ball.

Sophomore Jhase Thomas replaced Benefield in formation.

#2882. Warriors center Al Horford describes the rare feeling of playing 20 NBA seasons

Warriors center Al Horford describes the rare feeling of playing 20 NBA seasons originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

Al Horford has etched his name in history, becoming only the 13th player to play in 20 NBA seasons, joining an exclusive list that includes LeBron James, Vince Carter, Dirk Nowitzki, Kobe Bryant and others.

Horford is grateful to be in this position and sat down with NBC Sports Bay Area's Bonta Hill and Dalton Johnson on "Dubs Talk" to reflect on his achievement and discuss being in rarefied air.

"Surreal," Horford told Hill and Johnson. "It feels surreal, but it's pretty special and I'm grateful for it. It doesn't feel like it's 20 [seasons]. It has been a lot of basketball, a lot going on, but I really enjoy this and being a part of a team and getting to do this for a living."

Horford played three seasons of college basketball at the University of Florida, reaching the pinnacle of his career up to that point when he helped guide the program to two straight national championships in 2006 and 2007.

Horford figured that he would get a shot in the NBA after a stellar Florida tenure, but didn't realize the magnitude that his career as a pro would reach.

"When I declared for the draft, Billy Donovan, my coach at Florida back then, sat myself, Joakim [Noah] and Corey [Brewer] down and he was like, 'Hey, you guys are going to be great NBA players,' " Horford said. "He looked at me, like, 'You're going to play 10 to 12 years, it's going to be a good career, you'll make a couple All-Star teams, good contract,' like that's kind of that. So my mindset, looking at players when I came into the league, you were a vet when you were 30-31.

"That's how it was so I was like, 'Oh, I'm going to play 10 to 12 years and then maybe in my last year or two I would go overseas and play in Spain or Italy or something like that, and just ride it out. Like Dominique Wilkins, a lot of guys used to do that back then. So that was my mindset, and then the league just started going, and I just continued to play. I feel like the NBA as a whole really invested a lot in the performance and the taking care of the body, and I was always very conscious of that."

Horford comes from a basketball family with his dad, Tito Horford, also playing in the NBA. Watching how he prepared inspired him to do the same thing, and he credits the lessons he learned from his dad as being pivotal to his career longevity.

"I think it's because of my dad," Horford added. "My dad being a professional player, he was always trying to take care of his body, take care of himself, and every opportunity that I got, I always looked for that. Even from the beginning, even for my rookie year, I was getting treatment like a vet. There was really no reason for it, but I just wanted to build that base, and then time just kind of flew and here we are. Probably when I was like in year 14, I was like 'well, I think it's going to be a couple more' and then I keep telling my agent I'm going to keep playing, I'm going to keep it going. My body feels good, I feel good. So it's been a big blessing."

Horford has seen the league change considerably since he was a rookie and he has adapted to the game's growth. That is why he views his 20th season as a badge of honor, acknowledging how much work it took for him to stay relevant in a sport that stopped catering to his original style of play.

"It's special for me because the game has changed a lot," Horford said. "When I stepped into the league, I wasn't shooting beyond 15 feet. It was all paint, it was mid-range at most. I never imagined shooting threes. I started to be challenged as a player, like 'hey, the league is changing.' I had a GM with the Hawks, Rick Sund and after my first major injury, I tore my pectoral muscle. He was like 'listen, you're a physical player, you want to play down low but you're not going to make it playing like that, like these guys are bigger than you.' They're stronger, you have to start expanding your game, you have to start shooting the ball.'

"It was hard for me to understand, but I was like I'm going to start trying it, practicing it, and it felt really unnatural shooting the three-ball. I started with the corners, and progressed from there, went from there. It's just the evolution of the game. I was like, 'If I'm going to survive in this league, and I'm going to continue to play and be relevant, I have to be able to shoot the three really well.' I have to be able to guard one through five. There's different things that you have to do because if not, even if you're a great player, you won't make it. For me, I just don't want to be on a team, I want to help. I want to be an impact player and be able to play and not just say that I'm there. That was always the goal of mine, and that's why I prepare the way that I do so I can be in that position to help the team."

Horford endured an injury-rattled debut season in Golden State last year but re-signed on a two-year, $14 million contract in the offseason. Averaging 4.5 points, 4.3 rebounds and 1.0 assist in four games off the bench this season, his veteran leadership is key for a team hungry to return to the playoffs after a one-season absence.

#2881. Zscaler stock jumps 18% on report that CrowdStrike in talks to buy security vendor

Zscaler shares surged 18% in extended trading on Friday after Bloomberg reported that CrowdStrike is in talks to buy the cybersecurity vendor. Shares of CrowdStrike shares fell more than 8%.

The talks between the two companies are ongoing, Bloomberg reported, citing sources familiar in the matter. CrowdStrike declined to comment on the report. Zscaler didn't immediately respond to CNBC's request for comment.

CrowdStrike has been one of the big cybersecurity winners in the artificial intelligence boom, with its stock about doubling in value this year, lifting its market cap to $235 billion. Zscaler, meanwhile, is down 13% this year, pushing its market cap down to about $32 billion.

Along with CrowdStrike, Palo Alto Networks has seen its business boom as businesses race to upgrade their networks in the AI era. Palo Alto closed Friday at a nearly $282 billion market cap.

Should a deal take place, it would be among the largest in the history of the cybersecurity industry. Google acquired Wiz last year for $32 billion. In 2024, Cisco paid about $28 billion for Splunk. Earlier this year, Palo Alto closed its purchase of CyberArk for $25 billion.

CrowdStrike's deals to this point have been on the smaller side.The company announced plans earlier this month to acquire agentic observability platform Fiddler AI and AI trust platform Dreadnode.

#2880. Should the Eagles want Shane Steichen back?

The Eagles have been so bad this season that they've forced me to change what I write about. I really enjoy studying opposing teams for the weekly previews, but I'm already more interested in what the Eagles might look like next year. So, over the remaining weeks of the season, I'm going to examine potential coaching candidates and some offenses and defenses that interest me. Obviously, this depends on the Eagles making changes, and in this particular case, the Colts letting Shane Steichen go. Neither is guaranteed. However, I thought it would be interesting to look beyond the record and examine whether Steichen is still someone Eagles fans should want back.

All statistics are courtesy of Fantasy Points Data Suite and cover Weeks 1–9 of the 2026 season unless otherwise stated. If you want to see a detailed video review of Steichen's offense, please check it out here.

UPDATE: I had just finished writing this when the Colts released Daniel Jones. I guess they agree with me that the quarterback was the problem!

My overriding feeling watching the Colts was that this looks like an offense Jalen Hurts would enjoy playing in. There are plenty of flood concepts, dagger variations, and opportunities outside the numbers. Steichen still likes to give his quarterback a defined picture and create space through route combinations. The Colts use 2×2 formations on 50.1% of their plays, the third-highest rate in the league, and you regularly see different concepts on either side of the formation. Eagles fans will recognize that immediately. Sometimes that familiarity is reassuring, and sometimes it brings back a few frustrating memories of the past couple of years. I think Steichen's 2×2 concepts are a lot better than what the Eagles currently run, though.

The flood concepts were particularly noticeable because Steichen gets to them in several different ways. Sometimes the running back initially shows protection before releasing into the flat. Sometimes a tight end chips before getting into his route, changing the timing of the combination. Elsewhere, a switch release changes how the defense has to distribute the receivers. These are small details, but they matter because the defense doesn't get exactly the same picture every time. Steichen can attack the same area of the field while changing the release, the personnel involved, or the route that occupies the underneath defender. Even in a disastrous season, you can still see examples of him understanding how to overload zone coverage and manufacture an available throw. And we know how good Hurts is at throwing this intermediate deep out.

One of my favorite combinations is a smash variation with a short route underneath and a deep corner route behind it. The Colts repeatedly attack Cover 3 with this, putting the underneath defender in conflict while finding space outside the deep coverage. Sometimes the short route is more of a hitch, sometimes the release changes, and sometimes the Colts adjust the combination based on the coverage. I kept watching these plays and thinking that Hurts could make these throws. You aren't asking him to live on tight-window throws over the middle. There are opportunities to work outside the numbers, throw vertically, and get the ball to a receiver whose separation the concept created.

That was an important part of my evaluation. I'm obviously selecting successful examples here, but these aren't all plays where an elite receiver destroys someone one-on-one. The Colts don't have much receiving talent available, yet there are still plays every week where someone is wide open because the design works. After watching the Eagles this season, that stood out! I'm not claiming every call is brilliant, but I saw enough to push back against the idea that Steichen has suddenly forgotten how to coach an offense. Even with limited personnel, he can still create opportunities that don't require the quarterback or receiver to do something extraordinary. I watched every single offensive play with a positive EPA above 1, and I can't recall seeing one play where the quarterback made something out of nothing. Everything good was about the scheme and not about talent.

The screen game is another area where I still like his work. The Colts do more than catch the snap and immediately throw the ball outside, hoping the receiver beats a defender. They use misdirection, movement from the offensive line, and a clear effort to turn defensive aggression against itself. This example shows a clever answer to a linebacker blitzing through the B-gap. Elsewhere, I saw a bubble screen in one direction before bringing the ball back the other way, giving the defense something to react to before the actual screen develops. It doesn't work every time, but there is thought behind how they get the ball to someone in space. Their screens have averaged 8.13 yards per play compared with 5.16 for the rest of the league, which supports what I saw. That is only 31 plays, but the screen game has been a relative strength within an otherwise awful passing offense. This is an area the Eagles could do with improving.

The RPO game is recognizable, too. There are quick slants and throws to the flat that Eagles fans have seen before, but I especially like how Steichen incorporates pulling linemen into some of these plays. If a linebacker sees a center or guard pull, he has a strong reason to react downhill, especially when the offense has already established that movement in the running game. Steichen can then throw into the space that reaction creates. That is why I think the variety in the running game matters beyond the runs themselves. There are good examples of Steichen giving Jones an accessible answer and putting a defender in conflict from this year.

The obvious response to all of this is to ask why the offense has been so bad. That is fair, and my biggest takeaway is that the receiving talent and quarterback play have made this extremely difficult. Alec Pierce's absence has removed a significant vertical threat; Michael Pittman Jr. is gone, and Josh Downs has now departed as well. Some of the players in the clips I studied aren't even there anymore. Steichen's offense still needs receivers who can threaten the defense outside. The Colts have completed just 4 of their 28 targets traveling at least 20 yards downfield. They still send players vertically, but they aren't getting enough production from those opportunities, and defenses have less reason to concede space underneath. I do think this offense requires receivers who can win outside and get vertical.

I also watched the interceptions and a lot of the sacks, because you can't evaluate a playcaller by watching only his successful calls. Plenty of negative plays made it hard to blame Steichen. Jones misses open receivers and hesitates when the ball should come out. This third-down example sums it up. Late motion and a switch release help create an easy short completion to Downs, who is sitting there waiting for the ball. What more do you want the coach to create in that situation? There are calls where I can question the design, but there are also too many examples where the quarterback simply fails to execute a perfectly reasonable answer.

That doesn't mean Jones is responsible for everything. The Colts faced pressure within 2.5 seconds on 23.6% of their dropbacks, the second-highest rate in the league, and the recent disruption along the offensive line has made an already difficult situation worse. There are also plays where the route combinations don't create enough. The reliance on 2×2 can become frustrating when a defense takes away the quarterback's preferred side and the offense doesn't provide a convincing alternative. I think those defined reads can suit Hurts, but Eagles fans have also seen good defenses squeeze this type of passing game. There were negative plays where I didn't think Jones received particularly attractive options, and it would be dishonest to pretend otherwise. I still put more of the overall decline on the quarterback and personnel, but Steichen has to take responsibility for the offense he is putting on the field.

Some of his familiar answers also appear less dependable than they once were. The RPO throw into the flat is a good example because we've seen it work for Steichen, but there are plays this year where the defense anticipates it and blows it up immediately. That leaves a nagging concern about how much the offense has developed. Has he built enough counters to the things defenses now expect? The same applies to the under-center passing game. The Colts can run effectively from under center, but that hasn't consistently translated into passing success, and there are examples where the linebackers barely react to the play-action fake. I don't think the offense is completely figured out, but parts of it look familiar in a less encouraging way. Have defenses caught up to Steichen? Or is it because of poor talent? It's a hard question to fully answer.

The other concern is how often Steichen still needs someone to win outside. There are plenty of well-designed combinations, but there is also an element of throwing a go route on third-and-long and expecting the receiver to make a play. That is much less appealing when the target is Laquon Treadwell, and the Eagles no longer have A.J. Brown to rescue those situations. Steichen can scheme receivers open, but his offense still benefits enormously from a credible outside presence. Any reunion would need to account for that. I can see why the offense could suit Hurts, but I wouldn't assume bringing Steichen back automatically solves every problem the Eagles currently have.

The running game was more interesting than I expected, particularly after watching the Eagles' current offense. Outside zone is the foundation, but there is a broader menu around it, including trap, counter, pin-pull, and different ways to get centers and guards moving. You see tight ends crossing the formation to block interior defenders, motion drawing attention in one direction before the run goes elsewhere, and pullers arriving from different positions. I particularly enjoyed the pulling-center plays because they immediately brought back memories of Jason Kelce. Steichen still understands how to get an athletic lineman into space!

The Colts can stretch the defense horizontally with outside zone, trap a defender who is attacking upfield, or bring pullers through to create a different point of attack. That gives the offense more ways to respond to what the defense is doing. It also gives Steichen actions he can return to in the passing game. The overall rushing results have been better than the league average and there is more creativity here than I've seen from the Eagles.

The offense I watched was generally more spread out than some of the league's heavily condensed systems, and although the Colts use plenty of 12 personnel, I didn't see the same emphasis on receivers inserting into the box. Some people might view that as a little outdated. Others might look at the Eagles and think that giving Hurts more space while asking less of the receivers as run blockers would be a good thing. If you consider that the Eagles will likely go into next year hoping Devonta Smith and Makai Lemon are their top two receivers, do you want a modern offensive system that asks receivers to make important blocks? Steichen's offense isn't built around constantly presenting under-center outside zone and boot action like the Shanahan or McVay systems, but that doesn't mean it lacks useful connections between the run and pass.

There is still a concern that the offense can feel divided between under-center running and shotgun passing. The alignment tendencies themselves are broadly similar to the league's, so I wouldn't call the Colts unusually predictable simply because they run more from under center and pass more from shotgun. The bigger issue is that Steichen hasn't consistently turned the success of one phase into opportunities in the other this year. Some of that is execution, some is personnel, and some belongs to the coach.

I went back to the Colts' 2025 game against the Chargers to compare the offense. I deliberately picked an outstanding offensive performance to see the successful version and understand what looked different. The sequencing was excellent. I get into a lot of plays in the Patreon video. Early trap action established the movement of a pulling lineman, and Steichen then returned to a similar picture with play action to create space behind the defenders reacting to it. Elsewhere, motion and pulling action helped sell a run before an easy throw into the flat. The passing concepts developed throughout the game, too. The Colts attacked with the short-route/deep-corner combination, then adjusted the route distribution as the coverage changed. You could see Steichen understanding the defensive response and using it to build his next answer.

Even the handoffs contained useful details. A fake shovel pass to Tyler Warren gave defenders another action to process while the ball went to Jonathan Taylor. Those little movements created hesitation without requiring anyone to make a spectacular play. The receiving talent was better, and Pierce provided the vertical presence this year's offense has missed. I came away from that game thinking Steichen still understands how to construct and sequence an offense. It is much harder to establish those complementary calls when the quarterback misses the available throw or the receiver can't threaten the defense, which is part of why this season has become such a mess.

I still feel pretty good about Steichen as an offensive playcaller. This season has been bad, and there are legitimate concerns about how bad it has gotten. However, I didn't come away from this study believing the league has simply figured him out. I saw a coach still creating open receivers, designing effective screens, using motion purposefully, and building a varied running game. I also saw a quarterback and a depleted receiving group failing to execute enough of the opportunities available. The lack of talent is evident.

My answer on whether I would like Steichen back here depends heavily on the Eagles' plans for Hurts. If they are committed to him as next year's quarterback, Steichen would appeal to me a lot. The formations, defined reads, outside throws, and potential to expand the quarterback running game all make sense. I can imagine DeVonta Smith threatening defenses vertically, Makai Lemon handling some of the opportunities created for Downs, and Dontayvion Wicks competing for the bigger outside receiver role that Pittman occupied last year. Those aren't exact player comparisons, but I can picture how the pieces might fit. If the Eagles want a broader reset with a new young quarterback, I'm less certain he would be my first choice, and I want to examine some other candidates before reaching that conclusion.

This is an evaluation of Steichen's offense. His suitability to run the entire team as a Head Coach requires a separate discussion about leadership, staffing, and player development, and he would have to become available first. But as an offensive architect working with Hurts, I still think there is a lot to like. The Colts' season has fallen apart, yet I can see enough functional ideas underneath it to believe Steichen could build a productive Eagles offense again. After watching what we've watched this year, I would be interested in that.

#2878. I never wanted the American Dream and I felt like something was wrong with me—until I moved abroad

I got my first job at 17, working the register at an Eckerd's drugstore in Texas. My manager was only a few years older than me, and while my coworkers seemed motivated by the idea of working their way up to his position, I felt no desire to follow that path.

It all felt suffocating: the added responsibility, the longer hours, the idea that this was the ladder I was supposed to want to climb. I wanted to work, but I couldn't wrap my head around building my whole identity around a job.

I never wanted the American Dream I saw so many people around me striving to achieve. For a long time, I wondered if something was wrong with me. It wasn't until I finally made a long-term move abroad about a decade ago that I realized it was okay to want something different.

After college, I got a job in travel sales. I was good at it. Talking about travel all day suited the wanderlust I'd carried since my first trip abroad in my teens.

I continued working in sales, marketing and the odd temp role for years. But they were always just jobs. I didn't feel like they defined me or feel a calling to an industry or title the way people around me seemed to.

Of course, I wanted financial stability, but I was unwilling to sacrifice my time and well-being to get it. I just didn't connect with the version of ambition that meant grinding toward a corner office. What I wanted the most wasn't status; it was freedom, growth and new experiences.

The wanderlust eventually caught up to me, and at 26 I moved to Spain to teach English. Even though I was working, people saw it as taking a year off from my career and warned me it could damage my resume. I simply didn't care. Living for my resume had never made sense to me.

Looking back, I think being abroad offered an escape from the pressure to chase the traditional path of the American Dream. There, I felt free to enjoy life and figure out who I was without worrying about what box I needed to tick next.

I wanted to stay in Spain, but I couldn't find a visa that would let me live there long-term, so I returned to the U.S. I became a serial job hopper for almost 10 years, quitting a job every year to spend summers visiting friends in the Netherlands, the U.K. and Switzerland or solo traveling in Spain on the money I'd saved up.

On the personal side, I wasn't in a rush to get married or have kids. In my 30s, single and childfree, I felt like I was failing at adulthood, even though deep down, I didn't actually want the things I was supposedly behind on. Settling down just because everyone else was doing it never felt right to me.

And I couldn't shake the dream of living in Europe. In my 20s, people mostly assumed my desire to travel was a phase. By my 30s, the conversations had shifted and I was getting pulled aside. Friends, family and colleagues all had some version of the same message: It was time to get serious and build a life in Texas.

But my gut said to try one more time. So I did.

In 2015, on my fourth attempt, I finally made it to Spain for good. It was scary to shake things up when everyone around me was settling down, but deep down I knew at 35, this was my chance to stop trying to conform and start building a life that felt right for me.

In my new home, the pace was slower. I quickly noticed that people actually took their vacations. Strangers at a dinner party didn't immediately ask me what I did for work. Adults in their 30s, 40s and beyond were out living full social lives. Single, childfree women my age didn't feel like an anomaly.

Living abroad didn't solve everything overnight. I still had to figure out how to support myself. But having the space to define myself professionally without constant judgment changed what felt possible.

In 2017, I launched She Hit Refresh, where I help women over 30 figure out how to move to Europe and build new lives for themselves. It's not something I could have created from anywhere else, with any other experience than my own.

In the process, I've met so many others who left corporate America behind to create something different for themselves. I've seen more women asking the same questions I spent years asking myself. Burnt out, disillusioned with hustle culture, realizing that checking all the boxes of the American Dream they grew up on didn't deliver the feeling they were promised.

We're all figuring it out together; each of us writing a version of a life that didn't fit a standard template. Moving abroad helped me understand that success, for me, was never about earning more or owning more. It was always about more ease, more time, more space, more life.

For years, I thought I was behind. I felt like something was wrong with me for wanting a different kind of life. Looking back, I just hadn't found my place yet.

Cepee Tabibian is the founder of She Hit Refresh, where she's helped over 24,000 women age 30+ plan their move abroad. Grab her free Move Abroad After 30 Guide to start planning your own move. The daughter of Colombian and Iranian immigrants, Cepee grew up in Houston, Texas, before becoming an immigrant herself in Spain. Follow her @shehitrefresh.

#2877. Looking at Paul George’s torrid start shooting the 3

It's true that in the Boston Celtics' most recent defeat, a road loss to the Denver Nuggets, Paul George couldn't hit water if he fell out of a boat. Boston's second-leading scorer so far in the fledgling stages of the season misfired on all but one of his seven long-range tries, even throwing up an airball after a reload off an offensive board.

The 1-for-7 shooting night from deep was far from the only blemish on George's body of work Wednesday, too. He fouled out of a game for the first time as a Celtic, and foul trouble limited him to just 23 minutes played. But for as much as the nine-time All-Star had an off night, his cold shooting performance stands out as an outlier rather than a point of concern. After all, the numbers tell a story that suggests George's 3-point effectiveness so far in his maiden campaign in Boston has been almost unprecedented for a player of his age.

A fitting place to start when talking about George's long-range shooting chops this season is that he's spearheaded the Celtics' 3-point-heavy offensive attack. As has been the status quo under head coach Joe Mazzulla, the 3-point shot is once again a linchpin to Boston's modus operandi on that side of the ball, and no single Celtic has reaped the rewards of that style of play quite like George. On a team that employs plenty of elite 3-point marksmen such as Jayson Tatum, Payton Pritchard and Sam Hauser, it's impressively the veteran who paces the Celtics in 3s made per contest at 3.4.

The team-high 3.4 3s George is knocking down on a nightly basis is an especially impressive number when comparing that mark to players in their age-36 seasons. The list of players who have averaged three-plus converted triples per game at such a late stage in their NBA careers is microscopically small. Only Stephen Curry and James Harden have accomplished such a feat, and they're two of the greatest long-range snipers the game has ever seen. They're No. 1 and No. 2 on the league's all-time made 3s list, for reference.

Now, George's high-volume scoring from deep thus far would only be so valuable if the efficiency wasn't there, but he's blended volume and accuracy beautifully to this point. He's not only taking a lot of threes, but he's also finding nylon on a sizable chunk of those attempts. The California native has connected on well over 40 percent of his already 104 3s attempted — 42.3 percent to be exact, a career-high. Moreover, he ranks in the top ten among all qualified players in made 3s this season with 44, ahead of esteemed shooters such as Curry, Jalen Brunson, Donovan Mitchell, Tyrese Maxey and Kyrie Irving.

The fountain of youth is apparently located in Boston, as he is shooting the 3-ball at such a high level that Celtics fans have spent much of the season rubbing their eyes, wondering if they're watching the George of yesteryear who could go off for 30 on any given night. Highlighting his 3-point prowess alone might be unfair considering all the other things he's done well for the Celtics this season (defend off the ball, rebound, function as a secondary playmaker), but it's perhaps where he's made the biggest positive impact, and hopefully he can sustain the red-hot shooting.

Arizona is the 15th NFL franchise that Johnson has been a part of in his career, as the veteran quarterback has bounced between half of the teams in the league. Across 16 seasons, he has only started 11 games in his NFL career.

#2874. Retirees face a looming deadline for required withdrawals — missing it could trigger a tax penalty

Every year, millions of Americans must take required withdrawals from retirement accounts by Dec. 31.

Amid the busy holiday season, it's easy to miss the year-end deadline for required minimum distributions — a mistake that can trigger a hefty IRS penalty, experts said.

"One of the most common RMD mistakes is waiting until the last minute," said certified financial planner Alex Canellopoulos, director of wealth at Vista Capital Partners in Portland, Oregon.

Most retirees must start RMDs from pretax accounts at age 73. The withdrawal is based on the previous year's account balances on Dec. 31, your age and an IRS "life expectancy factor."

The first annual RMD deadline is April 1 of the year after you turn 73, and future withdrawals are due by Dec. 31. If you wait until April 1 for the first one, you'll need two RMDs that year.

RMDs also apply to certain inherited IRAs if the original IRA owner reached RMD age before their death.

In 2023, more than 14.8 million taxpayers age 70 or older had a traditional individual retirement account, according to the latest IRS data. The average year-end market value of the accounts was about $330,000.

Deferring RMDs until the last minute raises the likelihood of mistakes, such as overlooking certain retirement accounts or making withdrawal calculation errors, Canellopoulos said.

Some 7% of Vanguard investors at RMD age missed their yearly withdrawal in 2025, up slightly from 6.7% in 2024, according to a Vanguard analysis of about 400,000 RMD-age retirees with IRA balances published in December 2025.

Among those investors, the average missed RMD was $10,500 in 2025, Vanguard told CNBC.

Investors who don't take RMDs by the annual deadline may owe an excise tax to the IRS, worth up to 25% of the amount they should have withdrawn. For example, the excise tax could be up to $2,625 for a $10,500 missed RMD.

If investors miss a required withdrawal, they could reduce the penalty to 10% if they "timely corrected" the RMD within two years and file Form 5329, according to the IRS.

In some cases, the IRS could waive the penalty entirely due to "reasonable error" if investors take "reasonable steps" to fix the shortfall, according to the agency. Investors can file Form 5329 with a letter of explanation to request the waiver.

"If you don't ask, you're not going to get it," said certified public accountant Tim Steffen, director of advanced planning at financial services firm Baird. "I've never heard of anybody being denied the waiver."

Investors who give to charity may be skipping a specific donation strategy that can reduce or eliminate their annual RMDs, according to Lawrence Pon, a CFP and CPA based in Redwood City, California.

They can use a so-called qualified charitable distribution, or QCD, Pon said.

A QCD has a dual tax benefit: It doesn't count as taxable income and may also satisfy at least part of an investor's yearly RMD, Pon said.

The move requires a direct transfer from an investor's pretax IRA to an eligible nonprofit organization. They can donate up to $111,000 in 2026. While QCD eligibility begins at age 70½, transfers won't start counting toward RMDs until an investor reaches their RMD age.

Generally, taxpayers only get a tax break for large charitable donations if they itemize deductions on their tax returns. But QCDs still provide a tax benefit for charitable gifts when investors use the standard deduction.

QCDs "provide a real benefit if you are not itemizing [tax deductions]," said Pon.

While there's no tax deduction for QCDs, the transfer won't increase a taxpayer's adjusted gross income, or AGI. Raising AGI can cause other tax consequences, such as higher premiums for Medicare Part B and Part D.

#2873. A trading strategy to ride out the Nvidia uncertainty and generate income in the process

Jensen Huang's abrupt resignation has sent shockwaves through the tech sector, dragging Nvidia's (NVDA) stock down roughly 30% since Oct. 6. The market despises uncertainty, and losing a visionary founder, particularly one without a well-telegraphed succession plan, makes it worse.

However, for opportunistic investors, this panic has made the stock cheaper (obviously) while options have become significantly more expensive. Trading in the $160s, Nvidia's valuation has taken a big haircut, yet its underlying business fundamentals remain largely intact. While Huang was the architect of Nvidia's AI dominance, the company's moat — superior GPUs, the CUDA software ecosystem, and networking expertise — does not walk out the door with him. The company's structural advantage in the global artificial intelligence rollout persists.

Buying good companies at a discount is investing 101, but the current market dynamics may do something else for those who use options.  Huang's departure guarantees that Nvidia will be operating under a cloud of transition for the foreseeable future. Executing a permanent CEO search while navigating global regulatory scrutiny takes time. Consequently, Nvidia's implied volatility (IV)—the market's pricing of expected future price swings — has spiked to highs not seen since the tariff tantrum in the first half of 2025, and this elevated IV is highly likely to persist for several months as the leadership drama slowly unfolds.

This prolonged period of high implied volatility can be a goldmine for option sellers, specifically those executing options overlays such as covered call strategies. When you buy NVDA shares at these depressed levels and simultaneously sell out-of-the-money call options against your position, the premiums you collect are directly tied to IV. Because fear and transition-related uncertainty have dramatically inflated these option prices, the income generated from selling covered calls is now significantly higher than usual.

This lowers the risk profile of holding the stock considerably over time. Consistently collecting these inflated premiums systematically lowers your overall cost basis in the underlying shares. If the stock trades sideways or recovers slowly during the CEO search, you are generating an outsized cash yield on a stagnant asset. If the stock experiences further downside turbulence, the heavy premium income acts as a substantial downside buffer, meaningfully reducing the strategy's overall risk over time.

By deploying a persistent covered call strategy, investors can leverage the exact fear driving the stock down to generate robust, risk-reducing income while maintaining exposure to the undeniable future of AI.

#2872. Kings' Darius Acuff Jr. recalls early starstruck moments from rookie NBA season

Kings' Darius Acuff Jr. recalls early starstruck moments from rookie NBA season originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

Darius Acuff Jr., much to the Kings' delight, has transitioned easily to the NBA in his rookie season.

The freshly turned 20-year-old point guard is playing with veteran-like composure, leading all rookies with 25.7 points per game. But, that does not exclude him from having starstruck moments facing off against the league's premier players.

Acuff, in an interview with NBC Sports California's Deuce Mason and Morgan Ragan, noted three stars he has played against so far who made a greater impression on him in person than what he has seen on film: Miami Heat forward Giannis Antetokounmpo, Los Angeles Lakers guard Luka Dončić and Portland Trail Blazers guard Damian Lillard.

He noted that he hasn't had a signature "welcome to the NBA moment," but that Lillard was close to giving him one.

"If he would've hit probably like two more threes on me, he probably would've had me," Acuff explained. "But I think he hit two on me and then I fouled him on two, so it didn't look as crazy. But when he hit those two stepbacks, it was starting to look a little crazy.

"So, if he would've hit like two more, that probably would've been it."

Lillard, in the Kings' 120-107 loss to the Trail Blazers, scored 29 points with all six of his field goals made from three. Acuff scored 17 points in that game – just the third time he has been held under 20 points all season.

Despite the Lillard flurry, Acuff has held his own so far in his rookie season. He hasn't been hit with an embarrassing moment that would qualify as a "welcome to the NBA" moment.

"Nobody [has] just killed me, or like, I got dunked on like that – none of that happened yet," Acuff said. "So as far as right now, no. But we're going to pray that doesn't happen."

After the game, Lillard gave Acuff plenty of praise for the rookie's proven ability early into his career. Acuff said he was appreciative of it, especially because he has modeled his game after Lillard's.

"He's one of the guys that I watch because I see a lot of similarities in our game. Especially as far as scoring as point guards and how much gravity we create, so, it's definitely great," Acuff said. "We saw it [in the Kings-Blazers game] how great of a player he still is. And I'm still learning. I'm still watching those guys no matter what. But just taking that challenge of getting better every game, every day, has been the best part I would say."

Acuff is quickly getting acquainted to NBA life on the court, but he's also trying to get acquainted to life off the court as well. Now that he has reached the professional level, he can enjoy the fruits of his labor with his family, but he's learning it's a hard balance to strike.

"I'm a person that likes to sit in the house a lot, and I'm realizing you don't really have to do that a lot in the NBA. Like, you're older now, you can explore life a little bit, and a lot of people have been telling me that," Acuff explained. "So, I'm trying, but it's hard because; you got a game, get home at 11, and you wake up and you're like, 'I got a game tomorrow.' So, you'd be like, 'Yeah, I want to work out then I'm chilling.'

When it comes to spending time with his family, Acuff said that his loved ones understand the time commitment necessary for being a professional basketball player.

At just 20 years old, Acuff has plenty of time to figure out everything on and off the court that will allow him to fully enjoy his NBA career.

Where to watch Grand Slam of Curling National: Schedule, times, channels, live streams, scores for 2026 originally appeared on The Sporting News. Add The Sporting News as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

The Grand Slam of Curling is back this week with the GSOC National in Sydney, Nova Scotia.

The third of five events in this year's series, the National features some intriguing lineup changes, including the first action this season for some high-profile Americans.

Five-time Olympian John Shuster won a qualifier to get into the field, and Milan Cortina 2026 mixed doubles silver medalist Korey Dropkin will step in as a sub for skip Matt Dunstone's side, which went unbeaten in winning the GSOC Masters two weeks ago.

Who will prevail this week in Nova Scotia? Here's all you need to know to watch all the action this week.

#2869. No. 5 Texas vs. Arkansas live updates: Kobe Black injured after Horns take 31-3 lead

It's Senior Day on the Forty Acres as quarterback Arch Manning plans to participate in what could be his final regular-season home game at Darrell K Royal-Texas Memorial Stadium as the No. 5 Texas Longhorns host the Arkansas Razorbacks.

An SEC championship game and College Football Playoff berth are on the line for Steve Sarkisian's team, which lost its margin for error with back-to-back losses to unranked opponents in October before surviving a home test against then-No. 7 Ole Miss and road challenges at then-No. 18 Missouri and then-No. 21 LSU.

Now Texas is hoping for the type of comfortable victory that has eluded the team in SEC play. At 2-5 in the conference, Arkansas represents a prime opportunity for one, although the Hogs proved how dangerous they can be in taking advantage of an injury to Gamecock quarterback LaNorris Seller to beat then-No. 17 South Carolina 34-6 last week.

Listed as questionable on the first three SEC-mandated player availability reports, Texas junior edge Colin Simmons was ruled out on Saturday in the report released 90 minutes before kickoff.

Senior cornerback Warren Roberson was upgraded from probable to available, while sixth-year offensive guard Laurence Seymore is a game-time decision.

Arkansas won the toss and elected to receive the start the game. After a touchback, a holding penalty drawn by Texas sophomore defensive end Lance Jackson put Arkansas behind the chains, but a positive run and completion put the Razorbacks in 3rd and 4. Initially ruled a completion at the sticks as KJ Jackson got the throw off under pressure, but review overturned the call to force a punt by Arkansas.

A short punt led to Ryan Niblett getting hit trying to field the ball, a 15-yard penalty that allowed Texas to start its first possession just short of midfield. Manning opened the game throwing it, completing a 16-yard pass to junior wide receiver Cam Coleman, a shorter pass to junior wide receiver Emmett Mosley V that went for five yards, and a screen to junior Ryan Wingo that was going to force Texas to convert a third down, but instead resulted a hands to the face penalty on the perimeter that produced another 15 yards in penalty yardage for the Longhorns.

Manning kept it on a zone read, but lost a yard as the play that stretched. A push pass to Wingo worked better, as the speedy wide receiver was able to take the edge, cut upfield, and pick up 12 yards to the 3-yard line. Trying to throw a play-action pass with Manning rolling to his right was unsuccessful, as the Texas quarterback had to throw the ball away. In a two-back formation, Manning kept a zone read and followed blocks by Mosley and fifth-year tight end Michael Masunas for a three-yard touchdown run and a 7-0 lead.

A tough run on first down by Sutton Smith gained four yards, but his second-down effort was stopped from the back side by senior defensive end Colton Vasek. On 3rd and 6, Jackson's intended receiver bobbled the catch and senior safety Jelani McDonald was in the right place at the right time, coming up with the interception and seeing nothing but open field in front of him on a 31-yard return and a 14-0 lead, bolstering McDonald's late-season Jim Thorpe Award campaign.

The interception was McDonald's fourth in the last two games.

After a first-down incompletion, KJ Jackson rumbled up the middle for a 12-yard gain. Smith ran for five yards on the next play, then Jackson connected with Chris Marshall up the sideline, and Marshall was able to make Roberson miss to gain 28 yards. Three runs by Cam Settles picked up another first down, including a big hole created on 3rd and 7 for seven yards. On first down, however, Sutton was stuffed, and Jackson had to settle for an underneath completion on second down for four yards. Facing 3rd and 6, Jackson tried to target Marshall on a slant, but cornerback Kade Phillips had sticky coverage, and Arkansas settled for a 30-yard field goal.

The next Texas possession fizzled out quickly after a run and catch by Brown — his third-down effort was blown up behind the line of scrimmage, and the punt unit committed a delay of game penalty.

A good play by sophomore defensive lineman Justus Terry stopped Arkansas for a two-yard run on first down, but a big hole in the Texas zone coverage afforded Jackson an easy completion over the middle for 16 yards. Jackson opted to snap it before the first quarter ended and it almost resulted in a disaster as Jackson won with an inside move and forced a fumble recovered by the Razorbacks.

On 3rd and 11, the Longhorns only rushed four, and while Jackson was able to get out of the pocket to his right without any pressure, the coverage held long enough to force Jackson to take a five-yard gain and the Arkansas punt unit to take the field, pinning Texas at its 5-yard line on a fair catch by Niblett.

A sixth-yard run by Terry create some space before Mosley found a seam on a screen pass to the boundary behind good blocking, including from senior right tackle Adnre Cojoe, for a 41-yard gain. Another explosive play was produced by the individual effort of Bishop, who spun out of a tackle attempt and ran through another 18 yards.

Wingo ran for five yards on a reverse with a nice upfield cut and Terry took three carries for 14 yards, but Manning couldn't hit Coleman in the end zone and then took a sack on third down when Cojoe got knocked backwards by a defensive tackle who was mostly just trying to set up a twist for his defensive end. A 37-yard field goal by senior Gianni Spetic made it 17-3.

Texas gave up a chunk play on a wheel route by Breylon Russell that either wasn't carried up the sideline or didn't have a safety get over the top of it, gaining 23 yards. A catch and run by Marshall went for 13 yards after Russell was stuffed. Sophomore nickel back Graceson Littleton delivered a big hit on Settles on the next play, but it came at the end of a seven-yard gain. Hoping for undisciplined play by Texas, Arkansas called an end around, but the Longhorns played it well for no gain, Jackson was stopped for a one-yard gain on third down, and then threw incomplete on fourth down, a drop by Russell after Jackson rolled to his left.

After taking over on its own 30-yard line, Manning hit Mosley on an arrow route for four yards, Brown showed some patience and leg drive to gain four yards up the middle, and Manning bounced a designed run behind effective blocks by Masunas, Wingo, and Coleman for 10 yards. A screen to Wingo only picked up two yards, but Manning hung in the pocket long enough to throw a dime to Mosley on a corner route for 29 yards, but the defender punched at the ball enough to force a review that upheld the call on the field.

Working from a clean pocket, Manning delivered another accurate throw, this time to sophomore tight end Emaree Winston, who ran a post route behind a corner by Mosley that put the safety in conflict — when the safety took Mosley, Winston was wide open for a 21-yard touchdown, the first of his career, as the linebacker .

Winston's touchdown catch put Texas up 24-3.

At the two-minute timeout, Arkansas had a nine-yard reception upheld on review, setting up 2nd and 1 from its 34-yard line. Two chunk completions moved the ball well into Texas territory, where the Longhorns held, sacking Jefferson on 3rd and 6 to force a 44-yard field goal attempt that missed wide left.

Trying to score before halftime, Wingo got open for a 29-yard reception and a 22-yard catch that showed off his ability to extend outside the framework of his body. When Bishop got out of bounds on a short reception to stop the clock, Sarkisian dialed up a fake swing pass to Brown that turned into a tunnel screen, gashing Arkansas up the middle as three offensive linemen struggled to find work, allowing Mosley to score from 17 yards out and extending the lead to 31-3.

Unfortunately for Texas, a late-half throw by Jackson resulted in a knee injury sustained by Longhorns junior safety Kobe Black, who had to be helped off the field.

#2868. Trump says he still has confidence in Lutnick

President Donald Trump on Friday said he has not lost confidence in Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick, pushing back on growing pressure from bipartisan lawmakers and even members of his own administration.

Trump, at a wide-ranging White House press conference, was asked about Sen. Tom Cotton, R-Ark., telling The Wall Street Journal that, if Lutnick is to be ousted, it should happen soon enough for the Republican-led Senate to confirm his replacement before Democrats take over the chamber.

#2867. NBA team grades: Report card for all 30 teams after the first month

The first month of the 2026-27 NBA season is in the books, and it's time to take stock of what's happened so far. Let's hand out some early grades.

It might be too soon to draw sweeping conclusions, but there's been enough basketball played to make preliminary judgements about the contenders falling short of expectations and the surprising teams surpassing what most experts anticipated. Evaluating each team's performance goes beyond its record, with roster construction, player development and overall trajectory factoring into whether a season is trending in the right direction.

Injuries to several stars and key contributors have shaped the league conversation through the opening month. Some teams have responded well to the adversity. Others have already dug themselves a hole that could prove difficult to escape. As a result, the grades cover a wide range of outcomes: six teams earned an A from USA TODAY Sports through the season's first month, while 10 received a D or F.

Here are grades for all 30 NBA teams now that the first month of the 2026-27 season is complete.

NBA PANIC METER: Which teams should worry after first month of season?

Records as of games played on Nov. 20. Preseason record projections via USA TODAY Sports.

The Hawks are right in the thick of the Eastern Conference playoff race again with guard Nickeil Alexander-Walker leading their backcourt, but there have been some troubling early signs. Jalen Johnson's production has dipped across the board from last season's breakout campaign, veteran closer C.J. McCollum is sidelined for at least another month and Atlanta has benefited from the NBA's second-easiest schedule to date. The Hawks' issues are flying under-the-radar, but they've struggled to show they belong among the East's top contenders when facing the NBA's best teams thus far.

After thriving without Jayson Tatum during much of last year's regular season, the Celtics haven't missed a beat in their first games since trading Jaylen Brown this past offseason. Tatum has elevated his playmaking, and Paul George looks rejuvenated as his new running mate in Boston. The Celtics have the NBA's best NET rating as of Nov. 18 and even got the better of Brown in their first matchup against the Philadelphia 76ers.

The Nets' roster is a mess because they've used seven first-round draft picks the past two years and none has demonstrated franchise-player potential to this point. Their top three guards (Egor Demin, Nolan Traore and Mikel Brown Jr.) haven't played much together yet due to minutes restrictions and skillset overlap. Their past four losses have come by an average of 24.5 points, and Julius Randle was spotted yelling at his teammates on the bench before calling their Nov. 18 loss to the Chicago Bulls "one of the worst games I've been a part of." After years of stockpiling assets, the Nets' rebuild still doesn't appear to have a clear path forward.

The Hornets perhaps expected to take a step back after trading LaMelo Ball this past offseason, but their start has muted much of the momentum generated from last year's encouraging run to the playoffs. Kon Knueppel lasted just nine minutes in his season debut before suffering a hamstring injury, and he's one of several key players to have missed games due to injury. The team, meanwhile, has the worst offensive rating in the league after finishing with the NBA's fifth-most efficient offense last season. Brandon Miller is shooting less than 35% from the field as the top offensive option, and Naz Reid, the primary acquisition in the Ball deal, was benched four games into the season by Hornets coach Charles Lee for "not meeting Hornets' standards." Just one month into the season, nearly everything that could have gone wrong for Charlotte has.

The Bulls are starting to emerge from a 1-9 start to the season, though their recent surge is happening right as No. 3 overall pick Caleb Wilson was diagnosed with a broken hand that will keep him out for the next few months. Wilson showed intriguing flashes in his early appearances, but it's still unclear whether Chicago's core featuring Josh Giddey and Matas Buzelis is viable long-term. The Bulls feel closer to escaping the bottom-half of the Eastern Conference than they have been because of Wilson, but his injury means they'll have to wait longer to figure out exactly what they have.

The Cavaliers are off to a rougher-than-expected start to the season, and it's particularly concerning because their starters are the reason they're scuffling along. James Harden is showing his age more regularly, struggling to finish at the rim and get to the free throw line. Evan Mobley has already suffered a toe injury. Peyton Watson, the team's main offseason acquisition, looked completely out of sync before missing action recently with a back injury. Rookie Meleek Thomas and forward Mario Hezonja have been bright spots off the bench, but Cleveland's core has failed to resemble the group that reached the Eastern Conference finals last season.

Cooper Flagg appears to have taken another step forward in his development after winning Rookie of the Year, and his fit alongside Kyrie Irving has been better than many expected under new coach Dusty May. They've positioned the Mavericks as a legitimate play-in contender despite playing one of the tougher schedules in the league through 15 games. Their early success together strengthens the case for Irving to remain in Dallas beyond the NBA trade deadline. With a first-year NBA coach and a 19-year-old franchise star, the Mavericks are outperforming expectations through the season's first month.

The Nuggets remain a threat in the Western Conference playoff race, but there are some early signs that Denver's reign as a legitimate title contender could be waning. Nikola Jokic's 3-point shot has largely disappeared from his repertoire, a development that began after he returned from a wrist injury last season. His points, rebounds and assists per game have decreased compared to recent years. Jamal Murray's strong play has been offset by relatively uninspiring starts for veterans Aaron Gordon and DeMar DeRozan. It's no coincidence the team has gone from first in the league in offensive rating last year to 10th as of Nov. 19. If Jokic can't return to his MVP form and cover some of the roster flaws like he so often has over the past five years, it's hard to envision this group being a championship contender.

The Pistons were a trendy regression candidate in the East, but they've already got wins over the Boston Celtics, Miami Heat, Philadelphia 76ers and San Antonio Spurs through the first month of the season. Cade Cunningham is back in the MVP conversation, and Ausar Thompson is becoming one of the league's most disruptive two-way playmakers. Early injuries to Jalen Duren and offseason addition Isaiah Joe have taken a little shine off Detroit's strong start, but guard Daniss Jenkins is beginning to blossom into the secondary perimeter scoring option Cunningham has lacked in previous seasons.

The Warriors got an unexpected gift when rookie Yaxel Lendeborg quickly emerged as the leading NBA Rookie of the Year candidate and helped solve the team's void on the wing with Jimmy Butler sidelined. They reeled off eight-straight wins with Lendeborg serving as Steph Curry's sidekick. But Golden State dropped five games in a row after Curry suffered an oblique injury on Nov. 11, a development that has exposed Draymond Green's limitations and the lack of offense creation on the roster beyond Curry. Even so, Curry's eventual return and the prospect of Butler coming back gives Golden State more intrigue and upside than most expected entering the season.

The Rockets have looked like NBA title contenders through the season's first month, with the return of point guard Fred Van Vleet helping jump start the league's second-most efficient offense. Houston's turnovers are way down and its 3-point shooting is among the best in the NBA at the moment. Alperen Sengun, meanwhile, is on pace to break his career highs in points and assists as part of the Rockets' deep front court, and Kevin Durant remains one of the NBA's elite scorers. This appears to be one of the NBA's most complete rosters, blending ascending young talent with veterans who have extensive playoff experience. Considering the Rockets also possess the assets to make another significant move before the trade deadline, they may be San Antonio's biggest threat in the Western Conference.

The Pacers were hoping to rebound from last season's injury-induced tank, but they lost star Tyrese Haliburton to a hamstring injury after just four games. Indiana is one of the worst shooting teams in the league after one month, and it's wasting another strong start from Pascal Siakam. The lack of shooting has complicated Ivica Zubac's transition to the lineup, with poor spacing creating fit issues for a player Indiana spent considerable assets to acquire. He's on pace for his worst statistical season in six years. The Pacers entered the season expecting to rejoin the Eastern Conference contender tier, but they look much closer to a lottery team than a playoff threat right now.

The Clippers are in rebuilding mode and off to an uneven start, with the expectation they could be sellers at the trade deadline. The good news is that Darius Garland, the centerpiece of last year's James Harden trade, appears to have rediscovered his All-Star form after an injury-riddled 2025-26 season. The development of first-round pick Keaton Wagler alongside Garland is likely more important than the results the rest of the way, and he's shooting better than 40% from 3-point range since Nov. 2. The Clippers are one of the league's better shooting teams overall, but they've offset that strength by ranking among the NBA's most turnover-prone teams. They're likely headed for the lottery, but that's exactly where most anticipated them to be.

The Lakers are off to a fantastic start with Luka Dončić a leading MVP candidate and on pace to surpass Michael Jordan for the highest scoring average (38.1) since the 1976 NBA/ABA merger. Walker Kessler is also providing the dominating interior presence Los Angeles hoped for when it traded for him this offseason and gave him a huge contract. Kessler is averaging a double-double, while Austin Reaves is scoring more efficiently than at any point in his career. If Dončić continues to play at this level, the Lakers will be in the NBA title discussion come playoff time.

NBA MVP RANKINGS: There's a new No. 1, as Luka Dončić cedes ground

The Grizzlies have been the biggest surprise of the 2026-27 season thus far, vaulting toward the top of the Western Conference standings when many expected them to begin a rebuild after trading away Ja Morant, Jaren Jackson Jr. and Desmond Bane. Instead, the team's recent draft picks, particularly Cameron Boozer and Cedric Coward, have helped spearhead a balanced and remarkably deep roster that already looks like a playoff contender. A recent injury to veteran Ty Jerome looms large, but Cam Spencer has filled the void admirably thus far. Memphis owns the NBA's best offensive rating as of Nov. 20. Few saw that coming.

NBA observers were in panic mode when the Heat began the Giannis Antetokounmpo era with four losses in a row, but Miami is 9-2 since then with an increasingly potent offense built around pace and space. Antetokounmpo is leading the Heat with the fourth-highest scoring average in the NBA (28.2 points), and Davion Mitchell leads the NBA in assists per game. Only the Spurs' starters have a better NET rating than the Heat lineup featuring Antetokounmpo, Bam Adebayo, Klay Thompson, Mitchell and Pelle Larsson among groups that have played at least 65 minutes together. One month into the season, Miami appears to have already discovered the lineup it can trust most when playoff games are on the line.

The Bucks were expected to be bad this season after trading Giannis Antetokounmpo, but they're off to the worst start in franchise history with the league's worst record and own the worst NET rating in the Eastern Conference as of Nov. 20. With veterans like Myles Turner, Kyle Kuzma and Gary Trent Jr. taking up salary cap space after being acquired in prior seasons, this rebuild appears to only just be beginning. Milwaukee entered the post-Giannis era without a clear franchise cornerstone, and the first month of the season has done little to change that. It will be painful for Bucks' fans this season.

The Timberwolves have done a fine job keeping pace in the competitive Western Conference after losing Anthony Edwards to a knee injury that required arthoscopic surgery. LaMelo Ball, Ayo Dosunmu, Jaden McDaniels and Rudy Gobert have formed a well-rounded core that could be dangerous if Edwards returns before the playoffs. There is no timetable on his recovery, though Edwards told reporters on Nov. 19 he is planning to to come back this season. Minnesota's ceiling ultimately depends on his health.

The Pelicans got off to a bad start, won four games in a row and now appear headed nowhere fast once again. Dejounte Murray's recent trade request brought renewed attention to one of the NBA's most confusing roster situations. New Orleans hasn't been able to win consistently despite Zion Williamson enjoying one of the healthiest stretches of his career, and Trey Murphy III is increasingly viewed as one of the league's more attractive trade targets. Jeremiah Fears and Derik Queen are intriguing young players, but both have contributed to the defensive issues that have left New Orleans among the NBA's worst teams on that end of the floor. Murray, meanwhile, is owed enough money that moving him won't be simple. If this start continues, Joe Dumars would have to consider a roster overhaul.

The Knicks deserve the benefit of the doubt after a sluggish start coming off an NBA championship, though the early-season struggles of Josh Hart and Mikal Bridges, as well as the ongoing contract situation involving Karl-Anthony Towns, bear watching. Jalen Brunson and Towns continue to form one of the NBA's most potent duos, and New York remains firmly positioned in the Eastern Conference playoff picture thanks in large part to one of the league's best defenses. The Knicks proved last season they can reach another level when the games matter most.

The Thunder are doing their best to stay afloat in the crowded Western Conference standings playing without Jalen Williams, Chet Holmgren and Cason Wallace due to injuries. Shai Gilgeous-Alexander remains one of the league's best players, and OKC's deep reservoir of first-round picks is paying off with younger players like Ajay Mitchell and rookie Aday Mara successfully stepping into larger roles. The Thunder have survived a difficult opening month and came out looking capable of remaining in the playoff mix. But bad injury luck could prevent them from contending for a title this season.

The Magic hoped to emerge as Eastern Conference contenders, but they finished the opening month of the season below .500 and lost star Paolo Banchero to injury after just two games. A recent four-game winning streak that included a victory over the Boston Celtics showcased the potential of a group built around Franz Wagner, Desmond Bane, Anthony Black and a healthy Jalen Suggs. But the franchise is still no closer to determining whether Banchero and Wagner can thrive together as a long-term foundation. Until Banchero returns, that question will remain unanswered, and Orlando appears destined to be part of the crowded pack of Eastern Conference teams battling to avoid the play-in tournament.

After considerable preseason hype and past stumbles out of the gate for LeBron James-led superteams, the Sixers finished the first month of the 2026-27 season tied with the Spurs for the NBA's best record. James and Jaylen Brown have blended in well with Tyrese Maxey, Joel Embiid and VJ Edgecombe, though the team's starting lineup had a surprising -4.9 NET rating playing together through 16 games. It speaks to some of the unexpected depth Philadelphia enjoys, with Dean Wade, Anfernee Simons and Kentavious Caldwell-Pope forming a versatile rotation. One month into the season, the 76ers look like the team to beat in the Eastern Conference.

The Suns have rebounded from a rough start to the season despite playing one of the toughest schedules in the league, and Devin Booker remains among the NBA's top 12 scorers. But their two most-used lineups have been outscored by an average net rating of 15.5 points per 100 possessions. Jalen Green is having the least productive season of his career, and centers Oso Ighodaro and Khaman Maluach have looked overmatched while replacing the injured Mark Williams. As currently constructed, Phoenix looks capable of reaching the play-in tournament. But unless the Suns can find an upgrade before the trade deadline, their ceiling appears to be a first-round playoff exit.

The Trail Blazers have taken a step forward with wins over the Lakers and Thunder already. Damian Lillard is providing a perimeter scoring punch, and his presence hasn't come at the expense of Deni Avdija. It's turned a lackluster offense into one with a top-10 NET rating thus far. It remains to be seen how Ja Morant's return from a hamstring injury affects the rotation, but early returns suggest Portland's crowded backcourt can co-exist. This might not be a legitimate title contender, but the Trail Blazers look as feisty as they have at any point since Lillard led them to the Western Conference Finals in 2019.

The Kings appear to be the most dysfunctional team in the league, with a core of expensive veteran players who don't seem interested in remaining in Sacramento but lack significant trade value. Rookie Darius Acuff Jr. has been a bright spot, on pace to average the most points by a first-year player since Michael Jordan. But the Kings haven't translated that production into wins and they have the league's worst NET rating. Meanwhile, last year's breakout rookie, 23-year-old center Maxime Raynaud, has yet to play due to injury. This directionless franchise is no closer to finding its way.

The Spurs have looked every bit like a team on a mission to avenge last June's NBA Finals loss, highlighted by a recent 12-game winning streak. Victor Wembanyama is an MVP candidate once again, and the returning core of Dylan Harper, Stephon Castle and De'Aaron Fox has made the absence of free-agent acquisition Tobias Harris largely an afterthought. A recent hip injury to Harper does not appear to be serious, and San Antonio is deep enough to withstand his absence if he misses additional time. One month into the season, the Spurs look like the favorites to win the NBA title.

After emerging as one of the NBA's pleasant surprises last season, the Raptors have stumbled out of the gate this year. Kawhi Leonard has yet to play because of a knee injury, and Scottie Barnes has also missed time. The team's defense has gone from a strength to a weakness, particularly in close games, and Toronto ranks among the NBA's worst rebounding teams because of a wing-heavy roster that lacks a reliable center. The Raptors traded for Leonard with championship aspirations in mind, but the first month of that pursuit has been anything but encouraging.

The Jazz's hopes of emerging as a playoff contender after a lengthy rebuilding have been decimated by injuries to Lauri Markannen and Jaren Jackson Jr., as well as a disastrous start that featured a 12-game losing streak. Keyonte George played more like a rising star in two recent Utah wins and, along with rookie Darryn Peterson and 20-year-old Ace Bailey, gives the franchise a promising young core. But the Jazz expected to compete for a postseason berth and that might not be possible after an opening month that could hardly have gone worse.

The Wizards remain near the bottom of the Eastern Conference standings, and their decision to give Trae Young a $212 million contract extension is already drawing scrutiny. Young is shooting a career-worst 30.4% from 3-point range, and Washington ranks as the NBA's second-worst 3-point shooting team. Opponents have also targeted Young defensively in crunch time. The team's struggles are particularly concerning because Anthony Davis is healthy and playing well, while rookie AJ Dybantsa has shown encouraging flashes despite struggling to shoot from outside since returning from injury. Washington may still have enough talent to reach the playoffs in the East, but its biggest investment is underperforming.
